-left regular and bipartite,
that the constraints are projections, and that the udPCP is left-canonical.

Perfect Completeness.

Fix
w\in\mathcal{L}
; then by the completeness of
\Psi
, there is an assignment
A_{0}:V_{0}\to\Sigma^{\prime}
which satisfies all of the constraints of
\Psi_{0}
and is equal to
w
on the vertices of
V_{0}
associated with
[n]
. Define the assignment
F:A\to B
by setting
F(v)=A_{0}(v)
for each
v\in B
, and setting
F((e,t))
to be the restriction of
A_{0}
to the vertices in
(e,t)
for each
(e,t)\in A
. It is easy to see that
F
assigns a valid alphabet symbol to each vertex, that it satisfies all of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}
, and that for any
t
and
(u,v)\in\supp(\mathcal{P}_{t})
, we have
D_{t}(u,F(u))=w_{t}
.

Unique Decoding Soundness.

Fix
\eta>0
, set
\varepsilon=\frac{\rho\cdot\eta}{2k}
and suppose
A:V\to\Sigma
is an assignment to
\mathcal{D}
that satisfies at least
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction of the constraints. We will show that there exists
w\in\mathcal{L}
such that the decoding error with respect to this
w
is at most
\eta
.

For each
(e,t)\in A
define
p(e,t)=\Pr_{v\in(e,t)}[A(v)\neq A(e,t)(v)]
, and note that the assumption that
A
satisfies at least
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction can be written as

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(e,t)\in A}[p(e,t)]\leqslant\varepsilon
. Note that if
p(e,t)>0
for some
(e,t)
, then it must be the case that
p(e,t)\geqslant\frac{1}{k+1}
. Applying Markov’s inequality we conclude that

\Pr_{(e,t)\in A}[p(e,t)=0]\geqslant 1-(k+1)\varepsilon.

(3)

In words, for at least
(1-(k+1)\varepsilon)
-fraction of
(e,t)\in A
, under the assignment
A
, there is perfect agreement with all
v
appearing in
e
and with
v=t
. Consider the restriction
A_{0}=A|_{B}
and view it as an assignment to the PCPP
\Psi_{0}
. Then

\Pr_{e\in E_{0}}[\Phi_{0,e}(A_{0}(e))=1]\geqslant\Pr_{(e,t)\in A}[p(e,t)=0]\geqslant 1-(k+1)\varepsilon\geqslant 1-\frac{\rho\cdot\eta}{2},

(4)

so
A_{0}
satisfies at least
(1-\rho\eta/2)
-fraction of the constraints of
\Psi_{0}
.
Here, in the first transition we used the fact that for a uniformly random
(e,t)\in E
, the distribution of
e
is uniform over
E_{0}
, and also that if
p(e,t)=0
, then
A_{0}
satisfies the constraint
\Phi_{0,e}
.
Combining (
4
) with the soundness guarantee of
Theorem

4.1
we get that the restriction of
A_{0}|_{X}
is
\frac{\eta}{2}
-close to
\mathcal{L}
. Namely, there exists
w\in\mathcal{L}
such that

\Pr_{t\in[n]}[A_{0}(t)\neq w_{t}]\leqslant\frac{\eta}{2}.

(5)

The decoding error of
\Psi_{0}
can now be bounded by:

\displaystyle\Pr_{t\in[n],(e,t)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}}[D_{t}((e,t),A(e,t))\neq w_{t}]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{(e,t)\in E}[A(e,t)(t)\neq w_{t}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Pr_{(e,t)\in E}[A(e,t)(t)\neq w_{t}\land A(e,t)=A_{0}|_{(e,t)}]

\displaystyle+\Pr_{(e,t)\in E}[A(e,t)\neq A_{0}|_{(e,t)}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\eta}{2}+(k+1)\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leqslant\eta.

By
A_{0}|_{(e,t)}
, we mean the restriction of
A_{0}
to the vertices in
e
and
t
. In the last transition we bound the first term using (
5
) and use the fact that when choosing
(e,t)\in E
the element
t
from the tuple is uniformly random in
[n]
. We bound the second term by the probability that
p(e,t)>0
, which is at most
(k+1)\varepsilon
by (
3
).
∎

4.2
Enlarging the Constraint Graph

Here, we describe how to enlarge the constraint graph of a bipartite, projection, left-canonical udPCP. When working with standard PCPs, one can simply create multiple disjoint copies of the constraint graph and be done. It is not hard to see that essentially all properties, including the soundness, of the PCP are preserved when copying the constraint graph. This transformation does not work for udPCPs, since it does not necessarily preserve unique decoding soundness. Indeed, there could be two completely different, fully satisfying assignments on each copy that decode to different members of the language. We circumvent this issue by using the left-canonical property.

Lemma 4.3
.

Suppose that

\mathcal{D}=(A\cup B,E,\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{\Phi_{e}\}_{e\in E},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]})

is a bipartite,
k
-left-regular, projection, left-canonical udPCP for the language
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\Sigma_{0}^{n}
with
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
-unique decoding soundness. Suppose additionally that the marginal of the complete decoding distribution over the vertices is uniform in
A
. Then for all
C\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a
{\sf poly}(C,|A|+|B|)
-time algorithm which takes as input
\mathcal{D}
and outputs a udPCP for
\mathcal{L}
,

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=(A\times[C]\cup B,E^{\prime},\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{\Phi^{\prime}_{e}\}_{e\in E^{\prime}},\{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}\},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]})

with the following properties:

•

Constraint graph.
The constraint graph has
C|A|+|B|
vertices, and each
(a,i)\in A\times[C]
is adjacent to the same vertices that
a
is adjacent to in the original constraint graph.

•

Degrees.
The left degree and the left decoding degree of each vertex is preserved, while the right degree of each vertex is multiplied by
C
.

•

Soundness.

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
has
(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2},\frac{\eta_{2}}{2k})
-unique decoding soundness.

Furthermore, the completeness, decision complexity, decoding complexity, and alphabet sizes of
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
are the same, and the marginal of the complete decoding distribution over the vertices is uniform over
A\times[C]
. If
\mathcal{D}
has projection constraints then
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
does as well and if
\mathcal{D}
is left-canonical then
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is as well.

Proof.

Construct the new udPCP

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=(A\times[C]\cup B,E^{\prime},\{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{\Phi^{\prime}_{e}\}_{e\in E^{\prime}},\{D^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]})

as follows. The edges are
E^{\prime}=\{((a,i),b)\in(A\times[C])\times B\;|\;(a,b)\in E\}
.
The constraint on
((a,i),b)
is the same as that on
(a,b)
in the original udPCP,
\mathcal{D}
. The new decoding distribution
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}
is generated by choosing
a\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}
, choosing
i\in[C]
uniformly at random, and outputting
(a,i)
.
The new decoder
D^{\prime}_{t}
on vertex
(a,i)
is the same as the original decoder
D_{t}
on vertex
a
.

It is clear that completeness is preserved, the alphabets are unchanged, the distribution of
(a,i)\in A\times[C]
is uniform in the complete decoding distribution of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. It is also straightforward to see that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
has projection constraints if
\mathcal{D}
has projection constraints and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is left-canonical if
\mathcal{D}
is left-canonical. Furthermore, the conditions regarding the constraint graph, and degrees are straightforward to check.

We complete the proof by showing
\mathcal{D}
has
(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2},\frac{\eta_{2}}{2k})
-unique decoding soundness.
Suppose
T_{1}:A\times[C]\to\Sigma_{A}
and
T_{2}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
are a
(1-\eta^{\prime})
-satisfying assignments to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
for
\eta^{\prime}:=\frac{\eta_{2}}{2k}
. For each
(a,i)\in A\times[C]
, let
p(a,i)
denote the fraction of constraints involving
(a,i)
that are not satisfied. Since each
(a,i)
has degree at most
k
, we have that if
p(a,i)\neq 0
then
p(a,i)\geqslant\frac{1}{k}
, and
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(a,i)\in A\times[C]}[p(a,i)]\leqslant\eta^{\prime}
. By Markov’s inequality, we have that

\Pr_{(a,i)\in A\times[C]}[p(a,i)=0]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\in A}\left[\Pr_{i\in[C]}[p(a,i)=0]\right]\geqslant 1-k\eta^{\prime}.

(6)

By Markov’s inequality again, we have that for at least
(1-k\eta^{\prime})
-fraction of
a\in A
,
\Pr_{i\in[C]}[p(a,i)=0]>0
, and hence for
(1-k\eta^{\prime})
-fraction of
a
, there exists an assignment in
\mathcal{D}
to
a
which satisfies all of the constraints involving
a
conditioned on the right side being assigned
T_{2}
. We call such
a
good. Let
T^{*}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
be the table such that
T^{*}[a]=T_{1}[(a,i)]
if there exists
i\in[C]
such that
p(a,i)=0
, and
T^{*}[a]
is arbitrary otherwise. Note that since
\mathcal{D}
is left-canonical, if
a
is good then
T_{1}[(a,i)]
is the same for all
i\in[C]
such that
p(a,i)=0
, so the assignment
T^{*}
is well defined.

It follows that for the
T^{*},T_{2}
satisfy
(1-k\eta^{\prime})
-fraction of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}
. Since
k\eta^{\prime}\leqslant\eta_{2}
and
\mathcal{D}
satisfies
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
-unique decoding, we conclude that there exists
w\in\mathcal{L}
such that

\Pr_{t\in[n],a\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}}[D_{t}(a,T^{*}[a])\neq w_{t}]\leqslant\eta_{1}.

(7)

We now bound the decoding error of
T_{1},T_{2}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle\Pr_{t\in[n],a\sim\mathcal{P}_{t},i\in[C]}[D_{t}((a,i),T_{2}[(a,i)])\neq w_{t}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Pr_{t\in[n],a\sim\mathcal{P}_{t},i\in[C]}[T_{2}[(a,i)]\neq T^{*}[a]]+\Pr_{t\in[n],a\sim\mathcal{P}_{t},i\in[C]}[D_{t}(a,T^{*}[a])\neq w_{t}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Pr_{a\in A,i\in[C]}[\text{$a$ is not good}\lor p(a,i)\neq 0]+\eta_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2k\eta^{\prime}+\eta_{1}

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}

In the second transition we are using the assumption that
a\in A
is uniform in the complete decoding distribution of
\mathcal{D}
and that if
a
is good and
p(a,i)=0
, then we must have
T^{*}[a]=T_{2}[(a,i)]
by definition of
T^{*}
. In the third transition we are using (
6
), (
7
) and the fact that at least
(1-k\eta^{\prime})
-fraction of
a\in A
are good.
∎

4.3
Making the udPCP Right-Regular

The next step is to convert the construction in
Lemma

4.2
into a bi-regular one. Towards this end we use the following transformation (similar transformations have been used in the context of standard PCPs in
[
52
,
28
]
).

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}
has a
k
-left-regular projection udPCP
\mathcal{D}=\left(A\cup B,E,\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right)
with
(\eta,\varepsilon)
-unique decoding soundness and minimum right degree
d_{0}
. Then for every integer
d^{\prime}\leqslant d_{0}
, there is a polynomial time transformation which takes
\mathcal{D}
and outputs a udPCP,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\left(A\cup B^{\prime},E^{\prime},\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right)
, for
\mathcal{L}
that is
(d^{\prime}k,d^{\prime})
-regular and has
(\eta,\varepsilon-O(d^{\prime-1/2}))
-unique decoding soundness. Furthermore
|B^{\prime}|=|E|=k\cdot|A|
and if
\mathcal{D}
is left-canonical, then
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is as well.

Proof.

Fix
d^{\prime}
as in the statement. By
Lemma

2.5
we get that for every
N^{\prime}\geqslant d^{\prime}
there is a
{{\sf poly}}(N^{\prime})
-time algorithm that constructs a
d^{\prime}
-regular bipartite expander graph with
N^{\prime}
vertices on each side and second singular value
O(d^{\prime-1/2})
, and we will use these graphs in our construction.

The udPCP
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is constructed as follows. Recall that
(A\cup B,E)
is the constraint graph of
\mathcal{D}
and that for each vertex
b\in B
,
\Gamma(b)
is the neighborhood of
b
in this graph. For each
b\in B
we construct a
d^{\prime}
-regular bipartite expander graph
H_{b}=(A_{b},B_{b},E_{b})
with second singular value
O(d^{\prime-1/2})
, and we identify
A_{b}
with
\Gamma(b)
.
With these expander graphs in mind, the left side of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is
A
, while the right side of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is
\bigcup_{b\in B}B_{b}
. The edges,
E^{\prime}
, are obtained as follows. For each
b\in B
, add the edges
E_{b}
from the graph
H_{b}
, where the left side,
A_{b}
, is identified with
\Gamma(b)
and the right side is
B_{b}
. Overall, the edges are
E^{\prime}=\cup_{b\in B}E_{b}
. Note that choosing
(a,b)\in E
uniformly at random and
b^{\prime}\in B_{b}
adjacent to
a
uniformly at random, the edge
(a,b^{\prime})\in E^{\prime}
is uniformly random.

The left and right alphabets of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
are still
\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B}
and the decoders of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
are still
\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}
. For each
(a,b^{\prime})\in E^{\prime}
, if
b^{\prime}\in B_{b}
, the constraint on
(a,b^{\prime})
is the same as the constraint on
(a,b)
in
\mathcal{D}
. Finally the decoding distributions for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
are the same as in
\mathcal{D}
.

This completes the description of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, and we now move on to the analysis.
It is clear from the construction that
|B^{\prime}|=\sum_{b\in B}|\Gamma(b)|=|E|=k|A|
. Also,
\mathcal{D^{\prime}}
is
(d^{\prime}k,d^{\prime})
-regular as each neighbor
b
of
a\in A
in
\mathcal{D}
contributes
d^{\prime}
neighbors for
a
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. We move on to showing that the left-canonical property is preserved (if
\mathcal{D}
satisfies it) as well as verifying completeness and soundness. Throughout, we write
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\sim H_{b}
to denote a uniformly random edge from
H_{b}
.

The left-canonical property is preserved:

suppose
\mathcal{D}
is left-canonical, and consider any vertex
a\in A
, let
b_{1},\ldots,b_{k}
be neighbors of
a
in
\mathcal{D}
, and let
b^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,b^{\prime}_{k}
be neighbors of
a
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, where
b^{\prime}_{i}\in B_{b_{i}}
for each
i\in[k]
. Fix any assignment to
b^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,b^{\prime}_{k}
, and consider the same assignment to
b_{1},\ldots,b_{k}
. Since
\mathcal{D}
is left-canonical, there is at most one assignment to
a
satisfying all of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}
, and since the constraints between
(a,b_{i})
in
\mathcal{D}
and
(a,b^{\prime}_{i})
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
are the same, there is also at most one assignment to
a
satisfying all of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. Hence,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is left-canonical as well.

Perfect Completeness:

fix
w\in\mathcal{L}
; by the perfect completeness of
\mathcal{D}
, there are assignments
T_{1}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
and
T_{2}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
that satisfy all of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}
, and additionally for each
t\in[n]
, the decoder
D_{t}
outputs
w_{t}
with probability
1
.
We take the assignments
T_{1}
and
T_{2}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, where
T^{\prime}_{2}:B^{\prime}\to\Sigma_{B}
is defined by
T^{\prime}_{2}[b^{\prime}]=T_{2}[b]
if
b^{\prime}\in B_{b}
. It is clear that
T_{1}
and
T_{2}^{\prime}
satisfy all of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and that for all
t\in[n]
, the decoder
D_{t}^{\prime}
outputs
w_{t}
with probability
1
.

Unique Decoding Soundness:

fix assignments
T_{1}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
and
T_{2}^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\to\Sigma_{B}
to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, and suppose that
T_{1},T_{2}^{\prime}
satisfy at least
\left(1-\varepsilon^{\prime}\right)
-fraction of the constraints, where
\varepsilon^{\prime}:=\varepsilon-O(d^{-1/2})
. For each
b\in B
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}
define

X_{b,\sigma}=\{b^{\prime}\in B_{b}\;|\;T^{\prime}_{2}[b^{\prime}]=\sigma\}\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{b,\sigma}=\{a^{\prime}\in A_{b}\;|\;\Phi_{(a^{\prime},b)}(T_{1}[a^{\prime}],\sigma)=1\}.

In words,
X_{b,\sigma}
is the set of vertices in
B_{b}
that are assigned
\sigma
under
T^{\prime}_{2}
, and
Y_{b,\sigma}
is the set of vertices in
a^{\prime}\in\Gamma(b)
such that the assignments
a\to T_{1}[a^{\prime}]
and
b\to\sigma
satisfy the constraint on
(a^{\prime},b)
in
\mathcal{D}
. It is clear that for each
b\in b
, the sets
\{X_{b,\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}
form a partition of
B_{b}
. Additionally, since the constraints in
\mathcal{D}
are all projections, for all
b\in B
the sets
\{Y_{b,\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}
form a partition of
A_{b}
. Define
F_{b,\sigma}:A_{b}\to[0,1]
,
G_{b,\sigma}:B_{b}\to\{0,1\}
by

G_{b,\sigma}(b^{\prime}):=\mathbbm{1}_{b^{\prime}\in X_{b,\sigma}}\quad\text{and}\quad F_{b,\sigma}(a^{\prime}):=\mathbbm{1}_{a^{\prime}\in Y_{b,\sigma}},

and denote
\mu(F_{b,\sigma})=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\in A_{b}}[F_{b,\sigma}(a^{\prime})]
and
\mu(G_{b,\sigma})=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b^{\prime}\in B_{b}}[G_{b,\sigma}(b^{\prime})]
.
Consider the randomized assignment
T^{*}_{2}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
obtained by setting
T^{*}_{2}(b)=\sigma
with probability
\mu(G_{b,\sigma})
. The expected fraction of constraints in
\mathcal{D}
satisfied by
T_{1}
and
T^{*}_{2}
is

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim B}\left[\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mu(G_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(F_{b,\sigma})]\right],

(8)

where
b\sim B
is sampled according to the stationary distribution over
b\in B
in the constraint graph of
\mathcal{D}
.
Next, the fraction of constraints satisfied by
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim B}\left[\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\sim H_{b}}[F_{b,\sigma}(a^{\prime})\cdot G_{b,\sigma}(b^{\prime})]\right]\geqslant 1-\varepsilon^{\prime},

(9)

where the inequality is because we assumed that
T_{1},T_{2}
satisfy at least
(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})
-fraction of constraints in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

Now fix a
b\in B
and a
\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}
. We will attempt to relate the inner expectation from (
9
) to a product in the summation of (
8
). By
Lemma

2.3
we get

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\sim H_{b}}[F_{b,\sigma}(a^{\prime})\cdot G_{b,\sigma}(b^{\prime})]\leqslant\mu(F_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(G_{b,\sigma})+O(d^{\prime-1/2})\cdot\sqrt{\mu(F_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(G_{b,\sigma})}.

Keeping
b\in B
fixed and summing this inequality over all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\sim H_{b}}[F_{b,\sigma}(a^{\prime})\cdot G_{b,\sigma}(b^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mu(F_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(G_{b,\sigma})+O(d^{\prime-1/2})\cdot\sqrt{\mu(F_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(G_{b,\sigma})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mu(F_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(G_{b,\sigma})+O(d^{\prime-1/2}),

where the second inequality uses Cauchy-Schwarz and the fact that
\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mu(G_{b,\sigma})\leqslant 1
and
\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mu(F_{b,\sigma})=1
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim B}\left[\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mu(F_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(G_{b,\sigma})]\right]\geqslant\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim B}\left[\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\sim H_{b}}[F_{b,\sigma}(a^{\prime})\cdot G_{b,\sigma}(b^{\prime})]\right]-O(d^{\prime-1/2})\geqslant 1-\varepsilon^{\prime}-O(d^{\prime-1/2}),

which is at least
1-\varepsilon
by the choice of
\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
It follows that there exists a choice for
T_{2}^{*}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
such that
T_{1}
and
T_{2}^{*}
satisfy at least
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction of constraints in
\mathcal{D}
. By the
(\eta,\varepsilon)
-unique decoding of
\mathcal{D}
, there exists
w\in\mathcal{L}
such that

\Pr_{t\in[n],a\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}}[D_{t}(a,T_{1}[a])\neq w_{t}]\leqslant\eta.

Notice that this is exactly the same as the decoding error of
\mathcal{D^{\prime}}
with assignments
T_{1},T_{2}
. This is because for all
t
,
\mathcal{D}_{t}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{t}
are the same function and the marginal distribution over
A
in
\mathcal{P}_{t}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}
is the same. Thus, we have

\Pr_{t\in[n],a^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\prime}}[D^{\prime}_{t}(a^{\prime},T_{1}[a^{\prime}])\neq w_{t}]=\Pr_{t\in[n],a\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}}[D_{t}(a,T_{1}[a])\neq w_{t}]\leqslant\eta.

It follows that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
satisfies
(\eta,\varepsilon-O(d^{-1/2}))
-unique decoding soundness.
∎

4.4
A Bi-regular Uniquely-decodable PCP of Arbitrary Size

In this section we put all of the previous pieces together to obtain a final udPCP that will go into the routing transformation of the next round. We highlight the following important properties:

•

The constraint graph is
d^{\prime}
-regular and bipartite.

•

The number of vertices in the constraint graph can be multiplied by any arbitrary
C\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

The marginal of the complete decoding distribution over the vertices is uniform.

We remark that the last item above is in contrast to our original udPCP, from
Lemma

4.2
, whose complete decoding distribution has a marginal which is uniform over only a subset of the vertices.

Lemma 4.5
.

There is
\rho^{\prime}>0
such that for every
\eta>0
there is a sufficiently large
d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that the following holds. For any alphabet
\Sigma_{0}
, there exists a function
s(N)=O_{|\Sigma_{0}|,\eta}\left(N\cdot{\sf poly}(\log(N))\right)
such that for any
C\in\mathbb{N}
and any size-
N
circuit
\varphi:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
, the language
{\sf SAT}(\varphi)
has a
udPCP which satisfies the following properties:

•

Constraint Graph.
The constraint graph is a
d^{\prime}
-regular bipartite graph.

•

Length.

C\cdot s(N)
.

•

Alphabet Size.

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(1)
.

•

Degrees.
The left and right degree are both
d^{\prime}
and the decoding degree is
1
.

•

Complete Decoding Distribution.
The marginal of the complete decoding distribution over the vertices is uniform.

•

Completeness.
The udPCP has perfect completeness.

•

Soundness.
The udPCP has
\left(\eta,\rho^{\prime}\cdot\eta^{5}\right)
-unique decoding soundness.

Proof.

Fix
\eta>0
and take the bipartite,
(k+1)
-left regular, projection, left-canonical udPCP
\mathcal{D}
, from
Lemma

4.2
with
\left(\eta,\frac{\rho\cdot\eta}{2(k+1)}\right)
-unique decoding soundness. Let
(A\cup B,E)
be the constraint graph of this udPCP, set
d^{\prime}=\Theta(\eta^{-2})
, and fix an arbitrary
C\in\mathbb{N}
. Also set
s(N)=2d^{\prime}(k+1)|A|=O_{|\Sigma_{0}|,\eta}\left(N\cdot{\sf poly}(\log(N))\right)
. Let
\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}
and
\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}
be the decoders and decoding distributions respectively of
\mathcal{D}
.

We start by repeating every edge in
\mathcal{D}

d^{\prime}
-times so that the minimum degree on the right hand side is
d^{\prime}
. Now apply
Lemma

4.3
with repetition parameter
C
. At this point, we get a
d^{\prime}(k+1)
-left regular udPCP which has
C|A|
vertices on the left,
|B|
vertices on the right, and
(\eta+\frac{\rho\eta}{2(k+1)},\frac{\rho\eta}{4(k+1)^{2}})
-unique decoding soundness.

Next, we apply
Lemma

4.4
with degree parameter
d^{\prime}=O(\eta^{-2})
chosen sufficiently large so that
O(d^{\prime-1/2})
is sufficiently small relative to
\frac{\rho\eta}{4(k+1)^{2}}
. Let
R=d^{\prime 2}(k+1)
. The resulting udPCP’s constraint graph has
C|A|
vertices on the left,
C|A|d^{\prime}(k+1)
vertices on the right, is
(R,d^{\prime})
-bi-regular, and has
(\eta+\frac{\rho\eta}{2(k+1)},\frac{\rho\eta}{5(k+1)^{2}})
-unique decoding soundness.

We then apply
Lemma

4.3
one more time with repetition parameter
d^{\prime}(k+1)
to obtain an
R
-regular udPCP. Since the constraint graph is fully regular, the number of vertices is just twice the number on either side, so the length of the resulting udPCP is
2Cd^{\prime}(k+1)|A|=C\cdot s(N)
. Let
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
be the resulting udPCP and
G^{\prime}=(A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime},E^{\prime})
be its constraint graph. Note that
G^{\prime}
is
R
-regular and
|A^{\prime}|=|B^{\prime}|
.
Since
C
was arbitrary, we can indeed obtain length
C\cdot s(N)
for any
C\in\mathbb{N}
for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. Since
\rho
and
k
are constants and
d^{\prime}
is large enough, one can check through
Lemma

4.3
that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
has
(1.1\eta,\frac{\rho\eta}{5(k+1)^{2}R})
-unique decoding soundness. Here we use the fact that

\eta+\frac{\rho\eta}{2(k+1)}+\frac{\rho\eta}{5(k+1)^{2}}\leqslant 1.1\eta.

It is also straightforward to verify that perfect completeness is preserved from
\mathcal{D}
, the alphabets are the same as
\mathcal{D}
, that the left decoding degree is still
1
as in
\mathcal{D}
, and that the marginal of the complete decoding distribution over the vertices is
uniform over
A
, the left side of the constraint graph.

Now we will further modify
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, to obtain a new udPCP,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}
whose parts are as follows. In the below, recall that each
a\in A
has decoding degree
1
, so we denote by
t(a)\in[n]
its only decoding neighbor.

•

Constraint Graph.
Starting with
G^{\prime}
, the constraint graph of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, choose an arbitrary matching
\pi:A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
, meaning the edges of the matching are
\{(a,\pi(a))\}_{a\in A^{\prime}}
. Add
R
copies of this matching to the graph (meaning between each
a
and
\pi(a)
, we add
R
parallel edges). The resulting
2R
-regular graph,
G^{\prime\prime}=(A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime})
, is the constraint graph of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}
.

•

Alphabet.
The alphabet for each vertex in
A^{\prime}
is the same as in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.For each right vertex
b\in B^{\prime}
, we require its alphabet to additionally include one
\Sigma_{0}
symbol. It is intended that this symbol holds the
t(\pi^{-1}(b))
-th index of some satisfying assignment.

•

Decoding Distribution.
For each
t\in[n]
, the decoding distribution
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}_{t}
now samples
a\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}
, and then outputs one of
a
or
\pi(a)
uniformly at random.

•

Decoder.
For each
a\in A^{\prime}
and symbol
\sigma
for
a
, the decoder
D^{\prime\prime}_{t}(a,\sigma)
is unchanged and outputs
D_{t}(a,\sigma)
. For
b=\pi(a)\in B^{\prime}
, the decoder outputs the
\Sigma_{0}
symbol held in
b
’s symbol (we note that it supposedly corresponds to what the decoder would have output if given the vertex
t(\pi^{-1}(b))=t(a)
and its label).

•

Constraints.
For each
(a,b)\in E^{\prime\prime}
, if
b\neq\pi(a)
, then
the constraint on
(a,b)
checks the constraint from
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. Specifically, given labels
\sigma_{a}
for
a
and
\sigma_{b}
for
b
is as follows. Recall that
\sigma_{b}
contains a label for
b
in the udPCP
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. Let
\sigma^{\prime}_{b}
be this symbol. The constraint checks if
\sigma_{a},\sigma^{\prime}_{b}
satisfy the constraint on
(a,b)
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. If
b=\pi(a)
, then the constraint checks the previous condition and in addition checks that the symbols held by
a
and
\pi(a)
lead to the same decoding under
D^{\prime}_{t}
. That is, given symbols
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
, the constraint additionally checks if
D^{\prime}_{t}(a,\sigma)=D^{\prime}_{t}(b,\sigma^{\prime})
.

We now verify that the guarantees of the lemma are still satisfied after the above modification. It is clear that the length is unchanged, the alphabet size only increases by a constant factor, the constraint graph is
2R
-regular, every vertex has decoding degree
1
, and perfect completeness is preserved. Regarding the new decoding distribution, it is clear that choosing
t\in[n]
and sampling
v\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}_{t}
, we have that
v
is uniformly random in
A^{\prime\prime}\cup B^{\prime\prime}
. This is because, choosing
t\in[n]
and
a\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}
we have that
a
is uniformly random in the left side. Since
\pi
is a matching, if we then output
a
or
\pi(a)
uniformly at random, the resulting vertex is uniform in the whole graph.

Finally, we show that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies
(1.11\eta,\frac{\rho\eta}{10(k+1)^{2}R})
unique decoding soundness. Suppose
T
is an assignment to
A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}
satisfying at least
(1-\frac{\rho\eta}{10(k+1)^{2}R})
-fraction of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. Abusing notation, we will also think of
T
as an assignment to
\mathcal{D}
by removing the
\Sigma_{0}
symbol attached to each
T(b)
.

Let us split the constraints into
E_{0}
, consisting of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}
, and
E_{1}
consisting of the new constraints added from the matching. Since each of
E_{0},E_{1}
consists of half of the overall constraints, we get that the fraction of satisfied constraints in both
E_{0},E_{1}
is at least
\left(1-\frac{\rho\eta}{5(k+1)^{2}R}\right)
-fraction of the constraints there, and hence applying the unique-decoding soundness of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, we get that there exists a
w\in{\sf SAT}(\varphi)
such that

\Pr_{t\in[n],a\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}}[D^{\prime}_{t}(a,T[a])\neq w_{t}]\leqslant 1.1\eta.

(10)

On the other hand the decoding error of
T
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}
relative to
w
can be bounded by

\begin{split}&\Pr_{t\in[n],a\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}}[D^{\prime\prime}_{t}(a,T[a])\neq w_{t}\lor D^{\prime\prime}_{t}(\pi(a),T[\pi(a)])\neq w_{t}]\\
&\leqslant\Pr_{t\in[n],a\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}}[D^{\prime}_{t}(a,T[a])\neq w_{t}]+\Pr_{t\in[n],a\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}}[D^{\prime}_{t}(\pi(a),T[\pi(a)])\neq D^{\prime}_{t}(a,T[a])]\\
&\leqslant 1.1\eta+\frac{\rho\eta}{5(k+1)^{2}R}\\
&\leqslant 1.11\eta,\end{split}

(11)

where in the third transition we bound the first term using (
10
) and the second term using the fact that it is at most the fraction of unsatisfied constraints in
E_{1}
. This shows that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}
has
(1.11\eta,\frac{\rho\eta}{10(k+1)^{2}R})
-unique decoding soundness which is as desired because
R=O(\eta^{-2})
.
∎

5
Routing Onto an HDX

In this section, we show how to transform
the udPCP construction from
Lemma

4.5
into a udPCP whose constraint graph is the base layer of an HDX from
Theorem

2.9
, and for that we use the fault-tolerant routing protocol of
[
8
]
.
The main difference is that we analyze unique-decoding soundness, whereas
[
8
]
analyze the standard notion of soundness.

Theorem 5.1
.

For every sufficiently small
\eta>0
, any
\delta\in(0,1)
, sufficiently large
C^{\prime}
, sufficiently large
k\in\mathbb{N}
and sufficiently large
d\in\mathbb{N}
the following holds. Any circuit
\varphi:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
of size
N
has a udPCP
\mathcal{D}
which satisfies the following properties:

•

Constraint graph.
Let
X
be the complex constructed from the algorithm in
Theorem

2.9
where the direct product soundness, exponent, direct-product dimension, target number of vertices, and prime parameters are set to
\delta
,
C^{\prime}
,
k
,
d
,
N\cdot{\sf poly}(\log N)
,
\Theta(\log^{C^{\prime}}(N))
respectively. The constraint graph of
\mathcal{D}
is
(X(1),X(2))
, where the multiplicity of each edge
(u,v)
is proportional to the weight of
(u,v)
in stationary distribution over
(X(1),X(2))
. The udPCP has length
|X(1)|=O_{|\Sigma_{0}|,\eta,\delta}(N{\sf poly}(\log(N))
.

•

Degrees.
Each vertex in the constraint graph has degree
{\sf poly}_{\delta}(\log N)
and decoding degree
{\sf poly}_{\delta}(\log N)
.

•

Alphabet Size.

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}\left(2^{{\sf poly}_{\eta,\delta}(\log(N))}\right)
.

•

Decision Complexity.

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\eta,\delta}(\log(N)))
.

•

Decoding Distribution.
The marginal of the complete decoding distribution over the vertices is the stationary distribution of
(X(1),X(2))
over
X(1)
.

•

Decoding Complexity.

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\eta,\delta}(\log N))
.

•

Unique-decoding Soundness.
The udPCP has
(\eta,\eta^{2}/6)
-unique decoding soundness.

5.1
The Zig-Zag Product

In order to convert from a udPCP over an arbitrary constraint graph
G=(V,E)
into a udPCP over the base layer
(X(1),X(2))
of an HDX, we need a way to associate the vertices of
G
with the vertices
X(1)
. As in
[
8
]
, to do so we rely on the zig-zag product of
[
55
]
, which we present next.

The zig-zag product takes a graph
G=(V,E)
, with minimum degree
m_{0}
, and a family of
k
-regular expander graphs
\mathcal{G}=\{\mathcal{G}_{m}\}_{m\geqslant m_{0}}
where
\mathcal{G}_{m}
has
m
vertices, and outputs a
k^{2}
-regular graph on
2|E|
vertices. We denote the output of the zig-zag product as
\ZZ(G,\mathcal{G})
. Typically, the family of graphs
\mathcal{G}
is chosen to have second singular value at most
\sigma
, for some chosen parameter
\sigma
.
Before describing the zig-zag product, it is convenient to describe the replacement product of
G^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}
. We denote this graph by
\Rep(G,\mathcal{G})
, defined in the following way.

•

For each vertex
v
, arbitrarily order its neighbors from
1
to
\deg(v)
. We will refer to the
c
th neighbor of
v
as the vertex labeled
c
under this ordering.

•

Replace each vertex
v\in V^{\prime}
with a copy of
\mathcal{G}_{\deg(v)}
, where
\deg(v)
is the degree of
v
in
G^{\prime}
. Hence
v
is replaced by a cloud of
\deg(v)
vertices, and we also label these vertices by
\{v\}\times[\deg(v)]
.

•

Let
(u,v)\in E^{\prime}
be an edge where
v
is the
c_{1}
th neighbor of
u
and
u
is the
c_{2}
th neighbor of
v
, then we add the edge
((u,c_{1}),(v,c_{2}))
to
\Rep(G,\mathcal{G})
. In addition, we add the edge
((v,c_{1}),(v,c_{2}))
to
\Rep(G,\mathcal{G})
for all
v
and
(c_{1},c_{2})
such that
((v,c_{1}),(v,c_{2}))
is an edge in
\mathcal{G}_{\deg(v)}
.

Note that
\Rep(G,\mathcal{G})
has
2|E|
vertices and is
(k+1)
-regular. We define the zig-zag product,
\ZZ(G^{\prime},\mathcal{G})
as follows.

•

The vertex set is
V(\ZZ(G,\mathcal{G}))=V(\Rep(G,\mathcal{G}))
.

•

We add an edge
((u,c_{1}),(v,c_{4}))
to
E(\ZZ(G,\mathcal{G}))
if there exist
c_{2}
and
c_{3}
such that all of
((u,c_{1}),(u,c_{2}))
,
((u,c_{2}),(v,c_{3}))
,
((v,c_{3}),(v,c_{4}))
are edges in
E(\Rep(G,\mathcal{G}))
. In words, the vertex
(u,c_{1})
is adjacent to
(v,c_{4})
if the latter vertex can be reached, in
\Rep(G,\mathcal{G})
, by first taking a step in the cloud of
u
, then taking a step between the clouds
u
and
v
, and finally taking a step in the cloud of
v
.

It is straightforward to verify that
\ZZ(G,\mathcal{G})
has
2|E|
vertices and is
k^{2}
regular.

5.2
Routing Protocols

We now discuss routing protocols and associated useful notations, and we start by explaining what a
T
-round routing protocol on a graph
G=(V,E)
is. It has three stages: input, communication, and output, described as follows.

Input.
Each vertex
v\in V
holds an initial message, which we think of as a symbol from some global alphabet
\Sigma
.

Communication.
During each round
r\in[T]
, each vertex
v\in V
performs some computation and sends a message, which is a symbol from
\Sigma
, to each of its neighbors. The message to each neighbor may be different. Moreover, the computation
v
makes depends on its initial message and any messages received from its neighbors in prior rounds. The history of messages
v
sent/received before step
r
is called the
transcript
of
v
in round
r
, so that
v
’s outgoing messages during round
r
are functions of this transcript. For
r=1
,
v
’s message depends only on its initial message.

Output.
After round
T
, each vertex calculates an output (as some function of its transcript in round
T
).

Definition 5.2
.

Given an arbitrary graph
G=(V,E)
, a
T
-round routing protocol on
G
, which we denote by
\mathcal{R}
, consists of the following items.

•

Initial Message.
An initial message
A:V\to\Sigma
where
\Sigma
is some alphabet.

•

Out Maps.
For each round
r\in[T]
, a map
\outmap_{r}:2E\times\Sigma^{*}\to\Sigma
, where
\outmap_{r}(u,v,\sigma)
denotes the message that
u
sends to
v
in round
r
given that
u
’s transcript at round
r
is
\sigma
. Here
2E
consists of all ordered pairs of adjacent vertices, meaning each edge
(u,v)\in E
appears as
(u,v)
and
(v,u)
in
2E
, and the map is defined only for
\sigma
which represent a valid transcript of the protocol up to step
r
.

•

In Maps.
For each round
r\in[T]
, a map
\inmap_{r}:2E\to\Sigma
where for each
r\in[T]
,
\inmap_{r}(u,v)
should be thought of as the message received by
v
from vertex
u
in round
r
of the protocol.

•

Output
. A function
A_{T}:V\times\Sigma^{*}\to\Sigma
, where
A_{T}(v,\sigma)
is the symbol output by
v
at the end of the routing protocol given that its transcript at the end of the routing protocol is
\sigma
. We refer to
A_{T}
as the function computed by the protocol.

We will also have to consider a quantity known as the
work complexity
of a routing protocol.

Definition 5.3
.

The work complexity of a
T
-round routing protocol over
G=(V,E)
is the maximum over
r\in[T]
and
(u,v)\in 2E
, of the circuit complexity of the function
\outmap_{r}(u,v,\cdot):\Sigma^{*}\to\Sigma
.

Although we are able to use the routing protocol of
[
8
]
in a black-box way, it will still be helpful to give a brief description of why routing protocols are useful for transforming the constraint graph of udPCPs. We refer the reader to
[
8
]
for a more detailed discussion.

In the setting of udPCPs, routing protocols are used as follows. Let
\mathcal{D}
be an initial udPCP with bipartite,
k
-regular constraint graph
G=(A\cup B,E)
, and suppose that
G^{\prime}=(V,E^{\prime})
is the target graph which we want to use as the constraint graph of a new udPCP,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. Suppose for this exposition that
G
is a matching, that
|V|=|A|=|B|
, and let
\pi:A\to B
denote this matching. Identifying both
A
and
B
with
V
, we can view
\pi
as a permutation on
V
, i.e. as a mapping
\pi:V\to V
. Now the initial message
A:V\to\Sigma
to the routing protocol should be thought of as the assignment of
A
and the goal of the communication of the routing protocol is to pass the value
A(v)
, which is initially held at vertex
v
, to the vertex
\pi(v)
. If this is done correctly, then each vertex
\pi(v)
can check if the assignment
A
actually satisfied the constraint on
(v,\pi(v))
in the original udPCP. Hence, the desired output function is
A_{T}(\pi(v),\sigma)=A(v)
, where here
\sigma
is the transcript of the honest routing protocol received by vertex
\pi(v)
. Assuming
\pi(v)
correctly computes
A_{T}(\pi(v),\sigma)=A(v)
, it can check itself if
A(v)
and its own initial value
A(\pi(v))
should satisfy the constraint on
(v,\pi(v))
.

The above gives a high level overview of the connection between routing protocols and transforming constraint graphs of udPCPs, and roughly speaking it describes what happens in the perfect completeness case. In order to show soundness, one has to consider what happens to the routing protocol under adversarial edge corruptions.

Definition 5.4
.

We say that an edge
(u,v)
is adversarially corrupted if, for every round, the outgoing message from
u
may be an adversarially selected symbol in
\Sigma
.

In
[
8
]
, routing protocols are considered in a slightly different setting. There, the target graph
G=(X(1),X(2))
is the base layer of an arbitrary HDX,
X
, and one considers the zig-zag product
Z=\ZZ(G,\mathcal{G})
, where
\mathcal{G}
is an appropriately chosen family of expanders. Instead of considering a function directly over the vertices of
G
, one considers a function over the zig-zag product,
Z
, which is then associated with
G
. Strictly speaking, the zig-zag product is not necessary to describe the
[
8
]
routing protocol, but it is helpful to have it in mind as it virtually appears in the definition of the initial messages and the function computed. We will similarly use
Z
and below we describe how routing protocols over
Z
translate to protocols over
G
.

Let us label the vertices of
Z
as
V(Z)=\{(u,v)\;|\;u\in X(1),(u,v)\in X(2)\}

and let the initial messages over
V(Z)
be given by
A^{\prime}_{0}:V(Z)\to\Sigma
. Fix a permutation
\pi:V(Z)\to V(Z)
. The goal of the routing protocol is to transmit
A^{\prime}_{0}(v)
to
A^{\prime}_{0}(\pi(v))
for all
v\in V(Z)
. The actual routing protocol should take place over
G
though, so we would like a way to simulate the initial messages and final function (which are over
V(Z)
), as functions over
X(1)
. To this end, define
\mathcal{H}=\{(j,u)\;|\;j\in V(Z),u\in X_{j_{1}}(1)\}
, and define the function
A_{0}:\mathcal{H}\to\Sigma
, where
A_{0}(j,u)=A^{\prime}_{0}(j)
. Now, we think of the initial message of a vertex
u\in X(1)
as holding all of the symbols
(A_{0}(j,u))_{j\in V(Z)\text{ such that }u\in X_{j_{1}}(1)}
. One can think of the function
A_{0}
as a function over the links of
X
: for each
j\in V(Z)
, each vertex
u\in X_{j_{1}}(1)
contains the symbol of
j
(among other things). Then, the routing protocol over
Z
naturally translates over to a link-to-link transfer protocol over
G
.

Below we describe the formal guarantee of the routing protocol from
[
8
, Lemma 4.6]
. In the soundness case, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 5.5
.

There exist
\varepsilon_{0},\alpha>0
and
d_{0},C^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that the following holds for sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a family of expanders, each with second singular value at most
\frac{\alpha}{2}
, and let
X
be any complex constructed as in
Theorem

2.9
with dimension parameter, exponent, target number of vertices, and prime set to
d\geqslant d_{0}
,
C^{\prime}
,
n
, and
q=\Theta\left(\log^{C^{\prime}}(n)\right)
respectively.
Let
Z=\ZZ(G,\mathcal{G})
, and let
\pi:V(Z)\to V(Z)
be any permutation over
V(Z)
.

Then there is a routing protocol on
G=(X(1),X(2))
with round complexity
T=O(\log(n))
and work complexity
q^{O(d^{2})}\log|\Sigma|
such that for any initial function
A_{0}:\mathcal{G}\to\Sigma
which satisfies

\Pr_{j\in V(Z)}[{\sf Maj}_{0.99}(A_{0}(j,u))\;|\;u\sim X_{j_{1}}(1))\neq\perp]\geqslant 1-\xi,

and for all possible adversarial corruptions of at most
\varepsilon
-fraction of edges with
\varepsilon\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}
, the protocol computes a function
A_{T}:\mathcal{G}\to\Sigma
which satisfies

\Pr_{j\in V(Z)}[{\sf Maj}_{0.99}(A_{T}(\pi(j),w)\;|\;w\sim X_{\pi(j)_{1}}(1))={\sf Maj}_{0.99}(A_{T}(j,u)\;|\;u\sim X_{\pi(j)_{1}}(1))]\geqslant 1-\xi-\frac{1}{\log n}.

In words,
Lemma

5.5
gives, for any permutation
\pi
over
V(Z)
, a routing protocol that is tolerant against up to
\varepsilon
errors. If an overwhelming majority value exists in at least
1-\xi
of the links in the input message, then the same overwhelming majority value exists in at least
1-\xi-\frac{1}{\log n}
of the links in the output message.

The completeness of the protocol from
Lemma

5.5
is stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
G=(X(1),X(2))
where
X=(X(1),\ldots,X(d))
is a
d
-dimensional complex, let
\mathcal{G}
be an appropriate family of expanders as above and let
A_{0}:\mathcal{G}\to\Sigma^{\prime}
be a function such that
A_{0}(j,v)
is the same for all
v\in X_{j_{1}}(1)
. Then for each permutation
\pi\colon V(Z)\to V(Z)
, an honest run of the protocol from
Lemma

5.5
satisfies

A_{0}(j,v)=A_{T}(\pi(j),u)

for every
(j,v),(\pi(j),u)\in\mathcal{G}
.

We will require the following fact from
[
8
]
which translates the uniform distributions over vertices and edges of
Z
to the stationary distributions over vertices and edges of
X
.

Fact 5.7
.

Choosing
j\sim V(Z)
uniformly at random and outputting
j_{1}
, we have that
j_{1}\in X(1)
is distributed according to the underlying distribution of
X(1)
. Additionally, choosing an edge
(j,j^{\prime})
of
Z
uniformly at random and outputting
(j_{1},j^{\prime}_{1})\in X(2)
, we have that
(j_{1},j^{\prime}_{1})
is distributed according to the underlying distribution of
X(2)
.

5.3
Proof of
Theorem

5.1

In this section we prove
Theorem

5.1
.

5.3.1
The Construction

Fix
\eta>0
,

\delta\in(0,1)
, a size
N
circuit
\varphi:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
and set
\mathcal{L}={\sf SAT}(\varphi)\subseteq\Sigma_{0}^{n}
. Let

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\left(A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime},E^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime},\{\Phi_{e}\}_{e\in E^{\prime}},\{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{D^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right)

to be the udPCP for
\mathcal{L}
guaranteed by
Lemma

4.5
with
\left(\frac{\eta}{100},\rho^{\prime}\cdot\left(\frac{\eta}{100}\right)^{5}\right)
-unique decoding soundness.
Note that by
Lemma

4.5
, we may take the length of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
to be
C\cdot s(N)
for any
C\in\mathbb{N}
, where
s(N)=O_{|\Sigma_{0}|,\eta}(N\cdot\log^{c_{0}}(N))
for some constant
c_{0}
. Here we are slightly abusing notation as technically, there is a separate udPCP for each
C
, but let us just refer to the udPCP as
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
for now and fix
C
later. The udPCP
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is bipartite and
d^{\prime}
-regular. We denote its constraint graph by
G^{\prime}=(A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime},E^{\prime})
, write
V^{\prime}=A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}
, and set
N^{\prime}=|V^{\prime}|
.

Take
\varepsilon_{0},\alpha,d_{0},C^{\prime}
to be the constants from
Lemma

5.5
and let
\mathcal{G}
be a family of
O(\alpha^{-2})
-regular expander graphs such that each graph has second singular value at most
\frac{\alpha}{2}
. In particular, we use
Lemma

2.4
where
d=O(\alpha^{-2})
, and obtain a family
\mathcal{G}
with one graph of each size
m
for all
m\geqslant\Omega(\alpha^{-2})
. Note that by
Lemma

2.4
,
each graph in the family
\mathcal{G}
can be constructed in time polynomial in the number of vertices.
We take the complex
X
from
Theorem

2.9
so that the following items hold:

•

the direct-product soundness parameter is set to the
\delta
fixed above,

•

the dimension parameter
d\geqslant d_{0}
,

•

the number of vertices satisfies
N\cdot\log^{c_{0}+10}(N)\leqslant|X(1)|\leqslant N\cdot\log^{10c_{0}+10}(N)
, and is sufficiently large in accordance with
Lemma

5.5
,

•

the prime
q
is set to
q=\log^{c^{\prime}}(N)
for some constant
c^{\prime}
,

•

N\cdot\log^{c_{0}+10}(N)\leqslant|X(2)|\leqslant N\cdot\log^{c_{1}}(N)
, where
c_{1}\geqslant c_{0}+10
is some constant. This item is implied by the fact that
d
is constant along with the prior two items and item 3 of
Theorem

2.9
.

Let us briefly argue why such a complex
X
can be constructed from
Theorem

2.9
. There, one can fix
d
arbitrarily and then choose a desired number of vertices, say
n^{\prime}
, arbitrarily. In our case, we set
n^{\prime}
to be in the range
[N\cdot\log^{c_{0}+10}(N),N\cdot\log^{10c_{0}+10}(N)]
, and note that we can assume
n^{\prime}
is sufficiently large in accordance with
Lemma

5.5
by assuming that the original circuit is sufficiently large. After
n^{\prime}
is fixed, we set
q=\Theta(\log^{C^{\prime}}(n^{\prime}))
and we run the algorithm of
Theorem

2.9
with direct-product soundness, exponent, dimension, target number of vertices, and prime parameters set to
\delta,C^{\prime},d,n^{\prime},q
and direct-product dimension set arbitrarily.
Theorem

2.9
guarantees that this
X
is constructed in
{\sf poly}(n^{\prime})={\sf poly}(N)
time.

Viewing
(X(1),X(2))
as a multigraph.

We will want to consider
(X(1),X(2))
as a weighted graph according to its stationary distribution and this is accomplished by having each edge
(u,v)\in X(2)
have multiplicity proportional to its weight under the stationary distribution. Specifically, recall that the stationary distribution over
(u,v)
samples a
d
-face
U\sim X(d)
and then

\{u,v\}\in\binom{U}{2}
uniformly, so the weight of
(u,v)
under this distribution is,
\frac{m(u,v)}{\binom{d}{2}}
, where
m(u,v)
is the number of
d
-faces containing
u
and
v
, and is hence
{\sf poly}(\log(N))
. For each
(u,v)\in X(2)
, we think of it as having multiplicity
m(u,v)
in the multigraph
(X(1),X(2))
.

Identifying
V^{\prime}
with
V(Z)
by adding a negligible number of vertices.

The first step will be to modify
G^{\prime}
so that it has size equal to
|V(Z)|
. Recall
|V(Z)|=2|X(2)|
, so

N\cdot\log^{c_{0}+10}(N)\leqslant|X(2)|\leqslant N\cdot\log^{c_{1}}(N),

(12)

for some
c_{1}\geqslant c_{0}
. We can thus write
|V(Z)|=s(N)\cdot Q+R
for integers
Q
and
R
satisfying
\log^{10}(N)\leqslant Q\leqslant\log^{c_{1}-c_{0}}(N)
and
0\leqslant R<s(N)
. We set the repetition parameter
C=Q
, so that we have
|V^{\prime}|=Q\cdot s(N)
and add
R
vertices to the constraint graph of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. On these
R
additional vertices we add an arbitrary bipartite
d^{\prime}
-regular graph with equality constraints. Note that these
d^{\prime}R
extra equality constraints are at most
\frac{d^{\prime}R}{d^{\prime}\cdot s(N)\cdot Q}\leqslant\frac{1}{\log^{10}(N)}
fraction of all constraints, so they can essentially be ignored. The decoders for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
are unchanged after adding
R
additional vertices (we can define the decoders arbitrarily when one of these
R
additional vertices is given as input). We define the new decoding distributions as follows. Let
\xi:=R/|V^{\prime}|
-so that the
R
-additional vertices account for
\xi
-fraction of the vertices overall in
G^{\prime}
and divide the
R
additional vertices into equal parts of size
n
and label them
1
through
n
.
For each
t\in[n]
, the decoding distribution
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}
is modified as follows

•

With probability
1-\xi
choose
u
according to the old decoding distribution for
t
without the
R
extra vertices.

•

With probability
\xi
choose one of the extra
R
vertices in the part labeled by
t
.

One can check that choosing
t\in[n]
and then
u\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}
as above, we still get that
u
is uniform in
V^{\prime}
(with the
R
additional vertices). Since
\xi\leqslant\frac{1}{\log^{10}(N)}
, it is easy to see that after adding the
R
-additional vertices, the udPCP still has
\left(\frac{\eta}{100},\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{2}\left(\frac{\eta}{100}\right)^{5}\right)
-unique decoding soundness.
It is also straightforward to check that the resulting udPCP has length
|V(Z)|
and perfect completeness. Furthermore, alphabet size,
d^{\prime}
-regularity, and left-decoding degree are maintained. Henceforth, we slightly abuse notation and still refer to the modified constraint graph as
G^{\prime}=(A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime},E^{\prime})
and assume that it has the same number of vertices as
Z
for the remainder of the section.

Breaking
G^{\prime}
into matchings and setting the routing protocols.

Since
G^{\prime}
is a
d^{\prime}
-regular bipartite graph we can decompose
E^{\prime}
into
d^{\prime}
perfect matchings
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{d^{\prime}}
. Since
|V(Z)|=|V^{\prime}|
we can associate
V^{\prime}
with
V(Z)
in an arbitrary manner, and we fix such an identification henceforth. Abusing notation, we view each
\pi_{i}
as a function from
V(Z)\to V(Z)
satisfying that
\pi_{i}^{2}
is the identity. In particular, for
j\in V(Z)
corresponding to some
a\in A^{\prime}
,
\pi_{i}(a)
is the vertex in
V(Z)
corresponding to the vertex in
B^{\prime}
matched with
a
under
\pi_{i}
, and vice-versa if
j\in V(Z)
corresponds to some
b\in B^{\prime}
.

In order to describe the construction, it will be helpful to define
\mathcal{H}=\{(j,u)\;|\;j\in V(Z),u\in X_{j_{1}}(1)\}
and consider an initial function
A_{0}:\mathcal{H}\to\Sigma^{\prime}
where recall that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is the alphabet of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. One should think of
A_{0}
as coming from a satisfying assignment of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, so that
A_{0}(j,u)
is the
\Sigma^{\prime}
symbol assigned to the vertex in
V^{\prime}
corresponding to
j
under this satisfying assignment. Moreover, one should think of every
A_{0}(j,u)
as being “held” at the vertex
u\in X(1)
, so that
u\in X(1)
holds many
\Sigma^{\prime}
symbols (and in particular, one for each link it is in). That is, an assignment to the new udPCP is supposed to provide such values for each
u\in X(1)
.

For each
i\in[d^{\prime}]
let
\mathcal{R}_{i}
be the routing protocol from
Lemma

5.5
for the permutation
\pi_{i}
. The protocols
\mathcal{R}_{i}
all have round complexity
T
for some
T=O(\log N)
. Here, one should think of the routing protocols as being run with the initial maps
A_{0}
above. Then in words, for every
j\in V(Z)
, the routing protocol
\mathcal{R}_{i}
is meant to route an assignment of the
V^{\prime}
vertex corresponding to
j
from every vertex in the link
X_{j_{1}}
to every vertex in the link
X_{\pi_{i}(j_{1})}(1)
. For each
i\in[d^{\prime}],r\in[T]
, let
\inmap_{i,r}
and
\outmap_{i,r}
denote the in map and out map respectively during round
r
of protocol
\mathcal{R}_{i}
. Also, let
\mathcal{A}_{i,T}
denote the output function of protocol
\mathcal{R}_{i}
.

The description of
\mathcal{D}
.

We denote the new dPCP that we construct as

\mathcal{D}=\left(X(1),X(2),\Sigma,\{\Phi_{e}\}_{e\in X(2)},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right),

and define its parts as follows.

•

Constraint Graph.
The constraint graph is the base layer of
X
, i.e.
(X(1),X(2))
(with the multiplicities proportional to the weights of the stationary distribution of
X(2)
over
X(1)
).

•

Alphabets:
For each
u\in X(1)
, an alphabet symbol for
u
consists of the following.

–

Initial Messages:
one
\Sigma^{\prime}
symbol for each
j\in V(Z)
such that
u\in X_{j_{1}}(1)
. This symbol is intended to be the value
A_{0}(j,u)
for each
j\in V(Z)
which is supposed to come from a satisfying assignment to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
as described above.

–

Routing Transcript:
the maps
\inmap_{i,r}:2E(G)\to\Sigma
for each
i\in[d^{\prime}]
and
r\in[T]
. For each
i\in[d^{\prime}]
and
r\in[T]
, this is the in map of round
r
for the protocol
\mathcal{R}_{i}
. Recall that
\inmap_{i,r}(u,v)
should be thought of as the message received by
v
from
u
during round
r
in the routing protocol for the matching
\pi_{i}
.

In addition, we constrain the alphabet of
u
in the following ways.

–

Hardcoding
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
Constraints:
for each
i\in[d^{\prime}]
and
u\in V^{\prime}
, we only allow symbols
\sigma
for
u
such that the values in
\sigma
corresponding to
A_{0}(j,u)
and
A_{i,T}(\pi_{i}(j),u)
satisfy the constraint
\Phi^{\prime}_{(j,\pi_{i}(j))}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, for every
j
such that
u\in X_{j_{1}}(1)
.

–

Hardcoding Decoding Consistency:
for all
t\in[n]
and
u\in V^{\prime}
, we only allow symbols
\sigma
for
u
which satisfy the following. Denoting by
\sigma_{j}
the symbol corresponding to
A_{0}(j,u)
in
\sigma
,
we require that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{t}(j,\sigma_{j})
is the same for all
j
such that
u\in X_{j_{1}}(1)
and
j\in\supp(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\prime})
.

We denote the constrained alphabet for
u
as
\Sigma_{u}
.

•

Constraints:
For each
(u,v)\in X(2)
, the constraint between
u
and
v
checks the following.

–

For each
j\in V(Z)
such that
u,v\in X_{j_{1}}(1)
, check that

A_{0}(j,u)=A_{0}(j,v)\quad\text{and}\quad A_{i,T}(j,u)=A_{i,T}(j,v),\;\forall i\in[d^{\prime}].

–

For each
i\in[d^{\prime}]
and
r\in[T-1]
, check that

\inmap_{i,r+1}(u,v)=\outmap_{i,r}(v,u)\quad\text{and}\quad\inmap_{i,r+1}(v,u)=\outmap_{i,r}(v,u).

In words, the first item checks that the initial message parts of the symbols assigned to
u
and
v
are consistent. The second item checks that the routing transcripts of
u
and
v
are consistent, that is, for each protocol
\mathcal{R}_{i}
and every round
r\in[T-1]
, the constraint checks that the message that
v
receives from
u
(according to
v
’s alphabet symbol), is equal to the message that
u
sends to
v
(calculated based on the prior round in maps held at
u
’s alphabet symbol), and vice-versa.

•

Decoding Distributions:
For each
t\in[n]
, the distribution
\mathcal{P}_{t}
is as follows

–

Choose
j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}
and think of
j\in V(Z)
as a vertex in identifying
V^{\prime}
(by the identification we have between
V^{\prime}
and
V(Z)
).

–

Output
u\sim X_{j_{1}}(1)
.

•

Decoders:
For each
t\in[n]
,
u\in\supp(\mathcal{P}_{t})

and label
\sigma\in\Sigma_{u}
for
u
, the decoder outputs

\mathcal{D}_{t}(u,\sigma)=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{t}(j,\sigma_{j}),

where
j
is an arbitrary vertex in
V(Z)
such that
u\in X_{j_{1}}(1)
, and
\sigma_{j}
refers to the part of
\sigma
which contains a label for
A_{0}(j,u)
. Note that the specific identity of
j
above does not matter due to the way the alphabet of
\Sigma_{u}
is constrained (specifically, the second item) and hence the decoder is well defined.

5.3.2
Proof of Everything Except Unique-Decoding Soundness

We start by proving all of the properties except for unique decoding soundness, which is deferred to the next section.

Constraint Graph and Length.

It is clear that the constraint graph is
(X(1),X(2))
and hence the length is
|X(1)|=O_{|\Sigma_{0}|,\eta}(N\cdot{\sf poly}(\log(N)))
by construction of the complex
X
.

Degrees.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\sf dec}}
denote neighborhoods and decoding neighborhoods in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and let
\Gamma,\Gamma_{{\sf dec}}
be defined similarly for
\mathcal{D}
. The degree of each vertex in the constraint graph is
{\sf poly}_{\delta}(\log n)
by the properties of the HDX. In particular, it follows from the fact that each vertex is contained in
q^{O(d^{2})}
many
d
-faces by item 3 of
Theorem

2.9
. As for the decoding degree, we have that
t\in\Gamma_{{\sf dec}}(v)
if and only if there exists
j\in V(Z)
such that
v\in X_{j_{1}}(1)
and
t\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\sf dec}}(j)
. Since
|\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\sf dec}}(j)|=O(1)
for all
j\in V(Z)
, and the number of
j\in V(Z)
such that
v\in X_{j_{1}}(1)
is
{\sf poly}_{\delta}(\log n)
, the size of
v
’s decoding neighborhood is
{\sf poly}_{\delta}(\log n)
.

Alphabet Size.

An alphabet for each vertex consists of the initial messages which the vertex holds, and the messages it received throughout all of the rounds of the routing protocol. For the initial messages, each vertex
v
contains one symbol from a
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(1)
-sized alphabet for each
j\in V(Z)
such that
v\in X_{j_{1}}(1)
. The number of such
j
is
{\sf poly}_{\delta}(\log(N))
since
v
has
{\sf poly}_{\delta}(\log(N))
neighbors in
(X(1),X(2))
and for each
u
, there are a constant number of
j\in V(Z)
such that
j_{1}=u
. Thus, the initial message part of the alphabet consists of
{\sf poly}_{\delta}(\log(N))
symbols from a
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(1)
-sized alphabet. For the routing transcript part, the alphabet of a vertex
v
consists of one message per round of the protocol. By the work complexity of the protocol, the message contains at most
{\sf poly}_{\delta}(\log(N))
bits, and there are
T=O(\log N)
rounds. Altogether, this shows that each alphabet symbol for
v
can be written using
{\sf poly}_{\delta}(\log(N))
symbols from a
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(1)
-sized alphabet and hence the alphabet size is
2^{O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\delta}(\log(N)))}
as desired.

Decision Complexity.

Fix an edge
(u,v)\in X(2)
. The decision complexity of
(u,v)
is the sum of the following 2 parts:
first, circuit sizes for checking that the alphabet symbols for
u
and
v
are valid symbols (i.e. that they satisfy the hardcoded constraints), and second, checking that the constraint is satisfied. For the first part, let us describe the circuit complexity for checking validity of
u
’s symbol, as the circuit complexity for checking
v
’s symbol is the same. The circuit needs to check that for each of
O_{\eta}(1)
matchings
\pi
, the parts of the alphabet corresponding to
A_{0}(j,\pi(u))
and
A_{i,T}(j,u)
satisfy some constraint in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
for every
j
such that
u\in X_{j_{1}}(1)
. Since the constraint in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is over
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(1)
sized alphabet symbols, its circuit complexity is
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(1)
. Moreover, there are at most
{\sf poly}(\log(N))
many such
j
’s. Altogether, this results in circuit complexity
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\eta}(\log(N)))
. For the second part, the circuit needs to calculate the outgoing messages of
u
and
v
in each of the routing protocols
\mathcal{R}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{R}_{d^{\prime}}
, and then check an equality on the computed message. As the work complexity is
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}(\log(N)))
and there are
O_{\eta}(1)
routing protocols, this results in
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\eta}(\log(N)))
circuit complexity as well. Overall, the decision complexity is
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\eta}(\log(N)))
.

Decoding Distribution.

Choosing
t\in[n]
uniformly and
j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}
uniformly, we have that
j\in V(Z)
is distributed uniformly at random by the guarantee of starting udPCP
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
(specifically the fourth item of
Lemma

4.5
). Then the decoding distribution of
\mathcal{D}
chooses
u\sim X_{j_{1}}(1)
, and we have
u\sim X(1)
by
5.7
.

Decoding Complexity.

It is clear from the description of the decoder, that given a vertex
u
and a symbol
\sigma
for it, the decoder can restrict
\sigma
to a symbol from the alphabet of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and run the decoder of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
(which is another restriction of the symbol to a symbol from
\Sigma_{0}
). The circuit complexity for both of these tasks is on the order of logarithmic in the left alphabet size (which is also the length of the circuit’s input), and hence it is
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\eta,\delta}(\log(N)))
.

Completeness.

Fix
w\in\mathcal{L}
and let
A^{\prime}:V^{\prime}\to\Sigma^{\prime}
be the assignment to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
which satisfies all of the constraints and always decodes according to
w
. Now, define the initial messages according to
A^{\prime}
and run the routing protocols
\mathcal{R}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{R}_{d^{\prime}}
with these initial messages. Let
A
be the resulting assignment for
\mathcal{D}
. We will show that
A
is a valid assignment satisfying all of the constraints to
\mathcal{D}
and that it always decodes according to
w
.

For the first part, it is clear that all of the constraints of
\mathcal{D}
are satisfied, and it remains to check that
A
is a valid assignment, i.e. it satisfies the harded constraints of the alphabets. Indeed, for any
v
, any of the matchings
\pi_{i}
, and any
j
such that
v\in X_{j_{1}}(1)\cap X_{\pi_{i}(j)_{1}}(1)
, the alphabet symbols corresponding to
A_{0}(j,v),A_{i,T}(\pi(j),v)
are precisely
A^{\prime}(j)
and
A^{\prime}(\pi(j))
by
Lemma

5.6
, so they satisfy the constraint in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. For the decoding hardcoded constraints fix any vertex
v\in X(1)
and
j\in V(Z),t\in[n]
such that
v\in X_{j_{1}}(1)
and
t
is in the decoding neighborhood of
j
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. Then by the completeness of
\mathcal{D^{\prime}}
, we have that
D^{\prime}_{t}(j,A^{\prime}(j))=w_{t}
. Since the symbol corresponding to the initial value of
j
under assignment
A(v)
is precisely
A^{\prime}(j)
, this shows that the folding for decoding consistency is satisfied.

5.3.3
Proof of Unique-Decoding Soundness

We will show that
\mathcal{D}
satisfies
(\eta,\eta^{2}/6)
-unique decoding soundness for a fixed
\eta
sufficiently small relative to the constant
\varepsilon_{0}
from
Lemma

5.5
.
Suppose there is an assignment to
\mathcal{D}
that satisfies at least
(1-\varepsilon)
-weight of the constraints, for
\varepsilon:=\eta^{2}/6
. Let
A:X(1)\to\Sigma
be the assignment to
\mathcal{D}
achieving this value. From
A
, we can derive the function
A_{0}:\mathcal{H}\to\Sigma^{\prime}
. Specifically, for each
(j,v)\in\mathcal{H}
, we set
A_{0}(j,v)
to be the part of the symbol
A(v)
corresponding to an assignment for
j
from the alphabet of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
,
\Sigma^{\prime}
. Now, define the following assignment,
B\colon V^{\prime}\to\Sigma^{\prime}
:

B(j)={\sf plurality}_{u\sim X_{j_{1}}(1)}\{A_{0}(j,u)\},

where the function
{\sf plurality}
refers to the most common value
A_{0}(j,u)
when choosing
u\sim X_{j_{1}}(1)
. It is shown in
[
8
]
that this assignment
B
is a nearly satisfying assignment for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

[
8
, Lemma 5.3]

Suppose that
A
satisfies
(1-\varepsilon)
-weight of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}
. Then
B
defined as above satisfies at least
\left(1-O\left(\frac{1}{\log N}\right)\right)\geqslant\left(1-\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{2}\left(\frac{\eta}{100}\right)^{5}\right)
-fraction of the constraints of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The lemma is implicitly shown in the proof of the soundness claim in Lemma 5.3 of
[
8
]
. Specifically, they argue that if
A
is a
(1-\varepsilon)
-satisfying assignment to
\mathcal{D}
, then for each of the routing protocols
\mathcal{R}_{i}
the routing transcript of
\mathcal{R}_{i}
corresponding to the assignments of
A
corresponds to a routing on
(X(1),X(2))
with at most
\varepsilon
-fraction of adversarial edge corruptions.

Additionally, since
A
is
(1-\varepsilon)
-satisfying, one can show that for
\left(1-\frac{1}{\log N}\right)
-fraction of
j\in V(Z)
, we have that the probability a random vertex in the link
X_{j_{1}}(1)
holds a value for
j
that is consistent with
B
, i.e. the probability
\Pr_{u\in X_{j_{1}}(1)}[A_{0}(j,u)=B(j)]
, is very close to
1
(in fact this statement is required for our proof of unique-decoding soundness too, so the argument is reproduced, essentially in
Lemma

5.9
, below). As a result, the initial messages
A_{0}
satisfy the condition in
Lemma

5.5
and applying
Lemma

5.5
, we get that for
\left(1-\frac{1}{\log N}\right)
-fraction of
j\in V(Z)
, the values
B(j)
and
B(\pi_{i}(j))
are consistent with those held at some vertex
u\in X_{j_{1}}(1)
. By the hardcoding of the alphabet of
u
, this shows that
B
satisfies the constraint on
(j,\pi_{i}(j))
, and applying this over all routing protocols shows that
B
satisfies almost all of the constraints in the original udPCP.
∎

Because
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
satisfies
\left(\frac{\eta}{100},\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{\eta}{100}\right)^{5}\right)
-unique decoding soundness with respect to
\mathcal{L}
for some constant
\rho
, there exists
w\in\mathcal{L}
such that

\Pr_{t\in[n],j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}}[D^{\prime}_{t}(j,B(j))\neq w_{t}]\leqslant\frac{\eta}{100}.

(13)

We can bound the decoding error of the assignment
A
for the udPCP
\mathcal{D}
as follows

\displaystyle\Pr_{t\in[n],u\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}}[D_{t}\left(u,A(u)\right)\neq w_{t}]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{t\in[n],j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t},u\sim X_{j_{1}}(1)}[D^{\prime}_{t}(j,A_{0}(j,u))\neq w_{t}]

(14)

\displaystyle\leqslant\Pr_{t\in[n],j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}}[D^{\prime}_{t}(j,B(j))\neq w_{t}]

\displaystyle+\Pr_{t\in[n],j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t},u\sim X_{j_{1}}(1)}[A_{0}(j,u)\neq B(j)]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\eta}{100}+\Pr_{t\in[n],j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t},u\sim X_{j_{1}}(1)}[A_{0}(j,u)\neq B(j)].

In the first transition we used the fact that
D_{t}\left(u,A(u)\right)=D^{\prime}_{t}(j,A_{0}(j,u))
, in the second transition we used the union bound, and in the third transition we used (
13
). Note that for any fixed
j
, we have

\Pr_{u\sim X_{j_{1}}(1)}[A_{0}(j,u)\neq B(j)]\leqslant\Pr_{u,v\sim X_{j_{1}}(1)}[A_{0}(j,u)\neq A_{0}(j,v)].

This is because
B(j)
is defined to be the most popular value of
A_{0}(j,u)
when sampling
u\sim X_{j_{1}}(1)
. Plugging this into (
14
) gives

\begin{split}\Pr_{t\in[n],u\in\mathcal{P}_{t}}[D_{t}\left(u,A(u)\right)\neq w(t)]&\leqslant\frac{\eta}{100}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{t\in[n],j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}}\left[\Pr_{u,v\in X_{j_{1}}(1)}[A_{0}(j,u)\neq A_{0}(j,v)]\right].\end{split}

(15)

We say
j\in V(Z)
is bad if

\Pr_{u,v\in X_{j_{1}}(1)}[A_{0}(j,u)\neq A_{0}(j,v)]\geqslant 2\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

(16)

The expectation from (
15
) can be bounded by

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{t\in[n],j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}}\left[\Pr_{u,v\in X_{j_{1}}(1)}[A_{0}(j,u)\neq A_{0}(j,v)]\right]\leqslant 2\sqrt{\varepsilon}+\Pr_{t\in[n],j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}}[\text{$j$ is bad}].

(17)

To bound the probability that
j
is bad, we will show that if
j
is bad, then the link
X_{j_{1}}(1)
must contain many violated constraints. To this end, let
\mathcal{E}\subseteq X(2)
denote the set of constraints that are not satisfied by the assignment
A
. By assumption,
A
satisfies at least
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction of constraints, so
\mu_{2}(\mathcal{E})\leqslant\varepsilon
. On the other hand, if
A_{0}(j,u)\neq A_{0}(j,v)
for any
j\in V(Z)
, and
(u,v)\in X_{j_{1}}(2)
, then the constraint on
(u,v)
is violated and
(u,v)\in\mathcal{E}
. We can thus show the following.

Lemma 5.9
.

If
j
is bad then
\mu_{j_{1},2}(\mathcal{E})\geqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, and consequently
\Pr_{t\in[n],j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}}[j\text{ is bad}]\leqslant\frac{{\sf poly}(d)}{q}
.

Proof.

Fix
j
that is bad and for each symbol for
j
in the original udPCP
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
,
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{j}
, let

U_{\sigma}=\{u\in X_{j_{1}}(1)\;|\;A_{0}(j,u)=\sigma\}.

Then

\mu_{j_{1},2}(\mathcal{E})\geqslant\Pr_{(u,v)\sim X_{j_{1}}(2)}[A_{0}(j,u)\neq A_{0}(j,v)]=1-\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{j}}\Pr_{(u,v)\sim X_{j_{1}}(2)}[u\in U_{\sigma}\land v\in U_{\sigma}].

(18)

Now, we use the fact that the graph
(X_{j_{1}}(1),X_{j_{1}}(2))
has second eigenvalue at most
O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\right)
by item 4 of
Theorem

2.9
, so we can bound

\begin{split}\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{j}}\Pr_{(u,v)\sim X_{j_{1}}(2)}[u\in U_{\sigma}\land v\in U_{\sigma}]&\leqslant\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{j}}\mu_{j_{1}}(U_{\sigma})^{2}+O(q^{-1/2})\mu_{j_{1}}(U_{\sigma})\\
&\leqslant O(q^{-1/2})+\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{j}}\mu_{j_{1}}(U_{\sigma})^{2}.\end{split}

(19)

Combining (
18
), (
19
), and the observation that
1-\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{j}}\mu_{j_{1}}(U_{\sigma})^{2}=\Pr_{u,v\sim X_{j_{1}}(1)}[A_{0}(j,u)\neq A_{0}(j,v)]
we get that

\mu_{j_{1},2}(\mathcal{E})\geqslant 1-\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\prime}_{j}}\mu_{j_{1}}(U_{\sigma})^{2}-O(q^{-1/2})=\Pr_{u,v\sim X_{j_{1}}(1)}[A_{0}(j,u)\neq A_{0}(j,v)]-O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\right)\geqslant 2\sqrt{\varepsilon}-O(q^{-1/2})\geqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon},

where the penultimate transition uses the assumption that
j
is bad from (
16
). This shows the first part of the lemma.

To obtain the “consequently” part of the lemma, recall that by
5.7
, when choosing
j\in V^{\prime}=A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}
uniformly at random and then outputting
j_{1}
, the resulting
j_{1}\in X(1)
is distributed according to the stationary distribution over
X(1)
. On the other hand, choosing
t\in[n]
and
j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}
, we have
j
uniformly distributed in
V^{\prime}
. Thus,

\Pr_{t\in[n],j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}}[j\text{ is bad}]=\Pr_{t\in[n],j\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}}[\mu_{j_{1}}(\mathcal{E})\geqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon}]=\Pr_{j\in V^{\prime}}[\mu_{j_{1}}(\mathcal{E})\geqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon}]\leqslant\frac{{\sf poly}(d)}{q}.

In the second transition we used the above observation on the distribution of
j_{1}
.
In the third transition we used
Lemma

2.10
,
\mu_{2}(\mathcal{E})\leqslant\varepsilon
,
and the fact that a uniformly random
j\in V^{\prime}
is on the side
A^{\prime}
with probability
1/2
.
∎

We can now conclude the proof of unique decoding soundness.

Proof of Unique Decoding Soundness.

Fix
\eta>0
sufficiently small, and suppose there is an assignment,
A
, to
\mathcal{D}
that satisfies at least
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction of the constraints, for
\varepsilon=\eta^{2}/6
. Then, combining (
15
), (
17
), and
Lemma

5.9
, we get that there is
w\in\mathcal{L}
such that the decoding error of
A
with respect to
w
is at most

\Pr_{t\in[n],u\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}}[D_{t}(u,A(u))\neq w_{t}]\leqslant\frac{\eta}{100}+2\sqrt{\varepsilon}+\frac{{\sf poly}(d)}{q}\leqslant\eta.\qed

6
Gap Amplification

Starting from the udPCP construction from
Theorem

5.1
, we now obtain a dPCP with small list-decoding soundness error by using a direct product based gap amplification.

Theorem 6.1
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
and a circuit
\varphi:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
of size
N
, the language
{\sf SAT}(\varphi)
has a dPCP satisfying the following:

•

Length.

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}\left(N\cdot{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)\right)
.

•

Left Alphabet.
For each left vertex
a
, the left alphabet of
a
is contained in
\Sigma^{k}
for some
\Sigma\supseteq\Sigma_{0}
of size
|\Sigma_{2}|=2^{O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))}
and
k=O_{\varepsilon}(1)
. Hence the left alphabet size is
2^{O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}\left({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)\right)}
.

•

Right Alphabet.
The right alphabet is
\Sigma^{\sqrt{k}}
where
\Sigma
and
k
are the same as in the left alphabet condition.

•

Degrees.
The left degree is
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
, the decoding degree is
{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)
, and the right degree is
{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)
.

•

Decision Complexity.

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
.

•

Decoding Complexity.

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
.

•

Decoding Distribution.
The complete decoding distribution is agnostic. Furthermore, denoting the complete decoding distribution by
\mathcal{Q}
, for every
\textbf{b}\in X(\sqrt{k})
we have that

\mathcal{Q}(\circ,\circ,\textbf{b})\leqslant\frac{{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)}{N}.

•

Completeness.
The dPCP has perfect completeness.

•

Soundness.
The dPCP has
(L,\varepsilon)
-list-decoding soundness for
L:=\varepsilon^{-O(1)}
.

We now dsecribe the construction for
Theorem

6.1
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
and a size
N
circuit
\varphi:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
and set
\mathcal{L}={\sf SAT}{(\varphi)}
. Set
\delta:=\delta(\varepsilon)>0
sufficiently small relative to
\varepsilon
and take
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
to be the udPCP for
{\sf SAT}{(\varphi)}
obtained through
Theorem

5.1
with the direct product soundness parameter there set to be
\delta/100
and
\eta>0
there to be a small absolute constant so that the result holds. After setting
\delta
, the other parameters are all chosen sufficiently large as described in
Theorem

5.1
, and in particular, we have that the parameters of
X
are as follows. The direct product parameter
k=O_{\delta}(1)
is sufficiently large, the dimension parameter is
d=O_{\delta}(1)
is sufficiently large, the number of vertices is
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|,\delta}(N\cdot{\sf poly}(\log(N)))
, the prime is
q={\sf poly}_{\delta}(\log N)
. Let us denote the parts of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
by

\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}=\left(X(1),X(2),\Sigma,\{\Phi^{{\sf hdx}}_{e}\}_{e\in X(2)},\{\mathcal{P}^{{\sf hdx}}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{D^{{\sf hdx}}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right).

The amplified dPCP takes the form

\mathcal{D}_{{\sf dp}}=\left(X(k)\cup X(\sqrt{k}),E,\Sigma^{k},\Sigma^{\sqrt{k}},\{\Phi^{{\sf dp}}_{e}\}_{e\in E},\{\mathcal{P}^{{\sf dp}}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{D^{{\sf dp}}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right).

Throughout the proof we will use boldface letters to denote vertices of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf dp}}
. The components of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf dp}}
are as follows:

•

Constraint Graph.
The bipartite inclusion graph with parts
X(k)
and
X(\sqrt{k})
. Throughout the section we will use
a
to denote a generic vertex in
X(k)
and
b
to denote a generic vertex in
X(\sqrt{k})
. We remark that
a
and
b
should also be thought of, respectively, as size
k
and
\sqrt{k}
subsets of
X(1)
. For
\textbf{a}\in X(k),\textbf{b}\in X(\sqrt{k})
we include an edge between them in
E
if
\textbf{a}\supseteq\textbf{b}
.

•

Left Alphabet:
For each
\textbf{a}\in X(k)
, its alphabet is a subset
\Sigma^{k}
and each symbol
\sigma
should be thought of as a map from assigning a
\Sigma
symbol to each
v\in\textbf{a}
. We constrain the left alphabet of each
\textbf{a}\in X(k)
to only contain maps
\sigma:\textbf{a}\to\Sigma
that satisfy the following properties:

–

Hardcoding
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
constraints.
For any
(u,v)\in X(2)
such that both vertices are contained in
a
,
\sigma(u)
and
\sigma(v)
satisfy the constraint on
(u,v)
from
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
.

–

Hardcoding for decoding consistency.
For any
t\in[n]
, and any two
u,v\in\textbf{a}
such that
t
is in the decoding neighborhood of both
u
and
v
in
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
, we have

D^{{\sf hdx}}_{t}(u,\sigma(u))=D^{{\sf hdx}}_{t}(v,\sigma(v)).

•

Right Alphabet.
For each
\textbf{b}\in X(\sqrt{k})
, its alphabet is
\Sigma^{\sqrt{k}}
, and we similarly think of each symbol as a map from
R\to\Sigma
assigning a
\Sigma
symbol to each
v\in\textbf{b}
.

•

Constraints.
For each edge of the constraint graph,
e=(\textbf{a},\textbf{b})
, the constraint
\Phi_{e}
checks that the assignments to
a
and
b
agree. That is, if the left and right vertices are assigned symbols
\sigma\in\Sigma^{k},\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\sqrt{k}}
respectively,

\Phi_{e}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=1\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\sigma|_{\textbf{b}}=\sigma^{\prime},

where
\sigma|_{\textbf{b}}\in\Sigma^{\sqrt{k}}
is the restriction of
\sigma
to the vertices in
b
.

•

Decoding Distributions:
Let
\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf hdx}}
be the complete decoding distribution (over
[n]\times X(1)
) of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
wherein one chooses
t\in[n]
uniformly,
u\sim\mathcal{P}_{{\sf hdx}}(t)
, and outputs
(t,u)
. The total decoding distribution of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf dp}}
, denoted by
\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}
, is a distribution over triples
(t,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\in[n]\times X(k)\times X(\sqrt{k})
is as follows:

–

Choose
\textbf{a}\sim X(k)
, and then
\textbf{b}\subseteq\textbf{a}
uniformly.

–

Choose
u\in\textbf{b}
uniformly and choose
t\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf hdx}}(\cdot,u)
.

Then the decoding distribution
\mathcal{P}^{{\sf dp}}_{t}
is defined as
\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
.

•

Decoders:
For
(t,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\in\supp(\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}})
and left alphabet symbol
\sigma\in\Sigma^{k}
for
a
, the decoder outputs

D^{{\sf dp}}_{t}(\textbf{a},\sigma_{\textbf{a}})=D^{{\sf hdx}}_{t}(u,\sigma_{\textbf{a}}(u)).

where
u\in X(1)
is an arbitrary vertex contained in both
a
and
\supp(\mathcal{P}^{{\sf hdx}}_{t})
. Due to the hardcoding for decoding consistency, the above value is the same for any
u
and hence the decoder is well defined.

The above completes the description of the new dPCP, and the remainder of the section is dedicated to its analysis.

6.1
Proof of
Theorem

6.1
: Everything Except the Soundness

In this section we analyze the length, alphabet size, decoding degree, decision complexity and the completeness of the construction.

Length.

The length is
|X(k)|+|X(\sqrt{k})|=O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(N\cdot{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
by the properties of
X
from
Theorem

2.9
.

Alphabet Size.

The left and right alphabet sizes are at most
|\Sigma|^{k}
and
|\Sigma|^{\sqrt{k}}
respectively. Since
k=O_{\varepsilon}(1)
and
|\Sigma|=2^{O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))}
, both the left and right alphabet sizes are
2^{O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))}
.

Degrees.

For
\textbf{a}\in X(k)
, its degree is
\binom{k}{\sqrt{k}}=O_{\varepsilon}(1)
and its decoding degree is at most the sum of the decoding degrees, in
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
, of the vertices
u\in\textbf{a}
. Hence, the decoding degree is at most
k{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)\leqslant{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)
. For
\textbf{b}\in X(\sqrt{k})
, its degree is at most
d(\textbf{b})\cdot d^{k}
, where
d(\textbf{b})
is the number of
d
-faces of the complex
X
containing
b
. Since
d(\textbf{b})\leqslant{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)
by
Theorem

2.9
, the right degree is
{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)
.

Projection Decision Complexity.

Given a left alphabet symbol, it is clear that the unique right alphabet symbol which could satisfy the constraint is simply a restriction, so it can be computed with a circuit of size
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
. Additionally, the projection circuit needs to checking that the left alphabet symbol
\sigma\in\Sigma^{k}
satisfies (1) the hardcoding of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
constraints, and (2) checking that the left alphabet symbol
\sigma\in\Sigma^{k}
satisfies hardcoding for decoding consistency.

The circuit complexity of (1) is
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
as there are
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
edges
e\in X(2)
contained in
a
and each
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
constraint has circuit complexity
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
. The circuit complexity of (2) is also
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
. This is because, by the decoding degree of
\mathcal{D}
, there are
{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)
pairs of
u\in\textbf{a}
and
t\in[n]
such that
t
is in the decoding neighborhood of
u
in
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
and the decoders
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx},t}
all have circuit complexity
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
by the decoding complexity of
Theorem

5.1
. Finally, computing the restriction and checking that is is equal to a given string amounts to equality between two strings of
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
bits, which can be done by a circuit of size
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
. Altogether, this shows that the projection decision complexity is
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
.

Decoding Distribution.

To see that
\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}
is agnostic, note that for any
(t,\textbf{b})\in\supp(\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}(\cdot,\circ,\cdot))
, both of the distributions

\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}(\circ,\cdot,\textbf{b})
and

\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}(t,\cdot,\textbf{b})
are of
\textbf{a}\sim X(k)

conditioned on
\textbf{a}\supseteq\textbf{b}
, so they are the same.

For the second property, we describe the following way of choosing
(\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\sim\mathcal{Q}(\circ,\cdot,\cdot)
:

•

Choose
U\in X(d)
uniformly at random,

•

Choose
\textbf{a}\subseteq U
of size
k
uniformly and
\textbf{b}\subseteq\textbf{a}
of size
\sqrt{k}
uniformly,

•

Output
