(\textbf{a},\textbf{b})
.

It is clear by the above that for every

\textbf{b}\in X(\sqrt{k})
, we have

\mathcal{Q}(\circ,\circ,\textbf{b})=\sum\limits_{\textbf{a}\supseteq\textbf{b}}\mathcal{Q}(\circ,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{a}\supseteq\textbf{b}\\
U\in X(d),U\supseteq\textbf{a}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|X(d)|}\cdot\frac{1}{\binom{d}{{k}}\binom{k}{\sqrt{k}}}\leqslant\frac{{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)}{N}.

(20)

In the last transition, we use the fact that for a fixed
b
,
the number of
d
-faces
U
and
k
-faces
a
that contain
b
is at most
{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)^{d}\leqslant{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)
(as the degree of
X
is
{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)
), and also that
|X(d)|\geqslant N
.

Decoding Complexity.

It is clear that given a left vertex
a
and a symbol for it,
\sigma
, the decoder runs the decoder of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
on a restriction of
\sigma
, and hence the decoding complexity is
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
by
Theorem

5.1
.

Perfect Completeness.

Fix
w\in\mathcal{L}
. By the perfect completeness of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
, there is an assignment
A^{\prime}:X(1)\to\Sigma
which satisfies all of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
and decodes to
w_{t}
with probability
1
for all
t\in[n]
. Now consider the assignments
T_{1}:X(k)\to\Sigma^{k}
and
T_{2}:X(\sqrt{k})\to\Sigma^{\sqrt{k}}
such that
T_{1}[\textbf{a}]=A^{\prime}|_{\textbf{a}}
for each
\textbf{a}\in X(k)
and
T_{2}[\textbf{b}]=A^{\prime}|_{\textbf{b}}
for each
\textbf{b}\in X(\sqrt{k})
. It is clear that the alphabet hardcoding is satisfied by
T_{1}[\textbf{a}]
for every
\textbf{a}\in X(k)
. Indeed, for any
e\in X(2)
contained in
a
, the restriction of
T_{1}[\textbf{a}]
to the vertices in
e
is consistent with
A^{\prime}
and hence the constraint
e
is satisfied by the corresponding values of
T_{1}[\textbf{a}]
. As for the hardcoding for decoding consistency, for any
u\in\textbf{a}
and
t\in[n]
which is a decoding neighbor of
u
in
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
, we have that
D^{\prime}_{t}(u,T_{1}[\textbf{a}](u))=w_{t}
. This latter fact also shows that
T_{1},T_{2}
decode to
w_{t}
with probability
1
for all
t\in[n]
. Finally, it is straightforward to see that all of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf dp}}
are satisfied.

6.2
Proof of
Theorem

6.1
: Tools for List-Decoding Soundness

The proof of list-decoding soundness involves two steps. First, we show a list-decoding variant of the soundness guarantee of the direct product test, informally saying that there is always a small list of functions
f_{i}:X(1)\to\Sigma
which explains almost all of the passing probability of the direct product test in
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf dp}}
. Additionally, each
f_{i}
, when viewed as an assignment to
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
, is nearly a satisfying assignment of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
.
Second, we translate the list-decoding guarantee above into a list-decoding guarantee relative to valid members of the language
\mathcal{L}
. To accomplish this, we combine the unique decoding soundness of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
with fact that the
f_{i}
’s are nearly satisfying assignments of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
.

The first step outlined above is achieved via the following lemma.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
T_{1}:X(k)\to\Sigma^{k}
be a left assignment to
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf dp}}
and let
\delta
be defined as in the construction of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf dp}}
. Then, there exists an absolute constant
C
such that, there is a list of at most
L\leqslant\delta^{-C}
functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{L}:X(1)\to\Sigma
, along with disjoint sets
\mathcal{U}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{L}\subseteq X(k)
which together satisfy the following:

•

for each
i\in[L]
, we have
\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\geqslant\delta^{C}
and
{\sf dist}(f_{i}|_{\textbf{a}},T_{1}[\textbf{a}])\leqslant\delta
for all
\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
;

•

for any right assignment
T_{2}:X(\sqrt{k})\to\Sigma^{\sqrt{k}}
, we have

\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k),\textbf{b}\subseteq\textbf{a}}[T_{1}[\textbf{a}]|_{\textbf{b}}=T_{2}[\textbf{b}]\land\textbf{a}\notin\cup_{i=1}^{L}\mathcal{U}_{i}]\leqslant\frac{\sqrt{\delta}}{10}.

Towards the proof of
Lemma

6.2
, we first show an auxiliary lemma which says that there exists an assignment
X(k)\to\Sigma^{k}
which has only trivial agreement with all possible functions
F:X(1)\to\Sigma
.

Lemma 6.3
.

There exists a table
T^{*}:X(k)\to\Sigma^{k}
such that for any function
F:X(1)\to\Sigma
,

\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k)}\left[{\sf dist}(F|_{\textbf{a}},T^{*}[\textbf{a}])\leqslant\frac{1}{3}\right]\leqslant\frac{10}{\sqrt{d}},

where recall
d
is the dimension of the complex
X
.

Proof.

We use the fact that the direct-product dimension parameter,
k
, in constructing
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf dp}}
is large and in particular
k\geqslant 8
. First let us show that there exists a table
T^{*}
satisfying such that every pair for every pair
\textbf{a},\textbf{a}^{\prime}\in X(k)
with nonempty intersection, we have that
T^{*}[\textbf{a}]
and
T^{*}[\textbf{a}^{\prime}]
disagree at every
x\in\textbf{a}\cap\textbf{a}^{\prime}
. Indeed,
|\Sigma|
is super-polynomial in
n
whereas
k\cdot|X(k)|\leqslant n^{O_{\varepsilon}(1)}
, and hence we can arrange that the
T^{*}[\textbf{a}]:\textbf{a}\to\Sigma
have disjoint image over all
\textbf{a}\in X(k)
.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that this is not the case, and that there exists
F:X(1)\to\Sigma
, such that

\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k)}[{\sf dist}(F|_{\textbf{a}},T^{*}[\textbf{a}])\leqslant\frac{1}{3}]>\frac{10}{\sqrt{d}}

(21)

Now consider the probability that, when choosing
U\sim X(d)
,
\textbf{b}\subseteq U
of size
k-1
, and finally
\textbf{a},\textbf{a}^{\prime}
of size
k
such that
\textbf{b}\subseteq\textbf{a},\textbf{a}^{\prime}\subseteq U
, we have both
{\sf dist}(F|_{\textbf{a}},T^{*}[\textbf{a}])\leqslant\frac{1}{3}
and
{\sf dist}(F|_{\textbf{a}^{\prime}},T^{*}[\textbf{a}^{\prime}])\leqslant\frac{1}{3}
. For simplicity, let us refer to these two events as
{\sf E}(\textbf{a})
and
{\sf E}(\textbf{a}^{\prime})
respectively. Then,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim X(d),\textbf{b}\subseteq U}\left[\Pr_{\textbf{b}\subseteq\textbf{a},\textbf{a}^{\prime}\subseteq U}[{\sf E}(\textbf{a})\land{\sf E}(\textbf{a}^{\prime})]\right]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim X(d),\textbf{b}\subseteq U}\left[\Pr_{\textbf{a}}[{\sf E}(\textbf{a})~|~U,\textbf{b}]^{2}\right]\geqslant\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim X(d),\textbf{b}\subseteq U}\left[\Pr_{\textbf{a}}[{\sf E}(\textbf{a})~|~U,\textbf{b}]\right]^{2}>\frac{100}{d}.

In the first transition we used the fact that
\textbf{a},\textbf{a}^{\prime}
are independent, in the second transition we used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and in the third transition we used (
21
). Now using the fact that the probability of choosing
\textbf{a}=\textbf{a}^{\prime}
is at most
\frac{1}{d-k+1}\leqslant\frac{100}{d}
, we get there exist
a
and
\textbf{a}^{\prime}
distinct such that both
{\sf E}(\textbf{a})
and
{\sf E}(\textbf{a}^{\prime})
occur. Then

T^{*}[\textbf{a}]
and

T^{*}[\textbf{a}^{\prime}]
disagree on at most

\frac{2}{3}k+1<k
coordinates, which is a contradiction.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.2
.

Initialize
\mathcal{U}_{1}=\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\star}=\emptyset
and
i:=1
. Also set
T^{(1)}:=T_{1}
. While there exists
F:X(1)\to\Sigma
such that

\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k)}[{\sf dist}(F|_{\textbf{a}},T^{(i)}[\textbf{a}])\leqslant\delta\land\textbf{a}\notin\mathcal{U}^{\star}_{i}]\geqslant\delta^{C}

pick such
F
, set
f_{i}=F
, set
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\{\textbf{a}\in X(k)\;|\;{\sf dist}(F|_{\textbf{a}},T^{(i)}[\textbf{a}])\leqslant\delta\land\textbf{a}\notin\mathcal{U}^{\star}_{i}\}
, and update
\mathcal{U}^{\star}_{i+1}=\mathcal{U}^{\star}_{i}\cup\mathcal{U}_{i}
. Then increment
i
by
1
, and repeat. In the above we fix
C
to be some absolute constant which we can set arbitrarily large.

Note that this process must terminate in
J\leqslant\delta^{-C}
steps. Indeed, after each increment of
i
, the measure
\mu_{k}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\star}_{i}\right)
increases by at least
\delta^{C}
. At the end of the process, let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{J}
and
\mathcal{U}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{J}
be the functions and sets obtained respectively, and let
\mathcal{U}^{\star}:=\bigcup_{i\in[J]}\mathcal{U}_{i}
. The following conditions hold:

•

For each
i\in[J]
and
\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
we have
{\sf dist}(f_{i}|_{\textbf{a}},T_{1}[\textbf{a}])\leqslant\delta
.

•

\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\geqslant\delta^{C}
,

•

The sets
\mathcal{U}_{i}
are disjoint.

•

For any
\textbf{a}\notin\mathcal{U}^{\star}
, and any
i\in[J]
, we have
{\sf dist}(f_{i}|_{\textbf{a}},T[\textbf{a}])>\delta
.

•

For any
F:X(1)\to\Sigma
, we have

\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k)}[{\sf dist}(T[\textbf{a}],F|_{\textbf{a}})\leqslant\delta\land\textbf{a}\notin\mathcal{U}^{\star}]\leqslant\delta^{C}.

(22)

We will now show that
f_{1},\ldots,f_{J}
and
\mathcal{U}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{J}
satisfy
Lemma

6.2
. First, note that the number of functions is indeed
J\leqslant\delta^{-C}
for some absolute constant
C
and the sets
\mathcal{U}_{i}
are indeed disjoint as required by
Lemma

6.2
. It is also clear that the first item of
Lemma

6.2
holds by construction of the
\mathcal{U}_{i}
’s.

It remains to verify the second item. Let
T^{*}
be the table from
Lemma

6.3
— which, recall, does not have significant agreement with any function over
X(1)
— and define the table
T^{\prime}
as follows. For
\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}^{\star}
, set
T^{\prime}[\textbf{a}]=T^{*}[\textbf{a}]
and for
\textbf{a}\notin\mathcal{U}^{\star}
, set
T^{\prime}[\textbf{a}]=T[\textbf{a}]
. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that the second item fails, so that

\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k),\textbf{b}\subseteq\textbf{a}}[T^{\prime}[\textbf{a}]|_{\textbf{b}}=T_{2}[\textbf{b}]]>\frac{\sqrt{\delta}}{10}.

(23)

Choosing
U\in X(d)
,
\textbf{b}\subseteq U
of size
\sqrt{k}
, and
\textbf{a},\textbf{a}^{\prime}
of size
k
such that
\textbf{b}\subseteq\textbf{a},\textbf{a}^{\prime}\subseteq U
according to the direct product testing distribution, we have

\begin{split}\Pr_{U,\textbf{a},\textbf{a}^{\prime},\textbf{b}}[T^{\prime}[\textbf{a}]|_{\textbf{b}}=T^{\prime}[\textbf{a}^{\prime}]|_{\textbf{b}}]&\geqslant\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\left[\Pr_{\textbf{a},\textbf{a}^{\prime},\textbf{b}}[T^{\prime}[\textbf{a}]|_{\textbf{b}}=T_{2}[\textbf{b}]\land T^{\prime}[\textbf{a}^{\prime}]|_{\textbf{b}}=T_{2}[\textbf{b}]]\right]\\
&=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\left[\Pr_{\textbf{a},\textbf{b}}[T^{\prime}[\textbf{a}]|_{\textbf{b}}=T_{2}[\textbf{b}]]^{2}\right]\\
&\geqslant\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\left[\Pr_{\textbf{a},\textbf{b}}[T^{\prime}[\textbf{a}]|_{\textbf{b}}=T_{2}[\textbf{b}]]\right]\right)^{2}\\
&\geqslant\frac{\delta}{100}.\end{split}

(24)

In the third transition we used by Cauchy-Schwarz, and in the last transition we used (
23
).
Since the complex
X
supports a direct product test with soundness
\delta/100
, we get that there exists
F:X(1)\to\Sigma
such that

\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k)}\left[{\sf dist}(T^{\prime}[\textbf{a}],F|_{\textbf{a}})\leqslant\frac{\delta}{100}\right]\geqslant\delta^{C_{1}},

for some absolute constant
C_{1}
coming from the soundness of the direct product test as described in
Theorem

2.9
. However, this is a contradiction because

\displaystyle\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k)}\left[{\sf dist}(T^{\prime}[\textbf{a}],F|_{\textbf{a}})\leqslant\frac{\delta}{100}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k)}\left[{\sf dist}(T^{\prime}[\textbf{a}],F|_{\textbf{a}})\leqslant\frac{\delta}{100}\land\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}^{\star}\right]

\displaystyle+\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k)}\left[{\sf dist}(T^{\prime}[\textbf{a}],F|_{\textbf{a}})\leqslant\frac{\delta}{100}\land\textbf{a}\notin\mathcal{U}^{\star}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k)}\left[{\sf dist}(T^{*}[\textbf{a}],F|_{\textbf{a}})\leqslant\frac{\delta}{100}\right]+\delta^{C}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{10}{\sqrt{d}}+\delta^{C}

\displaystyle<\delta^{C_{1}}.

where in the second transition we used (
22
) along with the construction of
T^{\prime}
and in the third transition we used
\delta/100\leqslant 1/3
and
Lemma

6.3
. To obtain the contradiction in the last transition, we use the fact that the dimension parameter of the HDX
X
is sufficiently large relative to
1/\delta
and the fact that the absolute constant
C
fixed at the start of the proof can be chosen sufficiently large relative to the constant
C_{1}
coming from the direct product test soundness.
∎

6.3
Proof of
Theorem

6.1
: Proof of List-Decoding Soundness

We now accomplish the second step in the proof of the list-decoding soundness.
Fix a left assignment,
T_{1}:X(k)\to\Sigma^{k}
, to
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf dp}}
. Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{L}
and
\mathcal{U}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{L}
be the functions and subsets of
X(k)
obtained by applying
Lemma

6.2
with respect to
T_{1}
, and let
T_{2}:X(\sqrt{k})\to\Sigma^{\sqrt{k}}
be any right assignment to
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf dp}}
. We start by observing that each
f_{i}:X(1)\to\Sigma
is a nearly-satisfying assignment to
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
.

Lemma 6.4
.

For each
i\in[L]
, we have

\Pr_{(u,v)\sim X(2)}[\Phi^{{\sf hdx}}_{(u,v)}(f_{i}(u),f_{i}(v))=1]\geqslant 1-3\delta.

Proof.

Fix
i
and let
\mathcal{B}\subseteq X(2)
be the set of constraints not satisfied by
f_{i}
, so that
\mu_{2}(\mathcal{B})
is precisely the probability that a randomly chosen constraint, according to the measure of
X
over
X(2)
, is not satisfied by
f_{i}
. To prove the lemma, we show
\mu_{2}(\mathcal{B})\leqslant 3\delta
.

To start, notice that if there exists
\textbf{a}\in X(k)
such that
T_{1}[\textbf{a}](u)=f_{i}(u)
and
T_{1}[\textbf{a}](v)=f_{i}(v)
, then we automatically have
\Phi^{{\sf hdx}}_{(u,v)}(f_{i}(u),f_{i}(v))=1
due to how the alphabet of
a
is hardcoded. This shows that a constraint is highly likely to be satisfied if it is chosen by first choosing
\textbf{a}\sim\mathcal{U}_{i}
, and then choosing
u,v\in\textbf{a}
. In the sequence below, we write
\textbf{a}\sim\mathcal{U}_{i}
to denote
a
chosen according to
\sim X(k)
, conditioned on being in
\mathcal{U}_{i}
. After
a
is chosen,
u
and
v
are chosen as uniformly random vertices inside
a
.

\begin{split}\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim\mathcal{U}_{i},u,v\in\textbf{a},u\neq v}[(u,v)\notin\mathcal{B}]&\geqslant\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\textbf{a}\sim\mathcal{U}_{i}}\left[\Pr_{u,v\in\textbf{a},u\neq v}[T_{1}[\textbf{a}](u)=f_{i}(u)\land T_{1}[\textbf{a}](v)=f_{i}(v)]\right]\\
&\geqslant\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\textbf{a}\sim\mathcal{U}_{i}}\left[\Pr_{u\in\textbf{a}}[T_{1}[\textbf{a}](u)=f_{i}(u)]^{2}\right]-\frac{1}{k}\\
&\geqslant(1-\delta)^{2}-\frac{1}{k}\\
&\geqslant 1-2\delta-\frac{1}{k}.\end{split}

(25)

In the second transition we are going from a probability over distinct
u,v\in\textbf{a}
to a probability over
u,v\in\textbf{a}
independent, and the
-1/k
term accounts for the probability that
u=v
. In the third transition we use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the fact that
f_{i}
and
T_{1}[\textbf{a}]
agree on at least
(1-\delta)
-fraction of vertices in
a
for every
\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
.

Next, by item 3 of
Theorem

2.9
and the fact that
d
is sufficiently large relative to
k
, we get that the second singular value of the bipartite inclusion graph
(X(k),X(2))
is
O(1/k)
. Applying
Lemma

2.3
with the left and right subsets are
\mathcal{U}_{i}
and
\mathcal{B}
respectively gives:

\left|\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k),\{u,v\}\in\binom{\textbf{a}}{2}}[\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}_{i},\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{B}]-\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\mu_{2}(\mathcal{B})\right|\leqslant O\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)\cdot\sqrt{\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\mu_{2}(\mathcal{B})}.

Dividing both sides by
\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})
and rearranging gives

\displaystyle\mu_{2}(\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\leqslant\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim\mathcal{U}_{i},\{u,v\}\in\binom{\textbf{a}}{2}}[(u,v)\in\mathcal{B}]+O\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\mu_{2}(\mathcal{B})}}{\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\delta+\frac{1}{k}+O\left(\frac{1}{k\sqrt{\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\delta,

where in the transition we used (
25
), and in the last transition we used
\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\geqslant\delta^{C}
and the fact that
k
is sufficiently large relative to
1/\delta
.
∎

Now combining
Lemma

6.4
with the unique decoding soundness assumption of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf hdx}}
from
Theorem

5.1
, we get that for each
i\in[L]
, there is
w_{i}\in\mathcal{L}
such that

\Pr_{(t,u)\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf hdx}}}[D^{{\sf hdx}}_{t}(u,f_{i}(u))\neq\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}]\leqslant 5\sqrt{\delta},

(26)

and we recall that the marginal distribution of
u
in the probability above is the stationary over
X(1)
.
We are now ready to prove list-decoding soundness of
\mathcal{D}_{{\sf dp}}
by showing that the
w_{i}
obtained by (
26
) give the desired list.

Proof of List-Decoding Soundness for
Theorem

6.1
.

Take
L=\delta^{-C}
and
f_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i},w_{i}
as above for each
i\in[L]
. Set
\mathcal{U}^{\star}:=\bigcup_{i\in[L]}\mathcal{U}_{i}
. Then

\displaystyle\Pr_{(t,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}}\left[T_{1}[\textbf{a}]|_{\textbf{b}}=T_{2}[\textbf{b}]\land D_{t}(\textbf{a},T_{1}[\textbf{a}])\notin\{\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\}_{i\in[L]}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{(t,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}}\left[T_{1}[\textbf{a}]|_{\textbf{b}}=T_{2}[\textbf{b}]\land D_{t}(\textbf{a},T_{1}[\textbf{a}])\notin\{\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\}_{i\in[L]}\land\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}^{\star}\right]

\displaystyle+\Pr_{(t,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}}\left[T_{1}[\textbf{a}]|_{\textbf{b}}=T_{2}[\textbf{b}]\land D_{t}(\textbf{a},T_{1}[\textbf{a}])\notin\{\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\}_{i\in[L]}\land\textbf{a}\notin\mathcal{U}^{\star}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Pr_{(t,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}}\left[D_{t}(\textbf{a},T_{1}[\textbf{a}])\notin\{\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\}_{i\in[L]}\land\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}^{\star}\right]

\displaystyle+\Pr_{(t,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}}\left[T_{1}[\textbf{a}]|_{\textbf{b}}=T_{2}[\textbf{b}]\land\textbf{a}\notin\mathcal{U}^{\star}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Pr_{(t,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}}\left[D_{t}(\textbf{a},T_{1}[\textbf{a}])\notin\{\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\}_{i\in[L]}\land\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}^{\star}\right]+O\left(\sqrt{\delta}\right),

where in the last transition, we are using the second item of
Lemma

6.2
to bound

\Pr_{(t,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}}\left[T_{1}[\textbf{a}]|_{\textbf{b}}=T_{2}[\textbf{b}]\land\textbf{a}\notin\mathcal{U}^{\star}\right]=\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k),\textbf{b}\subseteq\textbf{a}}\left[T_{1}[\textbf{a}]|_{\textbf{b}}=T_{2}[\textbf{b}]\land\textbf{a}\notin\mathcal{U}^{\star}\right]\leqslant\frac{\sqrt{\delta}}{10}.

Next, we perform a union bound

\displaystyle\Pr_{(t,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}}\left[D_{t}(\textbf{a},T_{1}[\textbf{a}])\notin\{\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\}_{i\in[L]}\land\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}^{\star}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{L}\Pr_{(t,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}}\left[D_{t}(\textbf{a},T_{1}[\textbf{a}])\neq\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\land\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\sim[L]}\left[\Pr_{(t,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}}[D_{t}(\textbf{a},T_{1}[\textbf{a}])\neq\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\;|\;\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}]\right].

In the last line we let
i\sim[L]
be the distribution where
i
is chosen with weight proportional to
\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})
. Then, the last line follows from the fact that the
\mathcal{U}_{i}
’s are disjoint, so the events
\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
are mutually exclusive. Fix
i
and examine a probability from the last line more closely. Here, we unpack the joint distribution of
(t,\textbf{a})
under
\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}
. By definition, this distribution is obtained by sampling
\textbf{a}\sim X(k)
, then a random
u\in\textbf{a}
and finally
t\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf hdx}}(\cdot,u)
. We have

\begin{split}\Pr_{(t,\textbf{a},\textbf{b})\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf dp}}}[D_{t}(\textbf{a},T_{1}[\textbf{a}])\neq\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\;|\;\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}]&\leqslant\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim\mathcal{U}_{i},u\in\textbf{a},t\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf hdx}}(\cdot,u)}[D^{{\sf hdx}}_{t}(u,f_{i}(u))\neq\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}]\\
&+\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim\mathcal{U}_{i},u\in\textbf{a},t\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf hdx}}(\cdot,u)}[T_{1}[\textbf{a}](u)\neq f_{i}(u)].\end{split}

(27)

Now we bound each term individually. Towards the first term, define the function
H_{i}:X(1)\to[0,1]
as

H_{i}(u)=\Pr_{t\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf hdx}}(\cdot,u)}[D^{{\sf hdx}}_{t}(u,f_{i}(u))\neq\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}].

In particular, from (
26
) we have

\mu_{1}(H_{i}):=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{u\sim X(1)}[H_{i}(u)]=\Pr_{(t,u)\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf hdx}}}[D^{{\sf hdx}}_{t}(u,f_{i}(u))\neq\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}]\leqslant 5\sqrt{\delta}.

(28)

By
Lemma

2.3
and the fact that the bipartite inclusion graph on
(X(k),X(1))
has second singular value at most
O(1/k)
by item 3 of
Theorem

2.9
(and the fact that
d
is sufficiently large relative to
k
), it follows that

\left|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k),u\in\textbf{a}}[\mathbbm{1}_{\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}}\cdot H_{i}(u)]-\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\mu_{1}(H_{i})\right|\leqslant O\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)\cdot\sqrt{\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})}.

(29)

Now note that the first term on the right hand side of (
27
) is

\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim\mathcal{U}_{i},u\in\textbf{a},t\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf hdx}}(\cdot,u)}[D^{{\sf hdx}}_{t}(u,f_{i}(u))\neq\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k),u\in\textbf{a}}[H_{i}(u)\;|\;\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}]=\frac{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\textbf{a}\sim X(k),u\in\textbf{a}}[\mathbbm{1}_{\textbf{a}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}}\cdot H_{i}(u)]}{\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})}.

Thus, by (
29
),

\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim\mathcal{U}_{i},u\in\textbf{a},t\sim\mathcal{Q}_{{\sf hdx}}(\cdot,u)}[D^{{\sf hdx}}_{t}(u,f_{i}(u))\neq\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}]\leqslant 5\sqrt{\delta}+O\left(\frac{1}{k\cdot\sqrt{\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})}}\right)\leqslant 6\sqrt{\delta}.

The first inequality follows by (
28
) and the second inequality follows from the fact that
\mu_{k}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\geqslant\delta^{C}
and
k
is sufficiently large relative to
1/\delta
.

For the second term on the right hand side of (
27
), we have that

\displaystyle\Pr_{\textbf{a}\sim\mathcal{U}_{i},u\in\textbf{a}}[T_{1}[\textbf{a}](u)\neq f_{i}(u)]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\textbf{a}\sim\mathcal{U}_{i}}[{\sf dist}(f_{i}|_{\textbf{a}},T_{1}[\textbf{a}])]\leqslant\delta

by definition of
f_{i}
and
\mathcal{U}_{i}
. Altogether, we get that (
27
) is at most
6\sqrt{\delta}+\delta\leqslant 7\sqrt{\delta}
, and plugging this up gives that the list-decoding error is at most
O(\sqrt{\delta})
.
∎

7
Transformations on dPCPs

Theorem

6.1
gives us constructions of quasi-linear length dPCPs with arbitrarily small error in the list-decoding soundness, as required by
Theorem

3.12
, but with alphabet size that is super-polynomial, rather than constant. Our goal now is to reduce the alphabet size in
Theorem

6.1
down to a constant while maintaining all of the other desired properties; namely, length and small soundness. As discussed in the introduction, we achieve this via a series of transformations, and in this section we present a few of the basic ones here. The transformations in this section are both variants of well known transformations for PCPs.

7.1
Right Alphabet Reduction

We start with right alphabet reduction. This procedure is a simple application of error correcting codes along the lines of
[
28
, Section 5.2]
except that for our purposes, we also need to keep track of the degree related parameters and analyze the list-decoding soundness (rather than the standard soundness).

Lemma 7.1
.

Suppose a language
\mathcal{L}
has a projection dPCP

\mathcal{D}=\left(A\cup B,E,\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right)

with perfect completeness,
(L,\varepsilon)
-list-decoding soundness, and projection decision complexity
{\sf ProjComp}
. Then for every
0<\eta<1
, there is a polynomial-time algorithm which takes
\mathcal{D}
and outputs a new projection dPCP for
\mathcal{L}
,

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\left(A\cup(B\times[m]),E^{\prime},\Sigma_{A},\Omega,\{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right)

where
m=O_{\eta}(\log|\Sigma_{B}|)
and
\Omega
is an alphabet of size
O_{\eta}(1)
. Additionally,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
satisfies the following:

•

Degrees.
The decoding degree of each vertex and right degree of each vertex are the same as in
\mathcal{D}
. The left degree of each vertex is
m
times its left degree in
\mathcal{D}
.

•

Projection Decision Complexity.
The decision complexity is
{\sf ProjComp}+O(\log|\Sigma_{B}|)
.

•

Decoding Complexity.
The decoding complexity is preserved.

•

Preserves Agnosticity.
If the complete decoding distribution of
\mathcal{D}
is agnostic, then so is that of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

•

Completeness.

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
has perfect completeness.

•

Soundness.

(L,\varepsilon+3\eta)
-list-decoding soundness.

Proof.

Fix
\eta\in(0,1)
and let
\mathcal{D}
denote the original dPCP. Let us arbitrarily identify
\Sigma_{B}
with
\{0,1\}^{k}
for
k=\log(|\Sigma_{B}|)
and take
\mathcal{C}:\{0,1\}^{k}\to\Omega^{m}
to be the code from
Lemma

2.1
instantiated with relative distance
1-\eta^{3}
, alphabet size
|\Omega|=O_{\eta}(1)
, and blocklength
m=O_{\eta}\left(k\right)
.

The new dPCP
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is constructed as follows. The constraint graph is bipartite and has vertices
A\cup B\times[m]
. The edges of the constraint graph are all
(a,(b,i))\subseteq A\times\left(B\times[m]\right)
such that
(a,b)\in E
. The constraint on
(a,(b,i))
is as follows. Let
\sigma_{a}
be label for
a
and
\omega
be a label for
(b,i)
. Since
\mathcal{D}
has projection constraints, there is a unique
\sigma_{b}\in\Sigma_{B}
satisfying
\Phi_{(a,b)}(\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b})=1
. The constraint on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
checks if
\mathcal{C}(\sigma_{b})_{i}=\omega
. The new decoding distributions
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}
are generated by choosing
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}
, choosing
i\in[m]
uniformly, and outputting
(a,(b,i))
. We proceed to verifying the desired properties.

Degrees.

It is clear that decoding degree is preserved, as each
a\in A
has the same decoding neighborhood in both
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. The right degree is preserved because each
(b,i)\in B\times[m]
has the same neighbors in the new constraint graph as neighbors as
b
in the original constraint graph. For the left degree, note that each
a\in A
is adjacent to
(b,i)
for all
b
that are adjacent to
a
in the original constraint graph and all
i\in[m]
.

Projection Decision Complexity.

Fix a constraint
(a,(b,i))
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and suppose the assignment to
a
is
\sigma
. The projection circuit first computes the
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{B}
which is the unique
b
alphabet symbol that would satisfy the constraint in
\mathcal{D}
. This part has circuit complexity
{\sf ProjComp}
. Then, the projection circuit calculates the encoding of
\sigma^{\prime}
, under the linear-time encodable code, and outputs its
i
th symbol. This part has circuit complexity
O(\log|\Sigma_{B}|)
since
\mathcal{C}
is linear-time encodable. Overall, this shows that the projection decision complexity is
{\sf ProjComp}+O(\log|\Sigma_{B}|)
.

Decoding Complexity.

The decoder is unchanged and hence the decoding complexity is preserved.

Preserves Agnosticity

Let
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
denote the complete decoding distributions of
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
respectively. Note that for any
(b,i)\in B\times[m]
and
t\in[n]
, we have

\mathcal{Q}(\cdot,\cdot,b)=\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\cdot,\cdot,(b,i))

and

\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,b)=\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(t,\cdot,(b,i))
.
It follows that agnosticity is preserved.

Perfect Completeness.

Fix
w\in\mathcal{L}
and let
T_{1}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
and
T_{2}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
be the assignment satisfying all of the constraints to
\mathcal{D}
and decoding to
w
with probability
1
for every
t\in[n]
. Then consider the assignment to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
where the left assignment is still
T_{1}
and the new right assignment, which we call
T^{\prime}_{2}:B\times[m]\to\Omega
is defined as

T^{\prime}_{2}[(b,i)]=\mathcal{C}(T_{2}[b])_{i}.

It is straightforward to check that this assignment satisfies all of the constraints of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
(and
T_{1}
still decodes to
w
with probability
1
for every
t\in[n]
because it is unchanged).

List-Decoding Soundness.

Fix an assignment to
T_{1}:A\to\Sigma
and let
w_{1},\ldots,w_{L}\in\mathcal{L}
be the list guaranteed by the list-decoding assumption of
\mathcal{D}
. For each
a\in A
and
b\in B
such that
(a,b)\in E
, we let
T_{1}[a]_{\rightarrow b}\in\Sigma_{B}
be the unique symbol
\sigma
such that
\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{1}[a],\sigma)=1
. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the complete decoding distribution of
\mathcal{D}
generated by choosing
t\in[n]
,
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}
, and outputting
(t,a,b)
.
For each
b\in B
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}
, define

\delta_{b}(\sigma)=\Pr_{(t,a)\sim\mathcal{Q}(\cdot,\cdot,b)}[T_{1}[a]_{\rightarrow b}=\sigma\land D_{t}(a,T_{1}[a])\notin\{\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\}_{i\in[L]}].

For any
T_{2}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
, the expectation
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\delta_{b}(T_{2}[b])]
is precisely the list-decoding error in
\mathcal{D}
of
T_{1},T_{2}
relative to the list
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{L}\}
. Here and henceforth,
b\sim\mathcal{Q}
, refers to
b
sampled according to the marginal of
\mathcal{Q}
over
B
. By the list-decoding soundness assumption of
\mathcal{D}
, we have

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\delta_{b}(T_{2}[b])]\leqslant\varepsilon.

(30)

for any
T_{2}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
.

Now, fix an arbitrary right assignment
T^{\prime}_{2}:B\times[m]\to\Omega
for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. We will show that the list-decoding error of
T_{1}
and
T^{\prime}_{2}
relative to
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{L}\}
is at most
\varepsilon+3\eta
. We can express this list-decoding error as follows:

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim\mathcal{Q}}\left[\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\delta_{b}(\sigma)\cdot\Pr_{i\in[m]}[T^{\prime}_{2}[(b,i)]=\mathcal{C}(\sigma)_{i}]\right],

(31)

so our goal is to now bound the expectation above.
Fix a
b\in B
and examine the summation in the expectation. Define
{\sf list}_{b}
to consist of all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}
such that
\mathcal{C}(\sigma)
agrees with at least
\eta
-fraction of the entries of the string
(T[(b,1)],\ldots,T[(b,m)])\in\Omega^{m}
. By the list-decoding bound in
2.2
and the fact that
\eta>2\eta^{3/2}
, we have that
|{\sf list}_{b}|\leqslant 2/\eta
. We define a right assignment,
T^{*}_{2}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
, for
\mathcal{D}
as follows. For each
b\in B
, set
T^{*}_{2}[b]
to be the alphabet symbol
\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}
which maximizes
\delta_{b}(\sigma)
. Then, the summation from (
31
) can be bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\delta_{b}(\sigma)\cdot\Pr_{i\in[m]}[T^{\prime}_{2}[(b,i)]=\mathcal{C}(\sigma)_{i}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in{\sf list}_{b}}\delta_{b}(\sigma)\cdot\Pr_{i\in[m]}[T^{\prime}_{2}[(b,i)]=\mathcal{C}(\sigma)_{i}]+\sum_{\sigma\notin{\sf list}_{b}}\delta_{b}(\sigma)\cdot\Pr_{i\in[m]}[T^{\prime}_{2}[(b,i)]=\mathcal{C}(\sigma)_{i}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\sigma\in{\sf list}_{b}}\delta_{b}(\sigma)\cdot\Pr_{i\in[m]}[T^{\prime}_{2}[(b,i)]=\mathcal{C}(\sigma)_{i}]+\eta

\displaystyle\leqslant\delta_{b}(T^{*}_{2}[b])\cdot\left(\sum_{\sigma\in{\sf list}_{b}}\Pr_{i\in[m]}[T^{\prime}_{2}[(b,i)]=\mathcal{C}(\sigma)_{i}]\right)+\eta

\displaystyle\leqslant\delta_{b}(T^{*}_{2}[b])\cdot\left(\Pr_{i\in[m]}[\exists\sigma\in{\sf list}_{b},\;T^{\prime}_{2}[(b,i)]=\mathcal{C}(\sigma)_{i}]+\sum_{\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}\in{\sf list}_{b}}\Pr_{i\in[m]}[T_{2}(b,i)=\mathcal{C}(\sigma)_{i}=\mathcal{C}(\sigma^{\prime})_{i}]\right)+\eta

\displaystyle\leqslant\delta_{b}(T^{*}_{2}[b])\cdot(1+2\eta)+\eta

\displaystyle\leqslant\delta_{b}(T^{*}_{2}[b])+3\eta.

In the second transition we used
\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\delta_{b}(\sigma)\leqslant 1
and that
\Pr_{i\in[m]}[T^{\prime}_{2}[(b,i)]=\mathcal{C}(\sigma)_{i}]\leqslant\eta
for each
\sigma\notin{\sf list}_{b}
. In the third transition we used the definition of
T^{*}_{2}[b]
. In the fourth transition we used the inclusion-exclusion principle.
In the fifth transition we used the fact that
|{\sf list}_{b}|\leqslant 2/\eta
and that for
\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}
we have

\Pr_{i\in[m]}[\mathcal{C}(\sigma)_{i}=\mathcal{C}(\sigma^{\prime})_{i}]\leqslant\eta^{3}
.
Plugging this bound back into the expectation from (
31
), we get that the list-decoding error of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is at most

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim\mathcal{Q}}\left[\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\delta_{b}(\sigma)\cdot\Pr_{i\in[m]}[T^{\prime}_{2}[(b,i)]=\mathcal{C}(\sigma)_{i}]\right]\leqslant\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim\mathcal{Q}}\left[\delta_{b}\left(T^{*}_{2}[b]\right)\right]+3\eta\leqslant\varepsilon+3\eta,

where in the last transition we used (
30
) applied to the tables
T_{1},T^{*}_{2}
.
∎

7.2
Right Degree Reduction

The goal of this subsection is to describe a procedure which transforms an arbitrary dPCP,
\mathcal{D}
, into a right-regular dPCP
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
with constant right degree. Additionally, in the complete decoding distribution of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, the marginal distribution over the right vertices is uniform. This transformation is similar to the one in
Section

4.3
, but some extra care is needed since we deal with list-decoding soundness rather than unique-decoding soundness.

Lemma 7.2
.

Suppose a language
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\Sigma_{0}^{n}
has a projection dPCP

\mathcal{D}=\left(A\cup B,E,\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right)

with
(L,\varepsilon)
-list-decoding soundness and suppose there exists
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
(a,b)\in A\times B
, one can write
\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,b)=\frac{w(a,b)}{M}
for some
w(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for any
d^{\prime}\geqslant\min_{a,b}w(a,b)
, there is a polynomial-time algorithm which takes
\mathcal{D}
and outputs a projection dPCP

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\left(A\cup B^{\prime},E^{\prime},\{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right),

for
\mathcal{L}
such that the following hold:

•

Length.

|B^{\prime}|=M
and hence the length is
|A|+M
.

•

Degrees.
The left degree of every vertex is multiplied by
d^{\prime}
, the decoding degree of every vertex is preserved, and the right degree of every vertex is
d^{\prime}
.

•

Projection decision complexity.
The projection decision complexity is preserved.

•

Decoding complexity.
The decoding complexity is preserved.

•

Complete decoding distribution.
Call the complete decoding distribution
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
. Then,

–

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is
d^{\prime}M
-discrete.

–

For every
b\in B^{\prime}
, we have
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,\circ,b)=\frac{1}{M}
and hence the marginal of
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
over
B^{\prime}
is uniform.

–

For every
a\in A
, we have
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,a,\circ)=\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,\circ)

–

If
\mathcal{Q}
is agnostic, then
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is agnostic as well.

•

Completeness.
The dPCP
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
has perfect completeness.

•

List-decoding soundness.
(L,\varepsilon+O(d^{\prime-1/2}))
-list-decoding soundness.

Proof.

Let
G_{\mathsf{disc}}
be the bipartite graph on
A\cup B
, where there are
w(a,b)
multi-edges between
a
and
b
for each
(a,b)\in A\times B
.
The dPCP
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is constructed as follows. For each vertex
b\in B
, let
\Gamma_{\mathsf{disc}}(b)
be the neighborhood of
b
in
G_{\mathsf{disc}}
, where we view
\Gamma_{\mathsf{disc}}(b)
as a multiset. Specifically, each neighbor
a
is listed with multiplicity equal to the number of edges going from
b
to
a
, so that
|\Gamma_{\mathsf{disc}}(b)|=d(b)
. Then, let
H_{b}=(A_{b},B_{b},E_{b})
be a
d^{\prime}
-regular bipartite graph with
d(b)
vertices on each side and second singular value
O(d^{\prime-1/2})
, and identify
A_{b}
with
\Gamma_{\mathsf{disc}}(b)\subseteq A
.
Recall that such a graph can be constructed in polynomial-time by
Lemma

2.5
. We note that after this identification, there may be multiple vertices in
A_{b}
that have been identified with the same vertex
a\in\Gamma_{\mathsf{disc}}(b)
, so there may be multiple edges between two vertices.

The constraint graph of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
has left side
A
and right side
\cup_{b\in B}B_{b}
. We include an edge
(a,b^{\prime})
if for the unique
b
such that
b^{\prime}\in B_{b}
, it holds that
a\in\Gamma(b)
and
(a,b^{\prime})\in E_{b}
(we recall that each vertex in
A_{b}
is identified with a vertex in
A
). The constraint on
(a,b^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is the same as the constraint on
(a,b)
in
\mathcal{D}
, where
b^{\prime}\in B_{b}
. The left and right alphabets of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
remain
\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B}
. The decoders for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
also remain the same and are still
\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}
. By this we mean, for a left vertex
a
and a label
\sigma
in its alphabet, the decoder for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
still outputs
D_{t}(a,\sigma)
, and hence we still denote it by
D_{t}
. Finally, the complete decoding distribution of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
, which is generated by choosing
(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}
, sampling a uniform
b^{\prime}\in B_{b}
that is adjacent to
a
in
H_{b}
and outputting
(t,a,b^{\prime})
. The decoding distributions are thus given by
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}=\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
.

It is clear that the algorithm preserves the left alphabet, right alphabet, projection decision complexity, decoding complexity, and decoding degree. Furthermore, one can check that the left degree of each vertex in the constraint graph gets multiplied by a factor of
d^{\prime}
.

Complete Decoding Distribution.

Fix
a\in A
and
b^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}
and suppose
b^{\prime}
appears in
B_{b}
for
b\in B
. Let us first check that the complete decoding distribution is
d^{\prime}M
-discrete. We have

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,a,b^{\prime})=\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,b)\cdot\frac{c_{b}(a,b^{\prime})}{d^{\prime}}=\frac{w(a,b)\cdot c_{b}(a,b^{\prime})}{d^{\prime}M}

(32)

where
c_{b}(a,b^{\prime})
is the number of multiedges between

a
and
b^{\prime}
in the graph
H_{b}
, and is an integer in the range
[0,d^{\prime}]
. To see the first equality, note that in order for
(a,b^{\prime})
to be sampled from
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
, we first need
(a,b)
to be sampled from
\mathcal{Q}
, where
b
is such that
b^{\prime}\in H_{b}
. Then
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
chooses a neighbor of
a
in
H_{b}
, so there is a
c_{b}(a,b^{\prime})/d^{\prime}
probability of choosing
b^{\prime}
. The second transition is due to the assumption that
\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,b)=\frac{w(a,b)}{M}
given by the lemma statement. It follows that the complete decoding distribution is
d^{\prime}M
-discrete

To see the second item, note that the probability that
b^{\prime}
is output by the complete decoding distribution
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,\circ,b^{\prime})=\mathcal{Q}(\circ,\circ,b)\cdot\frac{1}{d(b)}=\left(\sum_{a\in A}\frac{w(a,b)}{M}\right)\frac{1}{d(b)}=\frac{1}{M}.

In the first transition we used the fact that conditioned on
b
, the right-vertex we output is uniform in
B_{b}
and
|B_{b}|=d(b)
.
In the second transition we used
\mathcal{Q}(\cdot,a,b)=w(a,b)/M
. In the last transition we used the fact that
\sum_{a\in A}w(a,b)=d(b)
.

For the third item, fix
a\in A
, then

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,a,\circ)=\sum\limits_{b\in B}\sum_{b^{\prime}\in B_{b}}\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,a,b^{\prime})=\sum\limits_{b\in B}\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,b)\sum_{b^{\prime}\in B_{b}}\frac{c_{b}(a,b^{\prime})}{d^{\prime}}=\sum\limits_{b\in B}\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,b)=\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,\circ).

In the second transition we used (
32
) and
in the third transition we used the fact that the degree of
a
in
H_{b}
is
d^{\prime}
.

For the fourth item, suppose that
\mathcal{Q}
is agnostic and take
b^{\prime}\in B_{b}
. Note that
\mathcal{Q}(\circ,\cdot,b)=\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,\cdot,b^{\prime})
, and for any
t\in[n]

we have
\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,b)=\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(t,\cdot,b^{\prime})
.
It follows
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is agnostic.

Perfect Completeness.

Suppose
w\in\mathcal{L}
and let
T_{1}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
,
T_{2}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
be the assignments satisfying all of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}
that are decoded to
w
with probability
1
over
\mathcal{Q}
. Define
T_{2}^{\prime}
by setting
T^{\prime}_{2}[b^{\prime}]=T_{2}[b]
for each
b^{\prime}\in B_{b}
. It is straightforward to check that
T_{1},T_{2}^{\prime}
satisfy all of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, and that
T_{1}
decodes to
w
with probability
1
over
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.

List-Decoding Soundness.

Fix an assignment
T_{1}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
.
Then by the
(L,\varepsilon)
-list-decoding soundness of
\mathcal{D}
we may find
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{L}\}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
such that for any
T_{2}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
, we have

\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{1}[a],T_{2}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{1}[a])\notin\{\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\}_{i\in[L]}]\leqslant\varepsilon.

To establish the desired list-decoding soundness for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, we will show that for any right-assignment, the list decoding error relative to the same list
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{L}\}
is small.

Fix any assignment
T^{\prime}_{2}:B^{\prime}\to\Sigma_{B}
and for each
b\in B
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}
, define the sets

X_{b,\sigma}=\{b^{\prime}\in B_{b}\;|\;T^{\prime}_{2}[b^{\prime}]=\sigma\}\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{b,\sigma}=\{a^{\prime}\in A_{b}\;|\;\Phi_{(a^{\prime},b)}(T_{1}[a^{\prime}],\sigma)=1\}.

In words,
X_{b,\sigma}
is the set of vertices in
B_{b}
that are assigned
\sigma
under
T^{\prime}_{2}
, and
Y_{b,\sigma}
is the set of vertices in
a^{\prime}\in A_{b}
such that the assignments
a\to T_{1}[a^{\prime}]
and
b\to\sigma
satisfy the constraint on
(a^{\prime},b)
in the original dPCP,
\mathcal{D}
. It is clear that the sets
\{X_{b,\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{b}}
form a partition of
B_{b}
. Additionally, since the constraints in
\mathcal{D}
are all projections, the sets

\{Y_{b,\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}
form a partition of
A_{b}
. Define the functions
F_{b,\sigma}:A_{b}\to[0,1]
,
G_{b,\sigma}:B_{b}\to\{0,1\}
where

G_{b,\sigma}(b^{\prime}):=\mathbbm{1}_{b^{\prime}\in X_{b,\sigma}}\quad\text{and}\quad F_{b,\sigma}(a):=\mathbbm{1}_{a\in Y_{b,\sigma}}\cdot\Pr_{t\sim\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\cdot,a,b)}\left[D_{t}(a,T_{1}[a])\notin\{\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\}_{i\in[L]}\right].

Abusing notation, we denote
\mu(F_{b,\sigma})=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a\in A_{b}}[F_{b,\sigma}(a^{\prime})]
and
\mu(G_{b,\sigma})=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b^{\prime}\in B_{b}}[G_{b,\sigma}(b^{\prime})]
. Consider the randomized assignment
T^{*}_{2}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
obtained by setting
T^{*}_{2}(b)=\sigma
with probability
\mu(G_{b,\sigma})
. By the list-decoding soundness applied with each fixing of
T^{*}_{2}
, we get

\begin{split}\varepsilon&\geqslant\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{T^{*}_{2}}\left[\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}\left[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{1}[a],T^{*}_{2}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{1}[a])\notin\{\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\}_{i\in[L]}\right]\right]\\
&=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}\left[\Pr_{b^{\prime}\in B_{b}}\left[\Phi_{a,b}(T_{1}[a],T_{2}[b^{\prime}])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{1}[a])\notin\{\left(w_{i}\right)_{t}\}_{i\in[L]}\right]\right]\\
&=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim\mathcal{Q}(\circ,\circ,\cdot)}\left[\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mu(G_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(F_{b,\sigma})]\right],\end{split}

(33)

In the third transition, we are using the fact that conditioned on
b
, the distribution of
a
under
\mathcal{Q}
is uniformly random in
A_{b}
.
On the other hand, the list-decoding error of the assignment
T_{1},T^{\prime}_{2}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
relative to the list
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{L}\}
, can be written as:

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim\mathcal{Q}(\circ,\circ,\cdot)}\left[\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(a,b^{\prime})\in E_{b}}[F_{b,\sigma}(a)\cdot G_{b,\sigma}(b^{\prime})]\right].

(34)

Hence, our goal is to now bound the expression above, and we will do so by relating it to (
33
) via the expander mixing lemma.
Fix a vertex
b\in B
and an alphabet symbol
\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}
. Using
Lemma

2.3
we get

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(a,b^{\prime})\in E_{b}}[F_{b,\sigma}(a)\cdot G_{b,\sigma}(b^{\prime})]\leqslant\mu(F_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(G_{b,\sigma})+O(d^{\prime-1/2})\cdot\sqrt{\mu(F_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(G_{b,\sigma})}.

Keeping
b\in B
fixed and summing this inequality over all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(a,b^{\prime})\in E_{b}}[F_{b,\sigma}(a)\cdot G_{b,\sigma}(b^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mu(F_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(G_{b,\sigma})+O(d^{\prime-1/2})\cdot\sqrt{\mu(F_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(G_{b,\sigma})}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mu(F_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(G_{b,\sigma})+O(d^{\prime-1/2}),

where the second inequality uses Cauchy-Schwarz and the fact that
\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mu(F_{b,\sigma})\leqslant 1
and
\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mu(G_{b,\sigma})=1
. Thus, we have

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim\mathcal{Q}(\circ,\circ,\cdot)}\left[\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(a,b^{\prime})\in E_{b}}[F_{b,\sigma}(a)\cdot G_{b,\sigma}(b^{\prime})]\right]\leqslant\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{b\sim\mathcal{Q}(\circ,\circ,\cdot)}\left[\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}}\mu(G_{b,\sigma})\cdot\mu(F_{b,\sigma})\right]+O(d^{\prime-1/2})\leqslant\varepsilon+O(d^{\prime-1/2}),

where in the last transition we used (
33
).
∎

Lemma

7.2
gives a right degree reduction when the complete decoding distribution is
M
-discrete and results in a dPCP of size at least
M
. For dPCPs with arbitrary complete decoding distribution, which may not be
M
-discrete for any small
M
, we can still obtain a right degree reduction by slightly altering the complete decoding distribution first. Below we state a simple fact about distributions that we will use for this task, which will also be used in subsequent sections.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\Delta
be a distribution over a support of size
n
, say
[n]
. Then for every
\varepsilon>0
and every integer
M\geqslant\frac{n}{2\varepsilon}
, there exist
w(1),\ldots,w(n)\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{i\in[n]}w(i)=M
and the distribution
\Delta_{\mathsf{disc}}
, given by
\Delta_{\mathsf{disc}}(i)=\frac{w(i)}{M}
, satisfies

•

\mathsf{TV}(\Delta,\Delta_{\mathsf{disc}})\leqslant\varepsilon
,

•

for every
i\in[n]
,
|\Delta_{\mathsf{disc}}(i)-\Delta(i)|\leqslant\frac{1}{M}
,

•

{\sf supp}(\Delta_{\mathsf{disc}})\subseteq{\sf supp}(\Delta)
.

As a consequence, every distribution with support size
n
is
\varepsilon
-close in total-variation distance to an
O_{\varepsilon}(n)
-discrete distribution.

Proof.

Suppose
\supp(\Delta)
has size
n^{\prime}\leqslant n
, and without loss of generality suppose
\supp(\Delta)=[n^{\prime}]
. For each
i\in[n^{\prime}]
, let
z_{i}=\lfloor M\cdot\Delta(i)\rfloor
and let
r=M-\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}z_{i}
. Note that
r\leqslant n^{\prime}
. Now increment the each of
z_{1},\ldots,z_{r}
by
1
and keep the remainder of the
z_{i}
’s the same. Let the resulting values be
z^{\prime}_{i}
after incrementing.

We have

\left|z^{\prime}_{i}-M\cdot\Delta(i)\right|\leqslant 1,

(35)

for all
i\in[n^{\prime}]
. Now define the distribution
\Delta_{\mathsf{disc}}(i)
by

\Delta_{\mathsf{disc}}(i)=\begin{cases}\frac{z^{\prime}_{i}}{M}\quad&\text{ if $i\in[n^{\prime}]$,}\\
0\quad&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Note that
\Delta_{\mathsf{disc}}(i)
is indeed a distribution over
[n]
because
\sum_{i\in[n]}z^{\prime}_{i}=M
and by construction
\supp(\Delta_{\mathsf{disc}})\subseteq\supp(\Delta)
. Moreover, (
35
) implies that for every
i\in[n]
,
|\Delta_{\mathsf{disc}}(i)-\Delta(i)|\leqslant\frac{1}{M}
, and as a consequence,

\mathsf{TV}(\Delta,\Delta_{\mathsf{disc}})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[n]}\left|\Delta(i)-\Delta_{\mathsf{disc}}(i)\right|\leqslant\sum_{i\in[n^{\prime}]}\frac{1}{2M}\leqslant\frac{n}{2M}\leqslant\varepsilon.\qed

The following result is a version of
Lemma

7.2
in which the given dPCP
\mathcal{D}
does not necessarily have a complete decoding distribution whose marginal over the edges is
M
-discrete . Instead, we require
\mathcal{D}
to have bounded decoding degree, and we show how to modify the complete decoding distribution into a discrete one without affecting the rest of the parameters by much. For sake of simplicity and because it suffices for our application, we focus on the case where decoding degree is
1
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Suppose a language
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\Sigma_{0}^{n}
has a projection dPCP

\mathcal{D}=\left(A\cup B,E,\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right)

with
(L,\varepsilon)
-list-decoding soundness, left degree
\mathsf{ld}
, and decoding degree
1
. Then for every
M\geqslant\frac{200\cdot\mathsf{ld}\cdot|A|}{\varepsilon^{2}\cdot n}
and for every
d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a polynomial-time algorithm which takes
\mathcal{D}
and outputs a dPCP

\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}=\left(A^{\prime\prime}\cup B^{\prime\prime},E^{\prime\prime},\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right)

for
\mathcal{L}
such that the following hold:

•

Length.

|B^{\prime\prime}|=d^{\prime}\cdot n\cdot M
and
A^{\prime\prime}\subseteq A
, so the length is at most
|A|+d^{\prime}\cdot n\cdot M
.

•

Degrees.
The decoding degree of every vertex is
1
.

•

Projection decision complexity.
The projection decision complexity is preserved.

•

Decoding complexity.
The decoding complexity is preserved.

•

Complete decoding distribution.
The complete decoding distribution, which we call
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}
, is
d^{\prime 2}\cdot n\cdot M
-discrete and its marginal over
B^{\prime}
is uniform. For any
b\in B
, we have

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}(\circ,\circ,b)=\frac{d^{\prime}}{d^{\prime 2}\cdot n\cdot M}

and for any
a\in A
we have

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}(\circ,a,\circ)\leqslant\max\left\{\frac{1}{nM}+\frac{\varepsilon}{10\mathsf{ld}|A|},\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,\circ)+\frac{\mathsf{ld}}{nM}\right\}.

•

Completeness.
The dPCP
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
has perfect completeness.

•

List-decoding soundness.
\left(L,\frac{9}{8}\cdot\varepsilon+O(d^{\prime-1/2})\right)
-list-decoding soundness.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the complete decoding distribution of
\mathcal{D}
and let
\mathsf{ld},\varepsilon
and
M
be as in the lemma statement. For each
a\in A
, we have that the decoding degree of
a
is
1
, and we denote by
t_{a}
its unique decoding neighbor. Since the decoding degree is
1
, we have

\sum_{t\in[n]}|\supp(\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,\cdot))|\leqslant\sum_{a\in A}|\supp(\mathcal{Q}(t_{a},a,\cdot))|\leqslant\mathsf{ld}\cdot|A|.

Hence, taking
S\subseteq[n]
to be the set of
t\in[n]
such that
|\supp(\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,\cdot))|\leqslant\frac{10\cdot\mathsf{ld}\cdot|A|}{\varepsilon\cdot n}
, we have by Markov’s inequality that

\Pr_{t\in[n]}[t\in S]\geqslant 1-\frac{\varepsilon}{10}.

(36)

Now, for each
t\in S
, let
\Delta_{t}
be the discretized distribution over
A\times B
obtained by applying
Lemma

7.3
to
\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
with denominator
M
. For
t\notin S
, let
\Delta_{t}
be an arbitrary distribution supported on
\supp(\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,\cdot))
such that for each
(a,b)\in\supp(\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,\cdot))
we have either

\Delta_{t}(a,b)=\frac{1}{M}\cdot\bigg\lceil\frac{M}{|\supp(\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,\cdot))|}\bigg\rceil\quad\text{or}\quad\Delta_{t}(a,b)=\frac{1}{M}\cdot\bigg\lfloor\frac{M}{|\supp(\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,\cdot))|}\bigg\rfloor.

It is straightforward to see that such a distribution indeed exists.

Now, let the distribution
Q^{\prime}
be generated by choosing
t\in[n]
uniformly at random, choosing
(a,b)\sim\Delta_{t}
, and outputting
(t,a,b)
. We note the following items:

•

The marginal over
[n]
of
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is uniform; this is clear by description of
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.

•

\supp(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})\subseteq\supp(\mathcal{Q})
; this is clear by construction of
\Delta_{t}
.

•

For each
(a,b)\in A\times B
, if
t_{a}\in S
then
|\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,b)-\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,a,b)|\leqslant\frac{1}{nM}
, otherwise
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,a,b)\leqslant\frac{1}{nM}+\frac{\varepsilon}{10\mathsf{ld}|A|}
; we explain this below.

•

\mathsf{TV}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
; we explain this below.

To see the third item first fix an
(a,b)\in A\times B
. If
t_{a}\in S
, then

\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,b)=\mathcal{Q}(t_{a},a,b)
,

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,a,b)=\Delta_{t_{a}}(a,b)
and the item follows from the guarantee of
Lemma

7.3
. If
t_{a}\not\in S
we have,

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,a,b)\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\cdot\Delta_{t_{a}}(a,b)\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{1+M/|\supp(\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,\cdot))|}{M}\leqslant\frac{1}{nM}+\frac{\varepsilon}{10\mathsf{ld}|A|}.

For the fourth item, we use the fact that
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
have the same marginal distribution over
[n]
, hence

\displaystyle\mathsf{TV}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in[n]}\mathcal{Q}(t,\circ,\circ)\cdot\mathsf{TV}(\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,\cdot),\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(t,\cdot,\cdot))

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{10}+\sum_{t\in S}\mathcal{Q}(t,\circ,\circ)\cdot\mathsf{TV}(\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,\cdot),\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(t,\cdot,\cdot))

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{8}.

The first transition uses (
36
) and the second transition uses the fact that

\mathsf{TV}(\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,\cdot),\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(t,\cdot,\cdot))=\mathsf{TV}(\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,\cdot),\Delta_{t})\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{40}

for all
t\in S
by
Lemma

7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
be the dPCP obtained by taking
\mathcal{D}
and changing its complete decoding distribution to
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. Note that the length, projection decision complexity, and decoding complexity are unchanged. For the decoding degrees, note that each
a
has decoding degree either
0
or
1
now. Completeness is preserved by the fact that
\supp(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})\subseteq\supp(\mathcal{Q})
, stated above, and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
still has
(L,9\varepsilon/8)
-list decoding soundness by the fact
\mathsf{TV}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})\leqslant\varepsilon/8
, stated above.

Let us now write
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,a,b)=\frac{w(a,b)}{n\cdot M}=\frac{d^{\prime}w(a,b)}{d^{\prime}\cdot n\cdot M}
for
w(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}
. In expressing
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
this way, we are using the fact that
a
has decoding degree
1
. Here, we multiplied both the numerator and denominator by
d^{\prime}
to ensure that all of the nonzero numerators are at least
d^{\prime}
, in accordance with
Lemma

7.2
.

We now apply
Lemma

7.2
on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
where its complete decoding distribution is written with denominator
d^{\prime}nM
, and the weight of each edge
(a,b)
is
d^{\prime}w(a,b)
. Call the resulting dPCP
\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}
and let
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}
be its complete decoding distribution. The lemma follows by checking the guarantees from
Lemma

7.2
. In particular, the length, projection decision complexity, decoding complexity, perfect completeness, and list-decoding soundness are immediate.

For the guarantee on the complete decoding distribution, we first note that by
Lemma

7.2
,
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}
has uniform marginal over the right side and is
d^{\prime 2}nM
discrete. This also implies that

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}(\circ,\circ,b)=\frac{d^{\prime}}{d^{\prime 2}\cdot n\cdot M}.

For the remaining guarantee on the complete decoding distribution, fix a left vertex
a
. We start by using the third part of the decoding distribution guarantee from
Lemma

7.2
to get:

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}(\circ,a,\circ)=\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,a,\circ)=\sum_{b\in B}\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,a,b).

If
t_{a}\notin S
, then
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}(\circ,a,\circ)=\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,a,\circ)\leqslant\frac{1}{nM}+\frac{\varepsilon}{10\mathsf{ld}|A|}
by the third item above and we are done. Otherwise,

|\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,\circ)-\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}(\circ,a,\circ)|=\left|\sum_{b\in B}\left(\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,b)-\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\circ,a,b)\right)\right|\leqslant\frac{\mathsf{ld}}{nM},

where in the last transition we use the fact that the summation has
\mathsf{ld}
many nonzero terms and each is at most
\frac{1}{nM}
by the third item listed above. The above implies,

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}(\circ,a,\circ)\leqslant\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,\circ)+\frac{\mathsf{ld}}{nM}.

Finally, to get decoding degree
1
, note that each vertex
a\in A
has decoding degree either
1
or
0
, and we can delete every vertex with decoding degree
0
without affecting any of the previous guarantees.
∎

8
Decoding Degree Reduction

In this section we show how to reduce the decoding degree of a dPCP assuming that its complete decoding distribution is agnostic. After performing this operation once, we will preserve constant decoding degree throughout the remainder of our construction.
At a high level, after
Theorem

6.1
the decoding degree is
{\sf poly}(\log(N))
, and hence holding this many
\Sigma_{0}
-symbols itself already causes superpolynomial alphabet size. Therefore any method of reducing alphabet size to a constant must first reduce decoding degree to a constant. This is a non-issue in standard PCPs (as there is no decoding going on), and partly the reason earlier composition techniques did not apply to dPCPs.

The statement of our decoding degree transformation is as follows.

Lemma 8.1
.

Suppose
\Sigma_{0}
is an alphabet and that the language
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\Sigma_{0}^{n}
has a projection dPCP

\mathcal{D}=(A\cup B,E,\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{\Phi_{e}\}_{e\in E},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]})

with perfect completeness,
(L,\varepsilon)
-list-decoding soundness for
L:={\sf poly}(1/\varepsilon)
, agnostic complete decoding distribution, projection decision complexity
\mathsf{ProjComp}
, decoding complexity
\mathsf{DecodeComp}
, and left degree, decoding degree, right degree that are at most
\mathsf{ld},\mathsf{ldd},d
respectively.
Let
\mathcal{I}=\{(t,b)~|~\exists a\in A,(a,b)\in\supp(\mathcal{P}_{t})\}
, and let
\{C_{t,b}\in\mathbb{N}\;|(t,b)\in\mathcal{I}\}
be a collection of integers. Then
\mathcal{L}
has a projection dPCP

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=(B^{\prime}\cup A,\Sigma_{A}^{d}\times\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{A},E^{\prime},\{\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\prime}\}_{t\in[n]},\{\Phi_{e}^{\prime}\}_{e\in E^{\prime}},\{D_{t}^{\prime}\}_{t\in[n]}),

satisfying the following properties:

•

Length.

B^{\prime}=\bigcup_{(t,b)\in\mathcal{I}}\{(t,b,i)~|~i\in[C_{t,b}]\}
, and in
particular the length is
|A|+\sum_{(t,b)\in\mathcal{I}}C_{t,b}
.

•

Degrees.
The left degree of every vertex is at most
d
, the right degree of every vertex is at most
{\sf ld}\cdot{\sf ldd}\cdot\max_{(t,b)\in\mathcal{I}}C_{t,b}
, and the decoding degree of every vertex is
1
. If
\mathcal{D}
is
d
-right regular, then
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is
d
-left regular.

•

Projection Decision Complexity.

O(d\cdot(\mathsf{ProjComp}+\mathsf{DecodeComp}))
.

•

Decoding Complexity.

O(d\log(|\Sigma_{A}|)+\log(|\Sigma_{0}|))
.

•

Decoding Distributions.
For each
(t,b,i)\in B^{\prime}
and
a\in A
,
t
is the unique index in the decoding neighborhood of
(t,b,i)
and we have

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}((t,b,i),\circ)=\frac{\mathcal{P}_{t}(\circ,b)}{C_{t,b}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(t,(t,b,i),a)=\frac{\mathcal{Q}(t,b,a)}{C_{t,b}},

where
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
are the complete decoding distributions of
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
respectively.

•

Perfect Completeness.

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
has perfect completeness.

•

List-Decoding Soundness.

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
has
(L\cdot{\sf poly}(1/\varepsilon),2\varepsilon^{1/5})
-list-decoding soundness.

Proof.

Let

\mathcal{D}=\left(A\cup B,\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},E,\{\Phi_{e}\}_{e\in E},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right)

be a dPCP for the language
\mathcal{L}
as in the premise of the lemma. Denote its complete decoding distribution by
\mathcal{Q}
, namely a sample is obtained by choosing
t\in[n]
uniformly,
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}
, and outputting
(t,a,b)
.

We denote the new dPCP for
\mathcal{L}
by

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\left(B^{\prime}\cup A,\Sigma_{A}^{d}\times\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{A},E^{\prime},\{\Phi_{e}^{\prime}\}_{e\in E^{\prime}},\{D_{t}^{\prime}\}_{t\in[n]},\{\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\prime}\}_{t\in[n]}\right),

and define its components in the following way. Below, we use
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{{\sf dec}}
to denote the neighborhood and decoding neighborhood functions in
\mathcal{D}
respectively, and use
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Gamma_{{\sf dec}}^{\prime}
to denote the neighborhood and decoding neighborhood functions in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
respectively.

•

Constraint Graph.
The constraint graph is as follows:

–

The left side
B^{\prime}=\bigcup_{(t,b)\in\mathcal{I}}\{(t,b,i)~|~i\in[C_{t,b}]\}
.

–

The right side is
A
.

–

The edge set
E^{\prime}
consist of all
((t,b,i),a)
such that
(a,b)\in E
.

•

Alphabets.
The alphabets are as follows.

–

The left alphabet is
\Sigma_{B^{\prime}}\subseteq\Sigma_{A}^{d}\times\Sigma_{0}
. For a fixed
(t,b,i)\in B^{\prime}
, we think of a label to it as assigning a
\Sigma_{A}
label for each
a\in\Gamma(b)
and a
\Sigma_{0}
label for
t
. Furthermore, we hardcode constraints on the alphabet of
(t,b,i)\in B^{\prime}
as follows. Writing
\Gamma(b)=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}\}
, for a label
\sigma\in\Sigma_{A}^{d}\times\Sigma_{0}
to
(t,b,i)
we denote by
\sigma_{a_{j}}
be the label assigned to
a_{j}
in
\sigma
, and by
\sigma_{t}
the label for
t
in
\sigma
. We denote by
\sigma^{(b)}_{a_{j}}
the unique value in
\Sigma_{B}
satisfying
\Phi_{(a_{j},b)}(\sigma_{a_{j}},\sigma^{(b)}_{a_{j}})=1
(recall that there is a unique such element in
\Sigma_{B}
since the constraints in
\mathcal{D}
are projections). Then, we require that
D_{t}(a_{j},\sigma_{a_{j}})=\sigma_{t}
for all
i\in[d]
, and that
\sigma^{(b)}_{a_{j}}
is the same for all
j\in[d]
.

–

The right alphabet is
\Sigma_{A}
.

•

Constraints.
For each edge
((t,b,i),a)\in E^{\prime}
, the constraint
\Phi^{\prime}_{((t,b,i),a)}:\Sigma_{B^{\prime}}\times\Sigma_{A}\to\{0,1\}
is defined as follows. Given labels
\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{A}
, let
\sigma_{a}
be the label for
a
in
\sigma
. The constraint
\Phi^{\prime}_{((t,b,i),a)}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})
is satisfied if and only if
\sigma_{a}=\sigma^{\prime}
.

•

Decoders.
For each
t\in[n]
, the decoder
D_{t}^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\times\Sigma_{B}\to\Sigma_{0}
is defined as follows. For
(t,b,i)\in B^{\prime}
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{B^{\prime}}
label for it, the decoder
D_{t}^{\prime}((t,b,i),\sigma)
outputs
\sigma_{t}
, the label for
t
in
\sigma
.

We note that by the constraints on the left alphabet symbol in
\mathcal{D}
, for any
a\in\Gamma(b)
, letting
\sigma_{a}
be the label for
a
in
\sigma
, we have that
D_{t}(a,\sigma_{a})=\sigma_{t}=D^{\prime}_{t}((t,b,i),\sigma)
.

•

Decoding Distributions.
For each
t\in[n]
, the decoding distribution
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\prime}
is defined as follows. To sample from
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\prime}
, sample
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}
,
i\in[C_{t,b}]
uniformly, and then output
((t,b,i),a)
. Since we think of the side-decoding distribution as uniform over
[n]
, the complete decoding distribution, denoted
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is obtained by sampling
t\in[n]
uniformly and then
((t,b,i),a)\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}
. We remark that the complete decoding distribution
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
can also be obtained by choosing
(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}
and then choosing
i\in[C_{t,b}]
uniformly and outputting
(t,(t,b,i),a)
.

Let us now analyze the parameters of the new dPCP
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. As usual, the main difficulty is in analyzing the list-decoding soundness.

Length.

It is clear that
|B^{\prime}|=\sum_{(t,b)\in\mathcal{I}}C_{t,b}
and hence the length is as described.

Degrees.

It is clear that the degree of every vertex in
B^{\prime}
is at most
d
, as for each
(t,b,i)\in B^{\prime}
,
\Gamma^{\prime}((t,b,i))=\Gamma(b)
, and in the case that
\mathcal{D}
is right-regular, it is clear that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is left-regular. As for the degrees of each
a\in A
, it is clear that its degree is at most
{\sf ld}\cdot{\sf ldd}\cdot\max_{(t,b)\in\mathcal{I}}C_{t,b}
as desired. Finally, the decoding degree of every vertex in
B^{\prime}
is
1
, as one can see that each
(t,b,i)\in B^{\prime}
is only responsible for decoding
t
.

Projection Decision Complexity.

Fix a constraint
((t,b,i),a)
and let
\sigma
be the symbols assigned to the
(t,b,i)
. The constraint must check that
\sigma
is a valid alphabet symbol. After that, the unique symbol to
a
that would satisfy the constraint can be found by taking a restriction in
\sigma
, and this has circuit complexity at most
\mathsf{ProjComp}
. To check that
\sigma
is valid, the projection circuit must compute one projection from
\mathcal{D}
and one decoding for each neighbor of
b
’s
d
neighbors and perform
d
equality checks. Overall, this shows the projection decision complexity is
O(d\cdot(\mathsf{ProjComp}+\mathsf{DecodeComp}))
.

Decoding Complexity.

The decoder outputs the restriction to a single symbol from an alphabet of size
|\Sigma_{A}|^{d}\cdot|\Sigma_{0}|
and hence the decoding complexity is
O(d\log|\Sigma_{A}|+\log|\Sigma_{0}|)
.

Perfect Completeness.

Fix any
w\in\mathcal{L}
. As
\mathcal{D}
has perfect completeness, there are assignments
T_{1}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
and
T_{2}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
which satisfy all of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}
and decode to
w_{t}
with probability
1
under
\mathcal{P}_{t}
for every
t\in[n]
. Then we construct assignments
T^{\prime}_{1}:B^{\prime}\to\Sigma_{A}^{d}\times\Sigma_{0}
and
T^{\prime}_{2}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
in the natural way as follows. Set
T^{\prime}_{2}=T_{1}
. For each
(t,b,i)\in B^{\prime}
write
\Gamma(b)=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}\}
, and take
T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]=(T_{1}[a_{1}],\ldots,T_{1}[a_{d}],D_{t}(a_{1},T_{1}[a_{1}])
. In words, this is the symbol in
\Sigma_{A}^{d}\times\Sigma_{0}
which contains
T_{1}[a_{i}]
as the label for
a_{i}
for all
i\in[d]
, and
D_{t}(a_{i},T_{1}[a_{i}])
as the label for
t
(we note that by the hardcoded constraints it does not matter which
a_{i}
we pick). Note that by the fact that
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
satisfy all of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}
and always decode to
w_{t}
under
\mathcal{P}_{t}
, this assignment indeed satisfies the constraints on the left alphabet and is a valid assignment. It is straightforward to verify that these assignments satisfy all of the constraints in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and decode to
w_{t}
with probability
1
under
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}
for every
t\in[n]
, so
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
has perfect completeness.

List-Decoding Soundness.

Fix any left assignment
T^{\prime}_{1}:B^{\prime}\to\Sigma_{A}^{d}\times\Sigma_{0}
.
Based on
T_{1}^{\prime}
, we construct a candidate list of words from
\mathcal{L}
and show that it satisfies the list-decoding soundness. Recall that for each
(t,b,i)\in B^{\prime}
and
a\in A
adjacent to it in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, the assignment
T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]
contains a symbol for
a
, which we denote by
T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{a}
. For each
a\in A
, let
{\sf cand}(a)
consist of all symbols
\sigma\in\Sigma_{A}
such that

\Pr_{(t,(t,b,i))\sim\mathcal{Q^{\prime}}(\cdot,a,\cdot)}[T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{a}=\sigma]=\Pr_{(t,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}(\cdot,a,\cdot),i\in[C_{t,b}]}[T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{a}=\sigma]\geqslant\varepsilon^{1/5}.

where the first equality is by the observation regarding how to generate
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{Q}
made in the definition of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
above.

Clearly,
|{\sf cand}(a)|\leqslant\varepsilon^{-1/5}:=J
for every
a\in A
. Therefore, one can find assignments
T_{1,1},\ldots,T_{1,J}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
such that for every
a\in A
and every
\sigma\in{\sf cand}(a)
there is some
j\in[J]
such that
T_{1,j}[a]=\sigma
. Since each
T_{1,j}
is a left assignment to
\mathcal{D}
, we can apply the list-decoding soundness of
\mathcal{D}
to it. Specifically, for each
j\in[J]
, let
{\sf List}_{j}=\{w_{j,1},\ldots,w_{j,L}\}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
be the list guaranteed by the
(L,\varepsilon)
-list-decoding soundness of
\mathcal{D}
for the left assignment
T_{1,j}
. Then by the list-decoding soundness of
\mathcal{D}
we conclude that for any right assignment
T_{2}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
and any
j\in[J]
, we have

\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{1,j}[a],T_{2}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{1,j}[a])\notin\{w_{j,1}[t],\ldots,w_{j,L}[t]\}]\leqslant\varepsilon.

(37)

We show that
{\sf List}=\bigcup_{j\in[J]}{\sf List}_{j}
satisfies the list-decoding soundness condition of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
with size
J\cdot L=L\cdot{\sf poly}(1/\varepsilon)
and error
2\varepsilon^{1/5}
. The size is clear, and next we show that for any right assignment
T^{\prime}_{2}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
, the list-decoding error of
T_{1}^{\prime}
,
T_{2}^{\prime}
relative to
{\sf List}
is small. We define the following events:

•

Let
\mathcal{E}_{(t,b,i),a}
be the event that the constraint on
((t,b,i),a)
is satisfied by assignments
T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]
and
T^{\prime}_{2}[a]
.

•

Let
\mathcal{F}_{j,(t,b,i),a}
be the event that
D^{\prime}_{t}((t,b,i),T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)])\notin\{(w_{j,1})_{t},\ldots,(w_{j,L})_{t}\}
.

It is clear that the list-decoding error can be expressed and bounded as follows

\begin{split}&\Pr_{(t,(t,b,i),a)\sim\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{(t,b,i),a}\bigwedge\left(\bigwedge_{j\in[J]}\mathcal{F}_{j,(t,b,i),a}\right)\right]\\
&=\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q},i\in[C_{t,b}]\end{subarray}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{(t,b,i),a}\bigwedge\left(\bigwedge_{j\in[J]}\mathcal{F}_{j,(t,b,i),a}\right)\right]\\
&=\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q},i\in[C_{t,b}]}\left[\mathcal{E}_{(t,b,i),a}\bigwedge\left(\bigwedge_{j\in[J]}\mathcal{F}_{j,(t,b,i),a}\right)\land\left(T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{a}\in{\sf cand}(a)\right)\right]\\
&+\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q},i\in[C_{t,b}]}\left[\mathcal{E}_{(t,b,i),a}\bigwedge\left(\bigwedge_{j\in[J]}\mathcal{F}_{j,(t,b,i),a}\right)\land\left(T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{a}\notin{\sf cand}(a)\right)\right]\\
&\leqslant\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q},i\in[C_{t,b}]}\left[\mathcal{E}_{(t,b,i),a}\bigwedge\left(\bigwedge_{j\in[J]}\mathcal{F}_{j,(t,b,i),a}\right)\land\left(T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{a}\in{\sf cand}(a)\right)\right]\\
&+\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q},i\in[C_{t,b}]}\left[\mathcal{E}_{(t,b,i),a}\land\left(T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{a}\notin{\sf cand}(a)\right)\right]\\
&\leqslant\sum_{j\in[J]}\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q},i\in[C_{t,b}]}[\mathcal{E}_{(t,b,i),a}\land\mathcal{F}_{j,(t,b,i),a}\land\left(T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{a}=T_{1,j}[a]\right)]\\
&+\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q},i\in[C_{t,b}]}\left[\mathcal{E}_{(t,b,i),a}\land\left(T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{a}\notin{\sf cand}(a)\right)\right]\end{split}

(38)

In the last transition we used the union bound, and we next bound the terms on the right hand side. We upper bound the second term on the right hand side of (
38
) as

\begin{split}&\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q},i\in[C_{t,b}]}\left[\mathcal{E}_{(t,b,i),a}\land\left(T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{a}\notin{\sf cand}(a)\right)\right]\\
&\leqslant\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{a}\left[1_{T_{2}^{\prime}[a]\not\in{\sf cand}(a)}\Pr_{(t,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}(\cdot,a,\cdot),i\in[C_{t,b}]}[T_{1}^{\prime}[(t,b,i)]_{a}=T_{2}^{\prime}[a]]\right]\\
&\leqslant\varepsilon^{1/5},\end{split}

(39)

where in the last inequality we used the definition of
{\sf cand}(a)
.

We now go on to bound the first term on the right hand side of (
38
). We bound the summand corresponding to each
j\in[J]
separately, and we fix a
j
henceforth. Define

•

\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{j,(t,b,i),a}
be the event
\mathcal{E}_{(t,b,i),a}\land\mathcal{F}_{j,(t,b,i),a}\land\left(T_{1,j}[a]=T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{a}\right)
,

so our task is now to upper bound
\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q},i\in[C_{t,b}]}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{j,(t,b,i),a}]
. For any constraint of
\mathcal{D}
, say
(a,b)\in E
, and label
\sigma
for
a
, we denote by
\sigma_{\to b}
the unique label for
b
such that
\Phi_{(a,b)}(\sigma,\sigma_{\to b})=1
. Here, the uniqueness follows since
\mathcal{D}
is a projection dPCP. For each
(t,b,i)\in B^{\prime}
, let
T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}
be the label deduced for
b
in
T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]
. In particular, by the hardcoded alphabet constraints we have that
T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}=\left(T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{a}\right)_{\to b}
for any
a\in\Gamma(b)
.

We say a symbol
\sigma\in\Sigma_{B}
is
b
-heavy if

\Pr_{(t,a)\sim\mathcal{Q}(\cdot,\cdot,b)}[T_{1,j}[a]_{\to b}=\sigma]\geqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

(40)

It is clear that for every
b\in B
, there are at most
J^{\prime}=1/\sqrt{\varepsilon}
many
b
-heavy symbols, hence we can find tables
H_{1},\ldots,H_{J^{\prime}}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
such that for every
b
-heavy symbol
\sigma
, there is some
j^{\prime}\in[J^{\prime}]
such that
H_{j^{\prime}}[b]=\sigma
. Thus, we may write

\begin{split}\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{j,(t,b,i),a}]&=\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{j,(t,b,i),a}\land(T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}\text{ is }b\text{-heavy})]\\
&+\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{j,(t,b,i),a}\land(T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}\text{ is not }b\text{-heavy})].\end{split}

(41)

To bound the first probability, notice that if
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{j,(t,b,i),a}\land(T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}\text{ is }b\text{-heavy})
occurs, then for some
j^{\prime}\in[J^{\prime}]
, both events below occur:

•

the assignments to
a
and
b
given by
T_{1,j}[a]
and
H_{j^{\prime}}[b]
respectively satisfy the constraint
\Phi_{(a,b)}
,

•

D_{t}(a,T_{1,j}[a])\notin\{(w_{j,1})_{t},\ldots,(w_{j,L})_{t}\}
.

The first item requires unpacking the event
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{j,(t,b,i),a}
. Indeed, if this event holds then
T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{a}=T_{1,j}[a]
, which implies
T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}=T_{1,j}[a]|_{\to b}
. Hence,
T_{1,j}[a]|_{\to b}\in\{H_{1}[b],\ldots,H_{J^{\prime}}[b]\}
because
T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}
is heavy. Now, to bound the first probability in (
41
), notice that for a fixed
j^{\prime}\in[J^{\prime}]
, the above two items occur simultaneously with probability at most
\varepsilon
. Indeed, this follows by (
37
) applied to
T_{1,j}
and
H_{j^{\prime}}
. Union bounding over all
j^{\prime}\in[J^{\prime}]
gives that

\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{j,(t,b,i),a}\land(T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}\text{ is }b\text{-heavy})]\leqslant J^{\prime}\cdot\varepsilon.

We move on to bounding the second probability in (
41
), and here we will use the agnostic property of
\mathcal{Q}
. We have

\begin{split}&\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q},i\in[C_{t,b}]}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{j,(t,b,i),a}\land(T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}\text{ is not }b\text{-heavy})]\\
&\leqslant\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q},i\in[C_{t,b}]}[T_{1,j}[a]_{\to b}=T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}\land T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}\text{ is not }b\text{-heavy}]\\
&=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(t,b)\sim\mathcal{Q},i\in[C_{t,b}]}\left[1_{T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}\text{ is not }b\text{-heavy}}\Pr_{a\sim\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,b)}[T_{1,j}[a]_{\to b}=T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}]\right]\\
&=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(t,b)\sim\mathcal{Q},i\in[C_{t,b}]}\left[1_{T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}\text{ is not }b\text{-heavy}}\Pr_{a\sim\mathcal{Q}(\cdot,\cdot,b)}[T_{1,j}[a]_{\to b}=T^{\prime}_{1}[(t,b,i)]_{\to b}]\right]\\
&\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon}.\end{split}

(42)

The first transition is by definition of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{j,(t,b,i),a}
, the second transition is clear, the third transition uses the agnostic property of
\mathcal{Q}
, and the last transition uses
the fact that the distribution of
a
in the probability of the fourth line is the same as the distribution of
a
in (
40
) and hence the probability in the expectation of the fourth line is always at most
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
by definition of not being
b
-heavy.

Altogether, this yields

\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{j,(t,b,i),a}]\leqslant J^{\prime}\cdot\varepsilon+\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

Hence, going back to the list-decoding error expression in (
38
), we get that each term in the summation of the last expression is at most
J^{\prime}\cdot\varepsilon+\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, and combined with (
39
) we get that the list-decoding error is at most

J\cdot(J^{\prime}\cdot\varepsilon+\sqrt{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon^{1/5}\leqslant 2\varepsilon^{1/5}.

Hence
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
has
(J\cdot L,2\varepsilon^{1/5})
-list-decoding soundness, where
J\cdot L=L\cdot{\sf poly}(1/\varepsilon)
.
∎

8.1
An Inner dPCP

Now we describe how to find a version of our dPCP that will be used as the inner dPCP for composition. Below, we highlight a few technical properties of this inner PCP construction which will be used later on in the composition step.

•

The decoding degree is
1
.

•

There is some integer
J
such that for every pair of vertices
(a,b)
, the weight given by the complete decoding distribution can be written as

\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,b)=\frac{w(a,b)}{J}
for some integer
w(a,b)
. Furthermore, for each
a
, we have that the sum
\sum_{b\in B}w(a,b)
is the same. This latter condition also implies that the marginal of the complete decoding distribution over the left vertices is uniform.

We will obtain these properties by using
Lemma

8.1
, which gives a dPCP with decoding degree
1
, thereby establishing the first item above. To achieve the second item, we carefully choose the parameters
C_{t,b}

so as to adjust the complete decoding distribution, following an approach similar to that of
Lemma

7.3
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Suppose the language
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\Sigma_{0}^{n}
has a projection dPCP

\mathcal{D}=(A\cup B,E,\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{\Phi_{e}\}_{e\in E},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]})

with perfect completeness,
(L,\varepsilon)
-list-decoding soundness, agnostic complete decoding distribution, projection decision complexity
\mathsf{ProjComp}
, decoding complexity
\mathsf{DecodeComp}
, and left degree, decoding degree, right degree that are at most
\mathsf{ld},\mathsf{ldd},d
respectively.
Then for every integer
M\geqslant|B|
,
\mathcal{L}
has a projection dPCP

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=(Z\cup A,E^{\prime},\Sigma^{d}_{A}\times\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{A},\{\Phi^{\prime}_{e}\}_{e\in E^{\prime}},\{D^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]})

which satisfies the following properties:

•

Length.
The left side has the form
Z=[n]\times[M]
, so the length is
n\cdot M+|A|
.

•

Degrees.
The decoding degree is
1
and the left degrees are at most
d
. Furthermore if
\mathcal{D}
is right-regular, then
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is left regular.

•

Projection Decision Complexity.

O(d\cdot(\mathsf{ProjComp}+\mathsf{DecodeComp}))
.

•

Decoding Complexity.

O(d\log|\Sigma_{A}|+\log|\Sigma_{0}|)
.

•

Decoding Distribution.
For each
t\in[n]
, the marginal of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{t}
over the left vertices,
Z
, is uniform in
\{t\}\times[M]
.

•

Completeness.
The dPCP has perfect completeness.

•

List-Decoding Soundness.

\left(L\cdot{\sf poly}(1/\varepsilon),2\varepsilon^{1/5}+\frac{|B|}{M}\right)
-list-decoding soundness.

Proof.

Fix
M\geqslant|B|
and take
\mathcal{D}
to be the dPCP from the lemma statement. We start by using
Lemma

7.3
to obtain a discretized version of the marginal of
\mathcal{P}_{t}
over
B
for each
t\in[n]
. We apply
Lemma

7.3
with denominator there set to
M
for each
t\in[n]
, which yields the following. For each
t\in[n]
, there are nonnegative integers
z^{\prime}_{t,b}
for each
b\in B
such that
M:=\sum_{b\in B}z^{\prime}_{t,b}
, and
