For every
b\in B
,
\sum_{a\in A}\mathsf{wt}(a,b)=d
.

–

For every
a\in A
,
\sum_{b\in B}\mathsf{wt}(a,b)=k
.

•

Completeness.
The dPCP has perfect completeness.

•

Soundness.
The dPCP has
({\sf poly}(1/\varepsilon),\varepsilon)
-list-decoding soundness.

Proof.

Let us start by recalling the parameters of our Outer dPCP and inner dPCP in
Table

1
and
Table

2
. Ultimately we will compose two times, where both times the inner dPCP is taken from
Table

2
(although instantiated on different languages and hence different language-related parameters
N^{\prime},n^{\prime},T^{\prime}
). Throughout all tables in this section, we abbreviate complete decoding distribution as CDD. Also, once we specify that a complete decoding distribution is
M
-discrete and can be written as
\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,b)=\mathsf{wt}(a,b)/M
, we refer to the left weighted degrees as the sum
\sum_{b\in B}\mathsf{wt}(a,b)
for each left vertex
a
, and the right weighted degree as the sum
\sum_{a\in A}\mathsf{wt}(a,b)
for each right vertex
b
. Viewing the bipartite constraint graph as having
\mathsf{wt}(a,b)
edges between
a
and
b
for each pair of vertices
a
and
b
, these quantities indeed correspond to degrees in this graph. Finally, all of the dPCPs in this section have perfect completeness, and all of the transformations we use preserve perfect completeness, so we do not include perfect completeness in any of the tables.

Henceforth, fix the circuit
\varphi:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
from the theorem statement. We will construct a dPCP for
{\sf SAT}(\varphi)
.

Parameter

Outer dPCP:
\mathcal{D}_{1}

Length

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(N\cdot{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))

Left alphabet size

2^{O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))}

Right alphabet size

O_{\varepsilon}(1)

Decoding Degree

O_{\varepsilon}(1)

Projection Decision Complexity

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))

Decoding Complexity

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))

List-Decoding Soundness

({\sf poly}(1/\varepsilon),\varepsilon)

CDD Marginal over Edges

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(N{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
-discrete

Left Weighted Degree

all at most
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))

Right Weighted Degree

all equal and
O_{\varepsilon}(1)

applied to the language
{\sf SAT}(\varphi)

with list-decoding soundness error
\varepsilon
.

O_{T^{\prime}}(N^{\prime 2}\cdot{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N^{\prime}))

for

2^{O_{T^{\prime}}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N^{\prime}))}

Right alphabet size

O_{\varepsilon}(1)

Decoding Degree

O_{\varepsilon}(1)

O_{T^{\prime}}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N^{\prime}))

-discrete

(so that
K(T^{\prime},N^{\prime})
from

is
O_{T^{\prime}}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N^{\prime}))
)

all equal and
O_{T^{\prime}}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N^{\prime}))

applied to the language

for some circuit

of size at most

over an alphabet of size

with list-decoding soundness error
\varepsilon
.

First Composition.

We apply the composition of
Theorem

9.1
with outer dPCP
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and with inner dPCPs given
Theorem

8.3
. Referring to the parameters from

and

, one can check that we get a dPCP, called
\mathcal{D}_{3}
, with parameters as described in

. In particular, the parameters

are
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
and

respectively.

Table 3:

\mathcal{D}_{1}
and

. In

and

are

and

respectively.

Let
A_{3}\cup B_{3}
be the sides of the constraint graph, and call
\mathcal{Q}_{3}
the complete decoding distribution of
\mathcal{D}_{3}
. By the discreteness of the marginal of
\mathcal{Q}_{3}
over the edges, we can thus write, for every
(a,b)\in A_{3}\times B_{3}

.

The alphabet size is now significantly smaller but is still super-constant. We would like to compose again to further reduce the alphabet size, but we note that now
\mathcal{D}_{3}
does not satisfy all of the requirements of the outer dPCP in
Theorem

9.1
. In particular,
Theorem

9.1
requires the outer dPCP’s complete decoding distribution to have uniform marginal over the right vertices — a property that we do not have in
Table

3
.

To fix this, we set
d^{\prime}=O(1/\varepsilon^{100})
, multiply all of the weights by
d^{\prime}
, and then apply
Lemma

7.2
with degree
d^{\prime}
. We let the resulting dPCP be
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{3}
, and let its constraint graph have sides
A_{3}\cup B^{\prime}_{3}
with complete decoding distribution
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{3}
. It is straightforward to check that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{3}
has all of the same parameters as
\mathcal{D}_{3}
in
Table

3
, except for the following changes

•

The length becomes
d^{\prime}J
, where
J=O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(N{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
is the discreteness parameter of the complete decoding distribution’s marginal over the edges.

•

The discreteness parameter of the CDD Marginal over edges is multiplied by
d^{\prime}
and is still
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(N\cdot{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
.

•

The right weighted degrees are all
d^{\prime}=O_{\varepsilon}(1)
.

•

The left weighted degrees are all multiplied by
d^{\prime}
and are still all equal to some
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log\log N))
.

Now we can compose
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{3}
with the inner dPCP from
Theorem

8.3
one more time using
Theorem

9.1
and again apply
Lemma

7.2
with degree
d^{\prime}
on the resulting dPCP. This time, the inner dPCPs are applied with parameters
N^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}
that are
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log\log N))
and
O_{\varepsilon,|\Sigma_{0}|}(1)
. Altogether, we obtain the dPCP,
\mathcal{D}_{4}
, whose parameters are listed in
Table

4
. We note that, similarly to in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{3}
, all of the parameters except for weighted right degrees come from
Theorem

9.1
. The weighted right degrees from
Table

4
are obtained by applying
Lemma

7.2
which changes the length to
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
times the discreteness parameter of the complete decoding distribution’s marginal over the edges, and increases all of the other parameters by an
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
factor which is absorbed.
∎

-discrete

and

. In

and

are

and

10.2
The Final dPCP with Constant Alphabet Size

Now we are finally ready to prove our main dPCP theorem,
Theorem

3.12
, which we restate below for convenience.

Theorem 10.2
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
C\in\mathbb{N}
and
L={\sf poly}(1/\varepsilon)
such that the following holds for all size
N
circuits
\varphi\colon\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
over some alphabet
\Sigma_{0}
. The language
{\sf SAT}(\varphi)
has a
2
-query, poly-time constructible dPCP with the following properties

•

Length.

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}\left(N\log^{C}N\right)
,

•

Alphabet Size.

O_{\varepsilon,|\Sigma_{0}|}(1)
,

•

Complete Decoding Distribution.
The marginal of the complete decoding distribution over the right vertices is uniform and the marginal of the complete decoding distribution over the left vertices is uniform.

•

Completeness.
The dPCP has perfect completeness,

•

Soundness.

(L,2\varepsilon^{1/5})
-list-decoding soundness.

Theorem

10.2
is obtained from
Theorem

10.1
by performing yet
another composition step. This time, though,
we will compose with a Hadamard-code based
dPCP construction, which has a constant size
alphabet (but an exponential length). Starting from
\mathcal{D}_{4}
, however, this exponential size dPCP is applied to a circuit of size
{\sf poly}(\log\log\log N)
, hence the overall length increase is only a factor of
{\sf poly}(\log(N))
, which is affordable to us.
Below we state the properties of the Hadamard dPCPs, which can be found in slightly different forms in
[
28
,
8
]
.

Theorem 10.3

(Hadamard dPCP)
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
there exist
L={\sf poly}(1/\varepsilon)
and functions
M,K:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
satisfying

M(T,N)=2^{O_{\varepsilon,T}(N^{2})}\quad\text{and}\quad K(T,N)=O_{\varepsilon}(1)

such that the following holds. For every alphabet
\Sigma_{0}
of size at most
T
and circuit
\varphi:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
of size at most
N
, the language
{\sf SAT}(\varphi)
has a projection dPCP of the form

\mathcal{D}=\left(\left([n]\times[M]\right)\cup B,E,\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{\Phi_{e}\}_{e\in E},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right)

which satisfies the following with
M:=M(T,N)
and
K:=K(T,N)
.

•

Length.

O(nM)
.

•

Alphabet Sizes.
The left alphabet size is
O_{\varepsilon,T}(1)
and the right alphabet size is
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
.

•

Degrees.

\mathcal{D}
has decoding degree
1
.

•

Projection Decision Complexity.

O_{\varepsilon,T}(1)
.

•

Decoding Complexity.

O_{\varepsilon,T}(1)
.

•

Decoding Distribution.
Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the complete decoding distribution. Then,
\mathcal{Q}
satisfies the following:

–

For each
(t,r)\in[n]\times[M]
and
b\in B
we can write
\mathcal{Q}(\circ,(t,r),b)=\frac{\mathsf{wt}((t,r),b)}{nMK}
for some
\mathsf{wt}((t,r),b)\in\mathbb{N}
.

–

For each
t\in[n]
, the distribution
\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot,\circ)
is uniform over
\{t\}\times[M]
and hence for every
(t,r)\in[n]\times[M]
,

\sum_{b\in B}w((t,r),b)=K.

•

Completeness.
The dPCP has perfect completeness.

•

Soundness.
The dPCP has
(L,2\varepsilon^{1/5})
-list-decoding soundness.

The proof appears in
Appendix

A
. On a high-level, the construction is identical to the one in
[
8
, Theorem 7.7]
, with minor additional considerations in the analysis.

We can now complete the proof of
Theorem

10.2
by composing
\mathcal{D}_{4}
with the Hadamard dPCP from
Theorem

10.3
which we denote by
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{Had}}
.

Proof of
Theorem

10.2
.

We use
Theorem

9.1
with
\mathcal{D}_{4}
as the outer dPCP, and the Hadamard based dPCP
\mathcal{D}_{\sf Had}
from
Theorem

10.3
as the inner dPCP. When applying
\mathcal{D}_{\sf Had}
, the parameters
N^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}
are
{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log\log\log N)
and
O_{\varepsilon,|\Sigma_{0}|}(1)
respectively. We also have to apply
Lemma

7.2
again on the resulting dPCP to obtain regularity for the right weighted degrees (and hence uniformity of the marginal of the complete decoding distribution over the right vertices). We call the resulting dPCP
\mathcal{D}_{5}
and list its parameters in
Table

5
. Here, we remark that it is important that the dPCP given by
Theorem

9.1
has complete decoding distribution whose marginal over the edges is
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}\left(N\cdot{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)\right)
-discrete, so the length blowup from applying
Lemma

7.2
is controlled. Similar to in the previous two compositions, all of the parameters in
Table

5
are given by
Theorem

9.1
, except for the weighted right degrees. These are obtained by applying
Lemma

7.2
which changes the length to
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
times the discreteness parameter of the complete decoding distribution’s marginal over the edges, and increases all of the other parameters by an
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
factor which is absorbed.
∎

Parameter

Composed dPCP:
\mathcal{D}_{5}

Length

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}\left(N\cdot{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)\right)

Left alphabet size

O_{\varepsilon,|\Sigma_{0}|}(1)

Right alphabet size

O_{\varepsilon}(1)

List-decoding soundness

({\sf poly}(1/\varepsilon),\varepsilon)

CDD Marginal over Edges

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}\left(N\cdot{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N)\right)
-discrete

Left Weighted Degree

all equal

Right Weighted Degree

all equal

Table 5:
Composing
\mathcal{D}_{4}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{Had}}
. In
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{Had}}
, the parameters
N^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}
are
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}({\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log\log\log N))
and
O_{\varepsilon,|\Sigma_{0}|}(1)
respectively.

11
Applications

In this section we describe applications of our main dPCP result (
Theorem

3.12
) to PCPPs, RLDCs, and CSP-reconfiguration.

11.1
Proof of
Theorem

1.7

Our 3-query PCPP construction is based on the dPCP from
Theorem

3.12
, along with an additional consistency check. Due to the latter, we have to take into account the list size of the dPCP, which makes our PCPP effective only for words which are very far from the language.

Theorem 11.1
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
the following holds for any alphabet
\Sigma_{0}
. For all circuits
\varphi:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
of size
N
, there exists a 3-query PCPP
\Psi=(X\cup A\cup B,E,\mathcal{P},\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{A}\cup\Sigma_{B},\{\Phi_{e}\}_{e\in E})
for the language
\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi)
with the following parameters:

•

Length.

|A|,|B|=O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(N\cdot{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}\log N)
,
|X|=n
and the edge set
E\subseteq X\times A\times B
consists of
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(N\cdot{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}\log N)
hyperedges, each has size
3
and includes one vertex from each one of
A,B
and
X
.

•

Distribution.
The marginal distribution of
\mathcal{P}
on
B
is uniform.

•

Alphabet Size.
Vertices in
A,B
have alphabets
\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B}
of size
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|,\varepsilon}(1)
each, the alphabet of
X
is
\Sigma_{0}
.

•

Soundness.
There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that for any
\eta\geqslant 1-\varepsilon^{C+1}
, if
w:X\to\Sigma_{0}
is
\eta
-far from
\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi)
then for any
T_{A}:A\to\Sigma_{A},T_{B}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
,

\Pr_{(x,a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}}[\Phi_{(x,a,b)}(w_{t},T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1]\leqslant 2\varepsilon.

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. We use
Theorem

3.12

to construct a dPCP for
\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi)
:

\mathcal{D}=\left(A\cup B,E,\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{\Phi_{e}\}_{e\in E},\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right)

with
(\mathsf{L},\varepsilon)
list-decoding. Then, we construct a PCPP
\Psi=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime},\{\Phi^{\prime}_{e}\}_{e\in E^{\prime}})
with
X\subseteq V^{\prime}
as follows:
V^{\prime}=A\cup B\cup X
where
|X|=n
,
E^{\prime}=\{(t,a,b)\ :\ t\in X,(a,b)\in\supp(\mathcal{P}_{t})\}
,
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma_{A}\cup\Sigma_{B}\cup\Sigma_{0}
. For an edge
(t,a,b)\in E^{\prime}
, the satisfying assignments of the constraint
\Phi_{(t,a,b)}
are tuples
(\sigma,\alpha,\beta)
such that
(\alpha,\beta)
satisfies the constraint
\Phi_{(a,b)}
of
\mathcal{D}
, and
D_{t}(a,\alpha))=\sigma
. Finally, the distribution
{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
is the complete distribution of
\mathcal{D}
, which we denote by
\mathcal{Q}
.

The completeness, query complexity, number of vertices, edges and alphabet size of
\Psi
follow directly from the construction. The marginal distribution over
B
is uniform as
\mathcal{Q}(\circ,\circ,\cdot)=\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\circ,\circ,\cdot)
is uniform by
Theorem

3.12
.

We prove the soundness condition. Suppose
w\in\Sigma_{0}^{n}
is
\eta
-far from
\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi)
, and fix assignments
T_{A}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
and
T_{B}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
to the dPCP
\mathcal{D}
. By the list-decoding property of the dPCP, there exists a list
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{\mathsf{L}}\}\subseteq\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi)
such that

\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a])\notin\{{(u_{i})}_{t}\}_{i\in[\mathsf{L}]}]\leqslant\varepsilon.

(69)

We conclude that

\displaystyle\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}[\Phi_{(t,a,b)}(w_{t},T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\land(w_{t}=D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a]))]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\land(w_{t}=D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a]))\land D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a])\notin\{{(u_{i})}_{t}\}_{i\in[\mathsf{L}]}]

\displaystyle+\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\land(w_{t}=D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a]))\land D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a])\in\{{(u_{i})}_{t}\}_{i\in[\mathsf{L}]}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon+\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[w_{t}\in\{{(u_{i})}_{t}\}_{i\in[\mathsf{L}]}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon+\sum_{i\in[\mathsf{L}]}\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[w_{t}={(u_{i})}_{t}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon+\mathsf{L}\cdot(1-\eta),

where in the third transition we us (
69
), in the fourth transition we use the union bound, and in the last transition we use the fact that
u_{i}\in{\sf SAT}(\varphi)
, that
w
is
\eta
-far from
\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi)
and that the marginal distribution of
\mathcal{Q}
over
t
is uniform. Since
L=1/\varepsilon^{C}
for some absolute constant
C>0
, given that
\eta\geqslant 1-\varepsilon^{C+1}
we conclude the the soundness error is at most
2\varepsilon
.
∎

11.2
Proof of
Theorem

1.8

Our 4-query PCPP with optimal proximity/soundness guarantees is constructed via a similar transformation as in
Theorem

11.1
with an additional encoding idea.

Theorem 11.2
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
the following holds for any alphabet
\Sigma_{0}
. For all circuits
\varphi:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
of size
N
over
\Sigma_{0}
, there exists a 4-query PCPP
\Psi=(X\cup Y\cup A\cup B,E,\mathcal{P},\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma\cup\Sigma_{A}\cup\Sigma_{B},\{\Phi_{e}\}_{e\in E})
for the language
\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi)
with the following parameters:

•

Length.

|A|,|B|=O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(N\cdot{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}\log(N))
,
|X|=n,|Y|=O_{\varepsilon}(n)
and the edge set

E\subseteq X\times Y\times A\times B

consists of
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(N\cdot{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}\log(N))
hyperedges, each has size
4
and includes one vertex from each one of
A,B,X,Y
.

•

Alphabet Size.
Vertices in
A,B
have alphabets
\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B}
of size
O_{|\Sigma_{0}|,\varepsilon}(1)
each, the alphabet of
X
is
\Sigma_{0}
and the alphabet of
Y
, denoted by
\Sigma_{Y}
, has size
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
.

•

Soundness.
For any
\eta>0
, if
w:X\to\Sigma_{0}
is
\eta
-far from
\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi)
then for any
T_{A}:A\to\Sigma_{A},T_{B}:B\to\Sigma_{B},T_{Y}:Y\to\Sigma_{Y}
,

\Pr_{(x,y,a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}}[\Phi_{(x,y,a,b)}(w_{x},T_{Y}[y],T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1]\leqslant 1-\eta+\varepsilon.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\Sigma^{n/\rho}
be a
linear time encodable code with distances
1-\mu
as in
Lemma

2.1
; we will choose the parameter
\mu
later and then
\rho=\rho(\mu)>0
is picked. Define the circuit
\varphi^{\prime}:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\times\Sigma^{n/\rho}\to\{0,1\}
which on input
(m,m^{\prime})\in\Sigma_{0}^{n}\times\Sigma^{n/\rho}

outputs
1
only if
\mathcal{C}(m)=m^{\prime}
and
\varphi(m)=1
. Note that since
\varphi
is of size
N
and the encoding circuit is linear in
n
, the size of
\varphi^{\prime}
is
\widetilde{O}(N+n/\rho)
. Also, we have

{\sf SAT}(\varphi^{\prime})=\{(m,m^{\prime})\in\Sigma_{0}^{n}\times\Sigma^{n/\rho}~|~m\in{\sf SAT}(\varphi),m^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}(m)\}.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
be a parameter to be chosen later, and let
\mathcal{S}\subseteq[n]\times[n/\rho]

be the edges of a bipartite expander with sides
[n]\times[n/\rho]
and right degree
d
, left degree
d/\rho
and second singular value at most
O(1/\sqrt{d})
. We note that
\mathcal{S}
can be constructed in polynomial time by
Lemma

2.5
.

Consider the circuit
\varphi^{\prime\prime}:(\Sigma\times\Sigma_{0})^{|\mathcal{S}|}\to\{0,1\}
defined as follows. Given an input
w^{\prime\prime}:\mathcal{S}\to\Sigma\times\Sigma_{0}
, define
w:[n]\to\Sigma_{0}
by picking for each
i\in[n]
an arbitrary
j
that
(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}
and
then defining
w(i)=w^{\prime\prime}(i,j)|_{i}
, and similarly
define
u:[n/\rho]\to\Sigma
.
The output
\varphi^{\prime\prime}(w^{\prime\prime})
is
1
if:

1.

For all
(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}
,
w^{\prime\prime}(i,j)=(w(i),u(j))
.

2.

\phi^{\prime}(w,u)=1
.

We note that the size of
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
is

\widetilde{O}(|\mathcal{S}|)
plus the size of

\varphi^{\prime}
, which is
\widetilde{O}_{d,\rho}(N)
.
Using
Theorem

3.12
we get a dPCP for the language
\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
with
(\mathsf{L},\varepsilon)
-list decoding, which we denote as

\mathcal{D}=\left(A\cup B,E,\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{\Phi_{(a,b)}\}_{(a,b)\in E},\{\mathcal{P}_{(x,y)}\}_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}},\{D_{(x,y)}\}_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}}\right).

We denote the complete decoding distribution of
\mathcal{D}
by
\mathcal{Q}
. We think of the decoder on
(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}
as outputting two elements, which we call
D_{(x,y)}(\cdot,\cdot)_{x}
and
D_{(x,y)}(\cdot,\cdot)_{y}
.

We construct the PCPP for
\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi)
as
\Psi=(X\cup Y\cup A\cup B,E^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{A}\cup\Sigma_{B},\{\Phi_{e^{\prime}}\}_{e\in E^{\prime}})
where
|X|=n,|Y|=n/\rho
, the edge set is

E^{\prime}=\{((x,y),a,b)~|~(x,y)\in\mathcal{S},(a,b)\in\supp(\mathcal{P}_{(x,y)})\}

and the distribution
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is taken to be
\mathcal{Q}
.
The alphabet of
A
is
\Sigma_{A}
, the alphabet of
B
is
\Sigma_{B}
, the alphabet of
X
is
\Sigma_{0}
and the alphabet of
Y
is
\Sigma
. For
((x,y),a,b)\in E^{\prime}
, a tuple
((\sigma,\tau),\alpha,\beta)
satisfies the constraint
\Phi_{((x,y),a,b)}
if

(\alpha,\beta)
satisfies
\Phi_{(a,b)}
,
D_{(x,y)}(a,\alpha)_{x}=\sigma
, and
D_{(x,y)}(a,\alpha)_{y}=\tau
.

The size and the other parameters of the PCPP follow from the parameters of the dPCP from
Theorem

3.12
where the circuit size is
|\varphi^{\prime\prime}|
, and in the rest of the argument we argue the soundness of
\Psi
. Let
w:X\to\Sigma_{0}
be
\eta
-far from
{\sf SAT}(\varphi)
, and fix
T_{A}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
,
T_{B}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
and
T_{Y}:Y\to\Sigma
. By the list-decoding soundness property of the
\mathcal{D}
there exists a list
\{w^{\prime\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{L}}\subseteq{\sf SAT}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
such that

\Pr_{((x,y),a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T[a],T[b])=1\;\land\;D_{(x,y)}(a,T[a])\notin\{w_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)\}_{i\in[\mathsf{L}]}]\leqslant\varepsilon.

(70)

As
w^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in{\sf SAT}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
we conclude that there exists
u_{i}\in{\sf SAT}(\varphi)
, such that we have
w^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=u_{i}(x),\mathcal{C}(u_{i})_{y}
for all
(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}
, and we fix such
u_{i}
henceforth.
Let
\mathcal{E}
be the event that
\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\;\land\;D_{(x,y)}(a,T_{A}[a])_{x}=w_{x}\;\land\;D_{(x,y)}(a,T_{A}[a])_{y}=T_{Y}[y]
.
The soundness of the PCPP is thus
\Pr_{((x,y),a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}[\mathcal{E}]
,
which can be written as

\displaystyle\Pr_{((x,y),a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{E}]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{((x,y),a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{E}\;\land\;D_{(x,y)}(a,T_{A}[a])\notin\{w_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x,y)\}_{i\in[\mathsf{L}]}]

\displaystyle+\Pr_{((x,y),a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{E}\land\;D_{(x,y)}(a,T_{A}[a])\in\{((u_{i})_{x},(\mathcal{C}(u_{i}))_{y})\}_{i\in[\mathsf{L}]}].

The first term is at most
\varepsilon
by (
70
), and we now focus on the second term. Let
\mathcal{E}_{y}
be the event that there are
i\neq i^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathcal{C}(u_{i})_{y}=\mathcal{C}(u_{i^{\prime}})_{y}
. We get that

\displaystyle\Pr_{((x,y),a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{E}\;\land\;D_{(x,y)}(a,T_{A}[a])\in\{((u_{i})_{x},(\mathcal{C}(u_{i}))_{y})\}_{i\in[\mathsf{L}]}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Pr_{((x,y),a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{E}\;\land\;D_{(x,y)}(a,T_{A}[a])\in\{((u_{i})_{x},(\mathcal{C}(u_{i}))_{y})\}_{i\in[\mathsf{L}]}\;\land\;\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{y}]+\Pr_{((x,y),a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{E}_{y}].

Note that for any
i\neq i^{\prime}
, the words
\mathcal{C}(u_{i})
,
\mathcal{C}(u_{i^{\prime}})
agree on at most
\mu
fraction of coordinates, so by the union bound

\Pr_{((x,y),a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{E}_{y}]\leqslant\binom{L}{2}\mu
. As for the other term on the right hand side, note that as
\mathcal{E}
requires that
D_{(x,y)}(a,T_{A}[a])=(w_{x},T_{Y}[y])
we get

\displaystyle\Pr_{((x,y),a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\mathcal{E}\;\land\;D_{(x,y)}(a,T_{A}[a])\in\{((u_{i})_{x},(\mathcal{C}(u_{i}))_{y})\}_{i\in[\mathsf{L}]}\;\land\;\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{y}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Pr_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}}[(w_{x},T_{Y}[y])\in\{((u_{i})_{x},(\mathcal{C}(u_{i}))_{y})\}_{i\in[\mathsf{L}]}\;\land\;\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{y}].

If the event above holds, then the values
\{(\mathcal{C}(u_{i}))_{y}\}
are all different, so at most one of them is equal to
T_{Y}[y]
.
Defining
A_{I}=\{y\ :\ \bar{\mathcal{E}_{y}}\;\land\;(\mathcal{C}(u_{I}))_{y}=T_{Y}[y]\}
and
B_{I}=\{x\ :\ w_{x}=(u_{I})_{x}\}
for
I\in[\mathsf{L}]
, we get that

\displaystyle\Pr_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}}[(w_{x},T_{Y}[y])\in\{((u_{i})_{x},(\mathcal{C}(u_{i}))_{y})\}_{i\in[\mathsf{L}]}\;\land\;\bar{\mathcal{E}_{y}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in[\mathsf{L}]}\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}}[1_{y\in A_{I}}\cdot 1_{x\in B_{I}}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{I\in[\mathsf{L}]}\mathbb{E}_{y\in[n/\rho]}[1_{y\in A_{I}}]\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\in[n]}[1_{x\in B_{I}}]+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{|A_{I}|}{n/\rho}\cdot\frac{|B_{I}|}{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 1-\eta+\frac{\mathsf{L}}{\sqrt{d}}.

In the second transition we used
Lemma

2.3
, and in the last transition we used the fact that
\mathbb{E}_{x\in[n]}[1_{x\in B_{I}}]\leqslant 1-\eta
for all
I
(as
w
is
\eta
-far from
{\sf SAT}(\varphi)
)
and that
\sum_{I\in[\mathsf{L}]}\mathbb{E}_{y\in[n/\rho]}[1_{y\in A_{I}}]=\Pr_{((x,y),a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\bar{\mathcal{E}}_{y}]\leqslant 1
.

Combining all of the above, we get that

\displaystyle\Pr_{((x,y),a,b)\sim\mathcal{Q}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\;\land\;D_{(x,y)}(a,T_{A}[a])_{x}=w_{x}]\leqslant 1-\eta+\binom{\mathsf{L}}{2}\cdot\mu+\frac{\mathsf{L}}{\sqrt{d}}.

As
\mathsf{L}=1/\varepsilon^{C}
for some absolute constant
C
by
Theorem

3.12
, choosing
\mu=\varepsilon^{2C}
and
d=4\varepsilon^{-2C}
,
we get that the error is at most
1-\eta+\varepsilon
.
∎

11.3
A Query-Preserving PCPP to RLDC Transformation

To prove
Theorem

1.9
we describe a generic transformation that converts a sufficiently good
3
-query PCPP into a
3
-query RLDC.

Theorem 11.3
.

Suppose for some absolute constant
C>0
we have the following two ingredients:

•

For any alphabet
\Sigma_{0}
, constant
\delta>0
, and circuit
\varphi\colon\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
of size
N
there is a 3-query PCPP
\Psi=(X\cup A\cup B,E,\mathcal{P},\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma,\{\Phi_{e}\}_{e\in E})
satisfying:

–

Length.

O_{|\Sigma_{0}|}(N\cdot{\sf poly}_{\varepsilon}(\log N))
.

–

Queries.
Each hyperedge
e=(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})\in E
satisfies
e_{1}\in X,e_{2}\in A,e_{3}\in B
, and choosing
e
uniformly,
e_{1}
is uniformly random in
X
,
e_{2}
is uniformly random in
A
, and
e_{3}
is uniformly in
B
.

–

Soundness.
For all
\eta\geqslant 1-\delta^{C}
, if
w:X\to\Sigma_{0}
is
\eta
-far from
\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi)
, then for any
T_{A}:A\to\Sigma_{A},T_{B}:B\to\Sigma_{B}

\Pr_{(t,a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}}[\Phi_{(t,a,b)}(w_{t},T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1]\leqslant 2\delta.

•

For any
\delta\in(0,1)
there is
\rho>0
, an alphabet
\Sigma_{0}
of size
O_{\delta}(1)
, and a code
\mathcal{C}_{0}:\{0,1\}^{k}\to\Sigma_{0}^{k/\rho}
with relative distance
\delta
, that is both quasi-linear time encodable and decodable.

Then for all
\mu\in(0,1)
sufficiently small, and sufficiently large
k
, there is a
3
-query RLDC with message length
k
, blocklength
k^{2}\cdot{\sf poly}_{\mu}(\log k)
, alphabet size
O_{\mu}(1)
, distance
\frac{1-\mu}{2}
, and decoding radius
\mu
. Furthermore, for all
\delta>0
, the RLDC has success-rate
1-16\delta-o(1)
on inputs that are
\delta
-close to a valid codeword.

Proof.

Fix
\mu>0
sufficiently small and take
\rho>0
and a code
\mathcal{C}_{0}
as per the second item with distance
1-\mu
, rate
\rho
, and appropriate alphabet
\Sigma_{0}
. For an index
i\in[k]
and a bit
b\in\{0,1\}
, let
\varphi_{i,b}:\Sigma_{0}^{k/\rho_{0}}\to\{0,1\}
be a circuit that given a word
w
of length
k/\rho
, outputs
1
if
w
is a member of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, and furthermore the
i
’th bit of the encoded message is equal to
b
. More precisely on an input
w\in\Sigma_{0}^{k/\rho}
, run the decoding map to get
m=\mathsf{Dec_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}}(w)
, then output
1
if and only if
\mathsf{\mathcal{C}_{0}}(m)=w
and
m_{i}=b
. Since the code
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is quasi-linear time encodable and decodable, the circuit size of
\varphi_{i,b}
is
\widetilde{O}_{\mu}(k)
. Define
\mathcal{L}_{i,b}\coloneqq\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi_{i,b})
.

Unifying the proof length for PCPP invocations.

For
i\in[k],b\in\{0,1\}
, take

\Psi_{i,b}=([k/\rho]\cup A_{i,b}\cup B_{i,b},E_{i,b},\Sigma,\{\Phi_{e}\}_{e\in E},\mathcal{P}_{i,b})

to be the PCPP from the premise of the statement for the language
\mathcal{L}_{i,b}
. Without loss of generality, we assume that all the PCPPs have the same alphabet which has size
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
.
The code we construct will contains multiple encodings of the message and relevant proofs, but we first address a small technical issue. The PCPPs are guaranteed to have length that is bounded by
k\cdot\log^{C}k
(as the circuit size is
\widetilde{O}_{\mu}(k)
), but
A_{i,b}
(as well as
B_{i,b}
) may differ in size across
i
and across
b
. Since each proof should be contained in a predefined part of the codeword we construct below, we must ensure that the right and left sizes of the PCPP have the same size across all invocations. Towards that, we set
C^{\prime}=C+10
and let
K=k\cdot\log^{C^{\prime}}k
. Given assignments to the PCPP
T_{A_{i,b}}:A_{i,b}\to\Sigma
and
T_{B_{i,b}}:B_{i,b}\to\Sigma
construct
\pi_{i,b}=(\pi_{i,b,A},\pi_{i,b,B})
as follows:

•

\pi_{i,b,A}=\left(T_{A_{i,b}}^{\lfloor K/|A_{i,b}|\rfloor},\nu^{K-\lfloor K/|A_{i,b}|\rfloor\cdot|A_{i,b}|}\right)
, that is, repeat
T_{A_{i,b}}
in full
\lfloor K/|A_{i,b}|\rfloor
-times, and put
\nu
in the rest to make the overall size exactly
K
. Here,
\nu
is an arbitrary symbol outside of
\Sigma
.

•

\pi_{i,b,B}=\left(T_{B_{i,b}}^{\lfloor K/|B_{i,b}|\rfloor},\nu^{K-\lfloor K/|B_{i,b}|\rfloor\cdot|B_{i,b}|}\right)
, that is, repeat
T_{B_{i,b}}
in full
\lfloor K/|B_{i,b}|\rfloor
-times, and put
\nu
in the rest to make the overall size exactly
K
.

Note that the uniform distribution over
[K]
and the distribution over
[K]
which is uniform over the subset
[\lfloor K/|A_{i,b}|\rfloor\cdot|A_{i,b}|]
, are
1/\log^{10}k
-close in statistical distance; similarly for
B_{i,b}
.
We think of the PCPP as the string
\pi_{i,b}=(\pi_{i,b,A},\pi_{i,b,B})
of length
2K
over alphabet symbols from
\Sigma
. We denote by
\pi_{i,b}(w)
an arbitrary assignment to the PCPP in the completeness case for
w
. By the perfect completeness of the PCPP, it is guaranteed that such an assignment exists which satisfies all of the constraints with
w
.

The construction.

Let
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma\times\{0,1\}
. We define
\mathcal{C}\colon\{0,1\}^{k}\to\Sigma_{0}^{r\cdot k/\rho}\times(\Sigma^{\prime})^{2K\cdot k}
as follows:

\mathcal{C}(m)=\mathcal{C}_{0}(m)^{r}\pi^{\prime}_{1,m_{1}}(\mathcal{C}_{0}(m))\cdots\pi^{\prime}_{k,m_{k}}(\mathcal{C}_{0}(m))

for
r=2\rho K
, where the proof
\pi_{i,m_{i}}^{\prime}
are constructed as follows. We first construct
\pi_{i,m_{i}}(\mathcal{C}_{0}(m))
, and to get
\pi^{\prime}_{i,m_{i}}(\mathcal{C}_{0}(m))
we append the bit
m_{i}
to each entry of
\pi_{i,m_{i}}(\mathcal{C}_{0}(m))
. This results in doubling the alphabet size of the proof. Overall, we get that a codeword consists of two equal length blocks:

•

The first block consists of
r
repetitions of
\mathcal{C}_{0}(m)
.

•

The second block consists of the concatenation of
\pi^{\prime}_{i,m_{i}}(\mathcal{C}_{0}(m))
for
i=1,\ldots,k
, where each entry of the proof has the bit
m_{i}
“appended”.

Moreover, the second block can be further partitioned into two equal sized blocks consisting of the “
A
”-vertices and “
B
”-vertices of each PCPP.

The decoder.

Given an index
i\in[k]
and
w\in\Sigma_{0}^{r\cdot k/\rho}\times\Sigma^{k\cdot r}
, the decoder
{\sf Dec}(w,i)
proceeds in the following way:

1.

Choose a random repetition of out of the
r
repetitions in the first block of
w
. Call this
w_{0}
.

2.

Sample a uniform index
b^{\prime}
in
[K]
, and read
\pi_{i,m_{i},B}^{\prime}
at index
b^{\prime}
.
If that symbol is
\nu
output
\bot
, and else proceed.

Recall that
\pi_{i,m_{i},B}^{\prime}
contains multiple copies of assignments to the right-side
B_{i,m_{i}}
. The index
b^{\prime}
corresponds to a vertex in
b\in B_{i,m_{i}}
, and the distribution we chose is
(1/\log^{10}k)
-close to being uniform over
B_{i,m_{i}}
.
We note that as the supposed value of
m_{i}
is
not known to the decoder yet, it does not know the correct distribution over the locations that need to be read in the PCPP (as they may be different for each one of the languages
\mathcal{L}_{i,m_{i}}
). However, since the distribution over the query on the
B
-side is uniform for each of
\mathcal{L}_{i,m_{i}}
, the query we made in this step is sampled according to a distribution which is statistically close to the correct distribution.

3.

Let
x
be the bit appended, so that supposedly
x=m_{i}
.

4.

The decoder now knows which language
\mathcal{L}_{i,x}
was supposedly used for the PCPP encoding, and in particular the decoder can calculate the length of
A_{i,x}
. Thus, the decoder also knows which locations in the
A
-part of
\pi_{i,x}(\mathcal{C}_{0}(m))
are the non-
\nu
ones. The decoder samples a uniformly random index
j\in[\lfloor K/|A_{i,x}\rfloor]
and looks at the
j
th copy of
\pi_{i,x}(\mathcal{C}_{0}(m))
from the
A
-part.
The verifier samples
(t,a,{\bf b})\sim\mathcal{P}_{i,x}
conditioned on
{\bf b}=b
, and then queries the locations corresponding to
a
in the
j
th copy of the
A
-part of
\pi_{i,x}(\mathcal{C}_{0}(m))
and
t
in
w_{0}
.

5.

If the read assignment to
a
does not have the bit
x
appended, output
\perp
.
Else, remove the appended bit from each of the read symbols and proceed.

6.

If the read assignment satisfies the constraint on
(t,a,b)
of
\Psi_{i,x}
, output
x
. Otherwise, output
\perp
.

Analysis of the construction.

We now analyze the construction.
It is easy to see that the code has distance at least
\frac{1-\mu}{2}
due to the repetitions in the first block. The length and completeness for relaxed local decoding are clear from the corresponding properties of the PCPP.

For the success rate, suppose the codeword,
w
, is
\delta
-close to some valid codeword
w^{\prime}
. Let
I_{1},I_{2},
and
I_{3}
be the
\mathcal{C}_{0}(m)^{r}
part, the
A
-parts of the proofs, and the
B
-parts of the proofs respectively. The queries of the decoder are distributed as follows. Over a uniformly random index to decode, the marginal distribution of the queries of the decoder is: (1) a uniformly random query in
I_{1}
,
(2) a query according to a distribution which is
o(1)
-close to uniform over
I_{2}
, and (3) a uniformly random query in
I_{3}
.
Let
\delta_{s}
be the fraction of locations in
I_{s}
on which
w
and
w^{\prime}
differ, so that
\frac{1}{2}|I_{1}|\delta_{1}+\frac{1}{4}|I_{2}|\delta_{2}+\frac{1}{4}|I_{3}|\delta_{3}
is the Hamming-distance between
w
and
w^{\prime}
and hence at most
\delta\cdot 4Kk
. As
|I_{1}|=2Kk
,
|I_{2}|=Kk
and
|I_{3}|=Kk
we get that

\delta_{1}+\frac{1}{4}\delta_{2}+\frac{1}{4}\delta_{3}\leqslant 4\delta
.
By the union bound, the probability that any of the queries of the decoder is a location in which
w
and
w^{\prime}
differ is at most

\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}+o(1)\leqslant 4(\delta_{1}+\frac{1}{4}\delta_{2}+\frac{1}{4}\delta_{3})+o(1)\leqslant 16\delta+o(1).

In this case, the decoder acts on
w
as it would act on
w^{\prime}
, hence it outputs the correct bit by the completeness. We conclude that the success rate is at least
1-16\delta-o(1)
.

We now analyze the soundness of the decoder. Suppose that there is a message
m\in\{0,1\}^{k}
such that

\mathsf{dist}(w,\mathcal{C}(m))\leqslant\mu,

and fix an
i\in[k]
to decode. Choosing the copy
w_{0}
of the supposed encoding of
m
under
\mathcal{C}_{0}
from the first block uniformly, we get that,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{w_{0}}[\mathsf{dist}(w_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0}(m))]\leqslant 2\mu.

Thus, by Markov’s inequality with probability at least
9/10
we have that the
w_{0}
chosen satisfies

\mathsf{dist}(w_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0}(m))\leqslant 20\mu.

(71)

Now the decoder, as described above, looks at the proof
\pi^{\prime}
from the second block which is supposedly the proof
\pi^{\prime}_{i,m_{i}}(w_{0})
. If one of the proof queries has the bit
m_{i}
appended, then the decoder will output either
m_{i}
or
\perp
, and we are done. Otherwise, they both have the bit
m_{i}\oplus 1
appended. In that case, the verifier of the PCPP of
\mathcal{L}_{i,m_{i}\oplus 1}
is run, and we lower bound its rejection probability.
By (
71
) and the fact
\mathcal{C}_{0}
has distance
1-\mu
, we get that
\mathsf{dist}(w_{i},\mathcal{L}_{i,m_{i}\oplus 1})\geqslant(1-\mu)-20\mu=1-21\mu
. In this case, the decoder will output
\perp
with probability at least
1-2\cdot(21\mu)^{1/C}-\frac{1}{\log^{10}k}
for some fixed constant
C>0
, by the soundness of the PCPP.
5
5

5

The
\frac{1}{\log^{10}k}
term is because the query we made to the
B
-side is only
\frac{1}{\log^{10}k}
-close to uniform in total variation distance.
In particular, for sufficiently large
k
and sufficiently small
\mu
, the decoder outputs either the correct bit or
\perp
with probability at least

\frac{9}{10}\cdot\left(1-2\cdot(21\mu)^{1/C}-\frac{1}{\log^{10}k}\right)\geqslant\frac{2}{3},

where the inequality follows by setting
\mu
to be sufficiently small.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.9
.

Invoke
Theorem

11.3
with the PCPP from
Theorem

11.1
and the codes from
Lemma

2.1
.
∎

11.4
Improved PSPACE Inapproximability of CSP-Reconfiguration

The reconfiguration inapproximability problem
[
48
,
15
,
47
]
studies the complexity of moving between two given solutions to a given CSP instance, by modifying the value of only a single variable at each step, and only going through assignments that satisfy a large fraction of the constraints. More precisely:

Definition 11.4

(Gap
1,ε

q
-CSP
Σ
Reconfiguration)
.

Let
q\geqslant 1
be an integer,
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, and
\Sigma
be a finite
alphabet. An instance of
\mathrm{Gap}_{1,\varepsilon}\,q
-CSP
Σ

Reconfiguration is specified by a
q
-CSP
Σ
instance
\Phi
and two
solutions
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{n}
. The task is to distinguish the
following two cases:

•

Yes Case.
There exist assignments

\sigma^{(0)},\ldots,\sigma^{(t)}\in\Sigma^{n}
such that

\sigma^{(0)}=\sigma
,
\sigma^{(t)}=\sigma^{\prime}
, each

\sigma^{(i)},\sigma^{(i-1)}
differ on at most one coordinate, and each

\sigma^{(i)}
satisfies all constraints in
\Phi
.

•

No Case.
There exist no assignments

\sigma^{(0)},\ldots,\sigma^{(t)}\in\Sigma^{n}
such that

\sigma^{(0)}=\sigma
,
\sigma^{(t)}=\sigma^{\prime}
, each

\sigma^{(i)},\sigma^{(i-1)}
differ on at most one coordinate, and each

\sigma^{(i)}
satisfies at least
\varepsilon
fraction of the constraints
in
\Phi
.

The recent work of
[
47
]
establishes a formal connection between PCPPs and hardness of CSP reconfiguration problems, where the better the parameters of the PCPPs are, the better of a gap one obtains for the reconfiguration problem.

Theorem 11.5

(Theorem 2,
[
47
]
)
.

Suppose for some
\eta\in(0,1)
, there is an alphabet
\Sigma_{0}
such that we have the following two ingredients:

•

Any error-correcting code
\mathcal{C}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\Sigma_{0}^{k}

6
6

6

In
[
47
]
, a binary code (rather than a code over a larger alphabet) is used. However, an inspection of their argument shows that they only use the fact the
messages
are binary. Thus, the alphabet size of the encoding could be taken as a large constant.
with relative distance
\eta\in(0,1)
that is encodable in space
{\sf poly}(n)
.

•

For any constant
\delta<\eta/2
and every circuit
\varphi:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
of size
N
there is a
q
-query PCPP which is constructible in space polynomial in
|\Sigma_{0}|,N
,
(\delta,\varepsilon(\delta))
-soundness, randomness
O(\log N)
, alphabet size
O_{\delta,|\Sigma_{0}|}(1)
.

Then for all
\delta<\eta/2
, there is an alphabet
\Sigma
of size
O_{\delta,|\Sigma_{0}|}(1)
,
\mathrm{Gap}_{1,1-\varepsilon(\delta)}\,(q+1)
-CSP
Σ
Reconfiguration is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-hard.

Since our PCPP from
Theorem

11.2
achieves strong relation between the proximity and rejection probability we can use it to instantiate the theorems above with it.

We can now prove
Corollaries

1.13
and
1.14
.

Proof of
Corollary

1.13
.

Take the code from
Lemma

2.1
with distance greater than
1-\delta
for some arbitrarily small
\delta
and take the
4
-query PCPP of
Theorem

11.2
, which rejects
\frac{1-\delta}{2}
-far inputs with probability
\frac{1-\delta}{2}+\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\delta)
arbitrarily small relative to
\delta
. Then, the result follows by plugging these two pieces into
Theorem

11.5
.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

1.14
.

The result follows by a simple reduction from
\mathrm{Gap}_{1,1-\varepsilon}\,(q+1)
-CSP
Σ
to
\mathrm{Gap}_{1,1-\frac{\varepsilon}{q+1}}\,2
-CSP
Σ
,
∎

Appendix A
The Final Inner dPCP (Hadamard dPCP)

In this subsection we prove
Theorem

10.3
. We do so in three steps:

•

We present the notion of right-assignment decoding soundness, which most dPCP constructions in the literature satisfy. We show that it implies our notion of list-decoding soundness.

•

We recall the definition of robust dPCPs from
[
28
]
. A construction of such dPCPs yields the desired structure of the left-vertices in the inner dPCPs for composition (see
Theorem

8.3
).

•

Then we show that the folklore Hadamard dPCP (taken from
[
8
, Lemma 7.7]
) satisfies all of our requirement for the inner dPCP.

A.1
Right-Assignment Decoding to Left-Assignment Decoding

Below is the more commonly used list-decoding soundness for dPCPs.
In it the list is constructed from a fixed assignment to the right side of the dPCP, as opposed to
Definition

3.3
wherein it is constructed from a fixed assignment to the left side of the dPCP.

Definition A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a decodable PCP for
\mathcal{L}
. Given
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
,
L\in\mathbb{N}
we say that
\mathcal{D}
satisfies
right-assignment
(L,\varepsilon)
-list-decoding soundness
if the following holds. For any right assignment
T_{B}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
there exists a list
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{L}\}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
such that for any
T_{A}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
,

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in[n]\\
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}\end{subarray}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a])\notin\{{(w_{i})}_{t}\}_{i\in[L]}]\leqslant\varepsilon.

In the definition above, the right-assignment
T_{B}
is fixed and induces a list of candidate words in the language, whereas in our definition of list-decoding soundness it is the left-assignment
T_{A}
that is fixed.
In the following we show a simple transformation between the two notations:

Lemma A.2
.

Suppose a language
\mathcal{L}
has a projection dPCP
\mathcal{D}=\left(A\cup B,E,\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}\right)

with right-assignment-decoding
(L,\varepsilon)
-list-decoding soundness. Then
\mathcal{D}
also satisfies
(L\cdot\varepsilon^{-1/5},2\varepsilon^{1/5})
-list-decoding soundness for the language
\mathcal{L}
.

Proof.

First, note that since
\mathcal{D}
is a projection dPCP, for each
a\in A
and an assignment to it
\sigma
, each neighbour
b\in\Gamma\!\left(a\right)
has a unique assignment that would satisfy the constraint
\Phi_{(a,b)}
, and we denote it by
(\sigma)_{b}
(the vertex
a
will always be clear from context). Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the complete decoding distribution of
\mathcal{D}
.

Fix an assignment
T_{A}:A\to\Sigma_{A}
, and consider the following candidate list: for each
b\in B
, let
{\sf cand}(b)
be the set of symbols
\sigma\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
such that
\Pr_{a\sim\mathcal{Q}(\circ,\cdot,b)}[T_{A}[a]_{b}=\sigma]\geqslant\varepsilon^{1/5}
. One can find
J=1/\varepsilon^{1/5}
assignments
T_{1}^{\prime},...T_{J}^{\prime}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
, such that for every
b\in B
and every
\sigma\in\mathsf{cand}(b)
there is some
j\in[J]
such that
T_{j}^{\prime}[b]=\sigma
. We use the assignments
T_{1}^{\prime},...,T_{J}^{\prime}
to invoke the right-assignment list-decoding property of the dPCP with respect to fixed right assignments (and with
T_{A}
being the left assignment). Thus, for each
j
we get a list
L_{j}^{\prime}=\{w_{1}^{j},\ldots,w_{L}^{j}\}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
such that

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\sim[n],\\
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}\end{subarray}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{j}^{\prime}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a])\notin\{{(w_{i}^{j})}_{t}\}_{i\in[L]}]\leqslant\varepsilon.

Define the list list
L^{\prime}=\cup_{j\in[J]}L_{j}^{\prime}
. To complete the proof, we will show that for any
T_{B}:B\to\Sigma_{B}
we have

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\sim[n],\\
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}\end{subarray}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a])\notin\{w_{i}^{j}[t]\}_{i\in[L],j\in[J]}]\leqslant J\cdot\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{1/5}\leqslant 2\varepsilon^{1/5}.

First, note that the probability on the left hand side can be written as

\displaystyle\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\sim[n],\\
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}\end{subarray}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a])\notin\{w_{i}^{j}[t]\}_{i\in[L],j\in[J]}]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\sim[n],\\
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}\end{subarray}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a])\notin\{w_{i}^{j}[t]\}_{i\in[L],j\in[J]}\land T_{A}[a]_{b}\in\mathsf{cand}(b)]

\displaystyle+\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\sim[n],\\
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}\end{subarray}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a])\notin\{w_{i}^{j}[t]\}_{i\in[L],j\in[J]}\land T_{A}[a]\notin\mathsf{cand}(b)].

The second term is bounded by
\varepsilon^{1/5}
by the definition of the
\mathsf{cand}(b)
list. For the first term, we use union bound:

\displaystyle\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\sim[n],\\
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}\end{subarray}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a])\notin\{w_{i}^{j}[t]\}_{i\in[L],j\in[J]}\land T_{A}[a]_{b}\in\mathsf{cand}(b)]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\sim[n],\\
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}\end{subarray}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a])\notin\{w_{i}^{j}[t]\}_{i\in[L],j\in[J]}\land(\cup_{j\in[J]}T_{A}[a]_{b}=T_{j}^{\prime}[b])]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{j\in[J]}\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\sim[n],\\
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}\end{subarray}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a])\notin\{w_{i}^{j}[t]\}_{i\in[L],j\in[J]}\land T_{A}[a]_{b}=T_{j}^{\prime}[b])]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[J]}\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\sim[n],\\
(a,b)\sim\mathcal{P}_{t}\end{subarray}}[\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{j}^{\prime}[b])=1\land D_{t}(a,T_{A}[a])\notin\{w_{i}^{j}[t]\}_{i\in[L],j\in[J]})]

\displaystyle\leqslant J\cdot\varepsilon.

In this third transition we used the fact that if
\Phi_{(a,b)}(T_{A}[a],T_{B}[b])=1
and
T_{A}[a]_{b}=T_{j}^{\prime}[b]
, then we must have
T_{B}[b]=T_{j}^{\prime}[b]
. In the last transition we used
the right-assignment
(L,\varepsilon)
-list-decoding soundness of
\mathcal{D}
.
∎

A.2
Going from Robust dPCPs to dPCPs

In order to present the Hadamard dPCP from prior work, we need to first present an alternative definition of dPCPs, called robust dPCPs, introduced by
[
28
]
. The main difference between robust dPCPs and our notion of dPCP is that robust dPCPs have a different notion of list-decoding soundness.

Definition A.3

(Robust Decodable PCPs)
.

Let
\varphi\colon\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be a circuit of size
N
. A PCP decoder for the language
{\sf SAT}(\varphi)
over a proof alphabet
\Sigma

is a probabilistic polynomial-time algorithm
\mathcal{D}
that on an input circuit

\varphi:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
of size
N
and an index
j\in[n]
,
tosses
r=r(N)
random coins to sample from a distribution
\mathcal{P}_{j}
supported over tuples containing
(I,f)
which are:

(1)

A sequence of
q=q(N)
locations
I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{q})
in a proof of length
m(N)
over the alphabet
\Sigma
; the parameter
q
is referred to as the query complexity of
\mathcal{D}
.

(2)

A (local decoding) function
f:\Sigma^{q}\to\Sigma_{0}\cup\{\bot\}

whose corresponding circuit has size at most
s(N)
, referred to henceforth as the
decision complexity
of the decoder.

For functions
\delta:\mathbb{Z}^{+}\to[0,1]
and
L:\mathbb{Z}^{+}\to\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, we say that a PCP
decoder
\mathcal{D}
is a dPCP for

\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi)
with soundness error
\delta
and list size
L
if the following
completeness and soundness properties hold for every circuit

\varphi:\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
of size
N
:

•

Completeness.

For any
y\in\Sigma_{0}^{n}
such that
\varphi(y)=1
, there exists a proof
\pi\in\Sigma^{m}
, also called
a decodable PCP, such that

\Pr_{j,I,f}[f(\pi_{I})=y_{j}]=1,

where
j\in[n]
is chosen uniformly at random and
I,f
are distributed according to
\mathcal{P}_{j}
and
the verifier’s random coins.

•

Soundness.

For any
\pi\in\Sigma^{m}
, there is a list of
0\leqslant\ell\leqslant L
strings

y^{1},\ldots,y^{\ell}
satisfying
\varphi(y^{i})=1
for all
i
, and furthermore

\Pr_{j\in[n],(I,f)\sim\mathcal{P}_{j}}\big[f(\pi_{I})\notin\{\bot,y^{1}_{j},\ldots,y^{\ell}_{j}\}\,\big]\leqslant\delta.

•

Robust Soundness.

We say that
\mathcal{D}
is a robust dPCP system for
\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi)
with robust soundness
error
\delta
if the soundness criterion can be strengthened to the following robust condition:

\mathbb{E}_{j\in[n],(I,f)\sim\mathcal{P}_{j}}\left[\mathrm{agr}(\pi_{I},\mathrm{BAD}(f))\right]\leqslant\delta,

where
\mathrm{BAD}(f):=\{w\in\Sigma^{q}\mid f(w)\notin\{\bot,y^{1}_{j},\ldots,y^{\ell}_{j}\}\}.

We have the following correspondence
between robust dPCPs as in
Definition

A.3
and our more usual notion of dPCPs.

Lemma A.4
.

Suppose a language
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\Sigma_{0}^{n}
has a robust dPCP with the following parameters

•

Length.

m
.

•

Query complexity.

d_{1}
.

•

Proof alphabet.

\Sigma
.

•

Randomness Complexity.

r
.

•

Completeness.
Perfect completeness.

•

Soundness.
Robust soundness error
\varepsilon
with list size
L
.

Then
\mathcal{L}
has a dPCP with the following parameters

•

Length.
The left vertex set has the form
[n]\times[2^{r}]
and the length is
m+n\cdot 2^{r}
.

•

Alphabets.
The left alphabet is a subset of
\Sigma^{d_{1}}
and the right alphabet is
\Sigma
.

•

Degree.
Decoding degree is
1
.

•

Complete Decoding distribution.
Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the complete decoding distribution and let the left and right sides be
A
and
B
respectively. Then the complete decoding distribution satisfies the following.

–

The marginal over
A
is uniform.

–

For each
a\in A
, the distribution
\mathcal{Q}(\circ,a,\cdot)
is uniform over
d_{1}
vertices in
B
.

•

Completeness.
Perfect completeness.

•

Soundness.

(L\cdot\varepsilon^{-1/5},2\varepsilon^{1/5})
-list-decoding soundness.

Proof.

Fix a robust dPCP for
\mathcal{L}
as in the premise.
We construct a dPCP
\mathcal{D}
for
\mathcal{L}
, whose parts are

\mathcal{D}=(A\cup B,E,\Sigma_{A},\Sigma_{B},\{\Phi_{e}\}_{e\in E},\{D_{t}\}_{t\in[n]},\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\in[n]}),

as follows.
The right vertices correspond to the proof locations of the robust dPCP. The left vertices are labeled as
[n]\times[2^{r}]
and they correspond to ever possible pair of index
t\in[n]
and randomness
\ell\in[2^{r}]
when choosing the queries given index
t
. By the randomness complexity of the robust dPCP, each query set corresponds to one of
2^{r}
random strings. The edges
e
correspond to all pair
(t,\ell)\in A
and
b\in B
such that the decoder of the robust dPCP given index
t
and randomness
\ell
queries the location corresponding to
b
in the proof. The left alphabet is
\Sigma^{d_{1}}
. For the vertex
(t,\ell)\in A
, we think of an alphabet symbol as corresponding to answer to the
d_{1}
queries made by the robust dPCP given index
t
and randomness
\ell
. It also corresponds to a labeling of its
d_{1}
neighbors on the right side.
We constrain the alphabet to only consist of values which would have made the robust dPCP verifier accept. The right alphabet is
\Sigma
, and each constraint between
(t,\ell)\in A
and
b\in B
checks that both assignments agree on the value assigned to
b
. The decoder is the same as in the robust dPCP. That is, it thinks of the symbol assigned to
(t,\ell)\in A
as the
d_{1}
answers to its queries in the robust dPCP and decodes accordingly. Finally, for each
t\in[n]
, the decoding distribution
\mathcal{P}_{t}
chooses
\ell\in[n]
randomly, chooses one of
d_{1}
many
b\in B
adjacent to
(t,\ell)
uniformly at random, and outputs
(t,(t,\ell),b)
.

It is straightforward to check that
\mathcal{D}
has the desired length, alphabets, decoding degree, and properties for the complete decoding distribution. For perfect completeness, for any
w\in\mathcal{L}
, using the perfect completeness of the robust dPCP we get that there is an assignment to
B
such that the robust dPCP always accepts and decodes to
w_{t}
. Then, we assign the left side according to the assignment to
B
. That is, for each
(t,\ell)\in A
, the assignment to
(t,\ell)
consists of the
d_{1}
symbols from
\Sigma
assigned to its
d_{1}
neighbors. It is straightforward to check that all of the constraints of
\mathcal{D}
are satisfied and the decoder for
\mathcal{D}
decodes
w_{t}
with probability
1
for all
t\in[n]
. Finally, it is straightforward to see that the definition of robust soundness corresponds directly to right-assignment
(L,\varepsilon)
-list-decoding soundness. Then, the desired list decoding soundness follows by applying
Lemma

A.2
.
∎

A.3
The Hadamard dPCP

We are now ready to present the Hadamard dPCP and prove
Theorem

10.3
. The result follows by plugging the robust (Hadamard) dPCP presented in
[
8
]
into the transformation of
Lemma

A.4
.

Lemma A.5

(Lemma 7.7 from
[
8
]
)
.

For every
T,N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
the following holds. Let
\varphi\colon\Sigma_{0}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
be a circuit of size at most
N
over an alphabet of size at most
T
. There exists
q=1/\varepsilon^{O(1)}
such that the language
\mathsf{SAT}(\varphi)
has a robust dPCP (according to
Definition

A.3
) with the following parameters:

1.

Length.

m(N)=q^{O(N^{2})}
.

2.

Alphabet Size.

q
.

3.

Randomness complexity.

r(N)=O(N^{2}\log(q))
.

4.

Query Complexity.

q^{O(\log|\Sigma_{0}|)}
.

5.

Soundness.
Robust soundness
\varepsilon
with list size
1/\varepsilon^{O(1)}
.

Proof of
Theorem

10.3
.

The result follows by plugging the robust dPCP of
Lemma

A.5
into the transformation of
Lemma

A.4
. The projection decision complexity and decoding complexity follow from the fact that each can be implemented as a circuit which outputs
0
or
1
and has an input consisting of at most two symbols from alphabets of size at most
O_{\varepsilon,T}(1)
.
Finally, the complete decoding distribution condition follows from the complete decoding distribution guarantee of
Lemma

A.4
.
∎
